#621. Scientists just found dinosaur blood that survived 115 million years

Scientists have discovered remarkably preserved traces of dinosaur blood inside 115-million-year-old bones, revealing that delicate biological molecules can survive far longer than previously believed. The breakthrough could transform how researchers study prehistoric animals and even influence the search for ancient life beyond Earth.

An international team led by Curtin University found fossilized red blood cells and chemical evidence of blood molecules in the remains of Aratasaurus museunacionali, a meat-eating theropod dinosaur discovered in northeastern Brazil. The findings, published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, also include amino acids associated with collagen, an important structural protein found in bones and other connective tissues.

The discovery provides the first definitive chemical confirmation of molecules derived from hemoglobin inside fossilized dinosaur red blood cells. Earlier research had identified structures resembling blood cells in ancient fossils, but scientists had not conclusively demonstrated the presence of these particular molecular remains within them.

Ancient Dinosaur Blood Preserved Inside Fossilized Bones

When researchers examined the dinosaur's limb bones, they identified numerous mineralized red blood cells inside vascular canals, the tiny passages that once contained blood vessels. Chemical analysis revealed preserved heme molecules, which are components of hemoglobin, the protein that allows red blood cells to transport oxygen.

Finding these molecules provided direct chemical evidence that the fossil contained remnants of ancient blood. It also demonstrated that some biological materials once thought too fragile to survive for millions of years can remain detectable under exceptional conditions.

The study was led by John Curtin Distinguished Professor Kliti Grice, founding director of Curtin University's WA-Organic and Isotope Geochemistry Centre and an Australian Research Council Laureate Fellow.

"To detect intact heme-derived molecules in 115-million-year-old red blood cells is astonishing: this really is a landmark discovery," Professor Grice said.

"It not only extends the known fossil record of blood, but also offers a direct molecular window into the physiology of long-extinct animals so we may better understand them and the world they lived in."

How Dinosaur Blood Survived 115 Million Years

The extraordinary preservation appears to be connected to a mineral called hausmannite. This mineral helped stabilize the fossilized blood cells and preserve their molecular components (including amino acids of collagen) over an immense span of geological time.

In effect, the mineral created a natural time capsule that protected microscopic evidence of the dinosaur's biology long after its death.

Researchers believe the conditions surrounding the dinosaur's burial were also crucial. They suspect the animal was quickly covered by sediment in an environment with little oxygen and abundant microbial activity. These conditions may have helped preserve biological material that would ordinarily have broken down and disappeared.

The international research effort brought together scientists from Curtin University's School of Earth and Planetary Sciences, the Japan Agency for Marine-Earth Science and Technology, the Brazilian National Museum, the Regional University of Cariri, and Johns Hopkins University.

A Discovery That Challenges Fossil Preservation Limits

The findings challenge a longstanding assumption in paleontology that molecules associated with blood, particularly those derived from hemoglobin, could not remain preserved across such enormous periods of time.

Beyond confirming the survival of ancient blood components, the discovery raises the possibility that other fossils may contain molecular information that conventional examinations have overlooked.

"These findings challenge longstanding assumptions that certain biomolecules such as hemoglobin cannot survive deep time," Professor Grice said.

"It opens exciting new avenues for molecular-level paleontology, allowing us to investigate the evolution of blood, metabolism and even activity levels in ancient animals."

Such information could eventually help researchers better understand how extinct animals functioned, including aspects of their metabolism and physiology that are difficult to reconstruct from bones alone.

Implications for Ancient Life Beyond Earth

The significance of the discovery may extend well beyond dinosaurs. Understanding how fragile biological structures can survive for millions of years could also help scientists identify possible traces of ancient life in other extreme environments, including those beyond Earth.

Professor Martin Van Kranendonk, head of Curtin University's School of Earth and Planetary Sciences, said the results are changing how researchers understand the preservation of molecular structures over vast timescales and under harsh conditions.

These insights could be valuable to astrobiology, a field that investigates the possibility of life elsewhere in the universe. If biological molecules or their chemical signatures can persist under the right conditions for more than 100 million years, scientists may need to reconsider where and how they look for evidence of ancient organisms.

"This study exemplifies how coordinated efforts across institutions, continents and scientific disciplines can unlock extraordinary insights into ancient life," Professor Van Kranendonk said.

The research was supported by a 2021 Australian Research Council Laureate Fellowship awarded to Professor Grice.

The study, titled "Heme signatures in mineralized red blood cells within vascular canals of a 115- million-year-old dinosaur bone," was published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences.

#620. La NASA busca a la próxima generación de astronautas

Read this release in English here.La NASA busca a los exploradores que ayudarán a escribir el próximo capítulo de los vuelos espaciales tripulados.

Ya está abierto el plazo de inscripción para la próxima promoción de astronautas de la agencia, que se incorporarán a la NASA en un momento en el que Estados Unidos regresa a la Luna, construye la Base Lunar y sienta las bases para las primeras misiones tripuladas a Marte.

La próxima generación de astronautas se entrenará para operar en la superficie lunar, vivir y trabajar fuera de la Tierra, y llevar a cabo misiones que ampliarán los límites de la exploración con seres humanos más lejos que nunca.

Los ciudadanos estadounidenses interesados en formar parte del cuerpo de astronautas tienen hasta las 11:59 p.m. EST (hora del este) del jueves 3 de diciembre para enviar la documentación completa de su solicitud a:

“Este es un momento extraordinario para incorporarse al cuerpo de astronautas de la NASA”, afirmó el administrador de la agencia espacial, Jared Isaacman. “Las personas que seleccionemos podrían pisar la Luna, vivir y trabajar en la Base Lunar, y ayudar a desarrollar los sistemas y operaciones que, en última instancia, llevarán a las tripulaciones a Marte. Esas misiones exigirán excelencia técnica, buen criterio, trabajo en equipo y valentía. El cuerpo de astronautas de la NASA siempre ha recurrido a estadounidenses excepcionales con la disposición de asumir retos extraordinarios al servicio de la nación. Si quieres unirte a esa tradición, ven a ayudarnos a escribir el próximo capítulo de la mayor aventura de la humanidad”.

La NASA prevé realizar su selección definitiva a finales de 2027 para comenzar la capacitación de la próxima promoción de candidatos a astronautas.

“La ventana de oportunidad está abierta. La misión nos espera. Y lo único que falta en la mayor aventura en la que se ha embarcado la humanidad eres tú”, afirmó Matt Anderson, administrador adjunto de la NASA.

La agencia abrió por última vez la convocatoria en 2024. De entre más de 8.000 candidaturas, en 2025 se seleccionaron 10 candidatos a astronautas, que actualmente están completando casi dos años de capacitación.

“Para quienes presentan su candidatura, convertirse en astronauta de la NASA es más que una oportunidad profesional”, afirmó Scott Tingle, astronauta jefe y presidente de la Junta de Selección de Astronautas del Centro Espacial Johnson de la NASA, en Houston. “Es una oportunidad para formar parte de un legado de exploradores que han ampliado los límites del conocimiento y desempeñar un papel en las misiones que marcarán el futuro de la humanidad en el espacio. La próxima generación de candidatos a astronautas podría ayudar a escribir la historia, al tiempo que inspira a futuros científicos, ingenieros y exploradores”.

Desde la década de 1960, la NASA ha seleccionado a 370 personas para capacitarlas como candidatos a astronautas en sus misiones, cada vez más exigentes. La agencia cuenta actualmente con 32 astronautas en activo, y los candidatos seleccionados se unirán a sus filas para formarse en caminatas espaciales, geología lunar, manejo de aeronaves de alto rendimiento y mucho más.

Los requisitos básicos para presentar la candidatura incluyen la ciudadanía estadounidense y un título de maestría en un campo STEM —como ingeniería, ciencias biológicas, ciencias físicas o informática— obtenido en una institución acreditada. El requisito del título de maestría también puede cumplirse mediante:

Los candidatos también deben contar con al menos tres años de experiencia profesional relacionada tras haber obtenido la titulación requerida.

En el caso de los pilotos, el requisito de experiencia profesional puede cumplirse mediante la siguiente experiencia de vuelo:

Para los médicos, el periodo de residencia puede contabilizarse como parte del requisito de experiencia profesional y debe completarse antes de julio de 2027.

Los candidatos a astronautas deben superar el examen médico de la NASA para vuelos espaciales de larga duración y demostrar sólidas habilidades de liderazgo, trabajo en equipo y comunicación.

Desde sus inicios, el cuerpo de astronautas de la NASA ha representado lo mejor del ingenio, la destreza y la determinación estadounidenses, llevando a cabo misiones que logran lo casi imposible y ampliando el conocimiento de la humanidad sobre la Tierra, la Luna y más allá.

Como parte de una edad de oro de innovación y exploración, la NASA enviará a astronautas en misiones cada vez más complejas cerca del Polo Sur lunar para explorar la Luna como nunca antes se había hecho. Además de aportar beneficios económicos e impulsar los avances científicos a través de sus misiones Artemis, la NASA establecerá una presencia humana duradera en la superficie lunar y sentará las bases para la primera misión tripulada a Marte.

Para obtener más información en inglés sobre el proceso de solicitud y lo que implica ser astronauta de la NASA, visita:

#619. The best comedies streaming on Netflix right now

Nothing feels as good as a deep, genuine laugh. It's an expression of joy! It's a workout. It's a sign that you're having a good time.

Netflix has a dazzling selection of comedy movies that give you that good-time feeling on demand. Whether you're craving action-spiked shenanigans, quirky coming-of-age adventures, awkward humor, stranger-than-fiction silliness, madcap musical numbers, or crime with a spree of laughs, there's a perfect pick for you. And we've made singling it out all the easier by highlighting the most hilarious below.

Here are the funniest movies now streaming on Netflix.

RuPaul's Drag Race fans, take notice: A slew of comedy queens (and some look queens) have come together for a disaster movie parody that's also a sensational romp.

Ginger Minj and Jujubee star as besties who land their dream jobs at Glamazonian Hyper Speed Rail. But their bond will be tested as one caters to impress the train line's high-class attendants (Brooke Lynn Hytes, Symone, and Marcia Marcia Marcia) and the other falls for a hot conductor. But when disaster strikes,  these friends will need to join together with the president (RuPaul Charles, naturally)to save the day — with lots of cameos along the way! — Kristy Puchko, Entertainment Editor

Starring: Ginger Minj, Jujubee, Brooke Lynn Hytes, Latrice Royale, Marty Lauter, Monét X Change, Symone, RuPaul Charles, Sarah Michelle Gellar, Nicole Richie, Michelle Visage, Chris Parnell, Jesse Tyler Ferguson, Natasha Leggero, Drew Droege, Joel McHale, Rachel Bloom, and Guy Branum

How to watch: Stop That Train! is now streaming on Netflix.

Looking for a revenge flick that pays tribute to teen classics like Mean Girls, Heathers, Cruel Intentions, and more? Then check out Do Revenge, a dark comedy sure to join the canon of iconic high school movies. Students Drea (Camila Mendes) and Eleanor (Maya Hawke) couldn't be more different... apart from the fact that both have major bones to pick with people who ruined their reputations. They decide to team up and execute one another's revenge plans, which leads down a winding road of morally dubious choices, snappy one-liners, and excellent outfits.

Mendes and Hawke have great chemistry, but it's Sophie Turner who damn near steals the show in a bit part that simply must be seen to be believed.* — Belen Edwards, Entertainment Reporter

Starring: Camila Mendes, Maya Hawke, Austin Abrams, Talia Ryder, Rish Shah, and Sarah Michelle Gellar

How to watch: Do Revenge is now streaming on Netflix.

If Too Much has you craving more Megan Stalter, you'll want to catch up with this underseen gem. Directed by Hannah Pearl Utt, Cora Bora centers on the titular musician (played by Stalter), who has left Portland and her girlfriend (Jojo T. Gibbs) behind to pursue her dreams in L.A. The couple's open relationship allows each to explore other connections — or, in Cora’s case, endure an awkward one-night stand with a sexy Flat Earther (Thomas Mann). But when Cora begins to fear that her girlfriend is catching feelings for someone else, it prompts the chaotic bisexual into a hasty homecoming.

In my review out of the film's SXSW premiere in 2023, I declared, "Cora Bora proves a sensational cringe comedy, spiked with wicked humor, skin-crawling awkward scenarios, and an ardent — if wounded — heart. Deftly dancing from brash jokes to tender dialogue, Stalter reveals a riveting range and stirring screen presence. Simply put, it’s easy to fall for Cora Bora." — K.P.

Starring: Megan Stalter, Jojo T. Gibbs, Manny Jacinto, Ayden Mayeri, Thomas Mann, Chrissie Fit, Andre Hyland, Chelsea Peretti, Margaret Cho, and Darrell Hammond

How to watch: Cora Bora is now streaming on Netflix.

Want a movie that's got excitement, comedy, a scorching message about the evils of gentrification, and is a kid-friendly romp? Then take a bite out of Vampires vs. the Bronx.

Oz Rodriguez's PG-13 horror-comedy centers on Afro-Latino teens who recognize that a flurry of missing person posters and an influx of rich white folks with tote bags means bad news for the neighborhood. Together, they team up Monster Squad-style to take down the bloodsuckers and save their community. With a sharp wit, a warm heart, a rich sense of atmosphere, and an equal appreciation for the Blade movies and '80s Amblin, Vampires vs. the Bronx is an easy watch full of rewards.* — K.P.

Starring: Jaden Michael, Gerald W. Jones III, Gregory Diaz IV, and Sarah Gadon

How to watch: Vampires vs. the Bronx is now streaming on Netflix.

Speaking of delicious, this comedy written by Liz Maccie and directed by her husband Stephen Chbosky will have you craving a home-cooked Italian meal.

Inspired by the true story of the restaurant Enoteca Maria, Nonnas stars Vince Vaughn as an average Joe who channels his grief over losing his mother and grandmother into a tantalizing new venture. He uses his inheritance to build a new restaurant in Staten Island, and hires Italian grandmothers (or nonnas) to become the chefs using their family recipes. Personalities clash, but ultimately these strong-willed women come together to create something stupendous.

It's a feel-good comedy full of love, joy, and laughs.* — K.P.

Starring: Vince Vaughn, Lorraine Bracco, Talia Shire, Brenda Vaccaro, Joe Manganiello, Linda Cardellini, and Susan Sarandon

How to watch: Nonnas is now streaming on Netflix.

This animated adventure from co-directors Alyce Tzue and John Ripa reimagines the Cinderella story from the point of view of so-called "evil stepsister" Lilith (voiced by Ali Wong). Sure, she'll grant you she's not always been kind to her orphaned stepsister, but as a goth girl in a kingdom of girly wannabe princesses all in pink, Lilith has had a tough time herself. So, she acts out with a stolen wand, accidentally derailing Cinderella's happily ever after. To save the day — and the kingdom — these sisters will have to reconnect and work together.

Along the way, there'll be fun fashion, magical mischief, and a hot troll named Gef. This is a fairy tale you won't want to miss. — K.P.

Starring: Ali Wong, Amanda Seyfried, Stephanie Hsu, Nikki Glaser, Daniel Radcliffe, Young Mazino, Peter Dinklage, and Bette Midler

How to watch: Steps debuts on Netflix on Nov. 20.

Want some side-splitting comedy about the ups and downs of female friendship? Then you'll relish Babes.

Directed by Pamela Adlon, this hilarious movie stars Survival of the Thickest's Michelle Buteau and Broad City's Ilana Glazer as two best friends since childhood, who begin to grow apart as they become parents. But fret not, sisterhood conquers all, even when things get literally shitty. As I cheered in my review, "Babes is a comedy that is unabashedly outrageous and rambunctiously heartfelt. While you may howl with laughter and gasp at a gross-out gag, you'll also walk away with a big smile — and maybe a bit more appreciation for your own quirks." — K.P.

Starring: Ilana Glazer, Michelle Buteau, John Carroll Lynch, Oliver Platt, Sandra Bernhard, Stephan James, and Hasan Minhaj

How to watch: Babes is now streaming on Netflix.

Eddie Murphy returns as wise-cracking Detroit police officer Axel Foley. 30 years after Beverly Hills Cop III, this sensational sequel sees Foley returning to Los Angeles to fight a new foe, reunite with old friends, and reconnect with his estranged daughter.

Reprising their supporting roles are Beverly Hills Cop co-stars John Ashton, Judge Reinhold, Paul Reiser, and Bronson Pinchot. New to the crew is Taylour Paige as Axel's hard-headed daughter, Joseph Gordon-Levitt as a local ally, Nasim Pedrad as a flustered real estate agent, and Kevin Bacon as a smirking antagonist. The sparks will fly. The punchlines hit powerfully. The soundtrack is fire. And just like that, the heat is back on! — K.P.

Starring: Eddie Murphy, Joseph Gordon-Levitt, Taylour Paige, Judge Reinhold, John Ashton, Paul Reiser, Bronson Pinchot, and Kevin Bacon

How to watch: Beverly Hills Cop: Axel F is now streaming on Netflix.

Zach Galifianakis' awkward celebrity interview web series Between Two Ferns might not seem like fodder for a full-length movie, but Between Two Ferns: The Movie goes double meta by presenting his success on Funny or Die as an insult to the actor's intention to host a "serious" interview show. Galifianakis the character sets off to correct the assumption that his original show is a joke by interviewing even more celebrities, like John Cho, Keanu Reeves, Tiffany Haddish, and more. — Alexis Nedd, Senior Entertainment Reporter

Starring: Zach Galifianakis, Lauren Lapkus, Ryan Gaul, Jiavani Linayao, Edi Patterson, Rekha Shankar, Mary Scheer, Mary Holland, Matt Besser, Phil Hendrie, Paul Rust, A. D. Miles, Blake Clark, Paul F. Tompkins, Demi Adejuyigbe, and Mandell Maughan

How to watch: Between Two Ferns: The Movie is now streaming on Netflix.

Raunchy, hilarious, and ultimately heart-warming, Bridesmaids is a terrific comedy even when the crush of wedding season has come to an end. Kristen Wiig stars as a down-on-her-luck baker who feels her longest-lasting relationship with her soon-to-be-married best friend (Maya Rudolph) is threatened when a picture-perfect bridesmaid (Rose Byrne) muscles in on maid-of-honor duties. Co-starring Melissa McCarthy, Ellie Kemper, Wendi McLendon-Covey, Jon Hamm, and Chris O'Dowd, this Paul Feig-directed comedy about female friendship offers everything from Saturday Night Live-style silliness to outrageous improvisations and an explosive poop joke that results in a hysterical sequence impossible to forget. Get ready to party with the bad girls of comedy. You won't regret it.* —K.P.

Starring: Kristen Wiig, Maya Rudolph, Rose Byrne, Wendi McLendon-Covey, Ellie Kemper, Melissa McCarthy, and Chris O'Dowd

How to watch: Bridesmaids is now streaming on Netflix.

What if the one who got away got a second chance? Back in the day, Sasha and Marcus were tight as a slap bracelet. But one fumbled night in a sweaty backseat seemed to shatter their future together.

Fifteen years later, she's a world-renowned chef and he's — well — he's working for his dad's HVAC business and playing with his band on the side. Still, fate arranges for them to reconnect, which could mean Marcus (Randall Park) has a shot to show Sasha (Ali Wong) how he feels. Comedy ensues as the stakes get high when Keanu Reeves crashes the party, being all weird and hot and having the audacity to be Keanu Reeves!* — K.P.

Starring: Ali Wong, Randall Park, James Saito, Michelle Buteau, Vivian Bang, Daniel Dae Kim, and Keanu Reeves

How to watch: Always Be My Maybe is now streaming on Netflix.

Chronicling the true story of late comedian Rudy Ray Moore — also known as Dolemite — this Eddie Murphy vehicle is worth every minute of viewing.

An outrageous showbiz comedy, Dolemite Is My Name is at once a poignant look at the life of an underdog and an unbelievably good time. With supporting performances by Da'Vine Joy Randolph, Wesley Snipes, Craig Robinson, and more, this biopic offers more beat-for-beat joy than many of its fictional counterparts. Sensational, aspirational, and electric: You'll love it. — Alison Foreman, Entertainment Reporter

Starring: Eddie Murphy, Keegan-Michael Key, Mike Epps, Craig Robinson, Da'Vine Joy Randolph, Tituss Burgess, and Wesley Snipes

How to watch: Dolemite Is My Name is now streaming on Netflix.

Comedian Diane Morgan has been gifting the world with her character of Philomena Cunk for a glorious decade and a half now. A dim-witted investigative journalist who butts heads with the real-world leading minds on each subject, Cunk proved to be a one-woman feast of absolute nonsense. The character Cunk started getting her own specials in 2016 with Cunk on Christmas, followed by Cunk on Britain and the epic globe-trotting mockumentary series Cunk on Earth (also streaming on Netflix).

Morgan's expanded her reach all the way to Cunk on Life, her latest side-splitter that tries to uncover the very meaning of existence itself across its humble 71-minute runtime. Does Cunk succeed? Well, Cunk definitely thinks she does, every damn time. But if not, at least we'll get to hear some Technotronic in the process, dammit! — Jason Adams, Contributing Writer

How to watch: Cunk on Life is now streaming on Netflix.

Sony Pictures Animation has given audiences such daring and dynamic animated movies as Surf's Up, Hotel Transylvania, and Spider-Man: Into the Spider-Verse. This zany 2021 release centers on a family who's battling back the robo-apocalypse with togetherness, internet savviness, and a wall-eyed pug named Monchi (voiced by social media icon Doug the Pug).

The Mitchells' adventure into chaos begins when daughter Katie (Abbi Jacobson) is poised to go off to college. Desperate for one last family-unifying road trip, her dad (Danny McBride) piles the whole family into his beater of a vehicle, unknowingly charting a fateful route into heroics. Co-directors Mike Rianda and Jeff Rowe infuse Katie's excitable perspective throughout the film by working in internet memes, social media-style reactions, and blitz of bonkers visuals. It’s a bold move that might alienate some viewers but has largely won the acclaim of critics and kiddos.* — K.P.

Starring: Danny McBride, Abbi Jacobson, Maya Rudolph, Mike Rianda, Eric Andre, and Olivia Colman

How to watch: The Mitchells vs. The Machines is now streaming on Netflix.

Writer/director Rian Johnson follows up his critically heralded whodunnit with a sequel that's even more explosive than Knives Out. Southern gentleman/detective Benoit Blanc (Daniel Craig) is back, drawling deductions and whipping out witticisms, much to the chagrin of a group of wealthy and conniving friends.

It was supposed to be a murder mystery weekend where homicide was just a game. But when tech scion Miles Bron (Edward Norton) brings together his closest friends and worst enemy, real blood will be spilled, and the game is afoot! And if you want more cryptic comedy, check out Glass Onion's sequel Wake Up, Dead Man! — K.P.

Starring: Daniel Craig, Edward Norton, Kate Hudson, Janelle Monáe, Dave Bautista, Kathryn Hahn, Leslie Odom Jr., and Jessica Henwick

How to watch: Glass Onion is now streaming on Netflix.

The Forty-Year-Old Version isn’t just any film about a struggling New York artist, but one about a Black female playwright who’s continually held back from creating something authentic to her perspective. Radha Blank's autobiographical directorial debut, which she also wrote, produced, and stars in, is a searing, funny, and unabashedly honest look at the compromises Black creatives are pushed to make in a world dominated by whiteness.

A teacher approaching her 40th birthday, Radha (Blank) gets the chance to produce one of her plays — only after she refuses to write a slave musical. But forced to change her vision to appease white audiences, Radha considers a totally different medium to express herself: becoming a rapper. The Forty-Year-Old Version may be one of the most slept-on Netflix originals, and it’s a shame we've yet to see another film from Blank since, itself an indictment of an industry that fails to continually platform raw and honest Black storytelling.* — Oliver Whitney, Contributing Writer

Starring: Radha Blank, Peter Kim, Oswin Benjamin, and Reed Birney

How to watch: The Forty-Year-Old Version is now streaming on Netflix.

Sometimes true crime can lead to some pitch-perfect dark comedy. This is the case for this outrageous offering, which stars Jack Black as infamous scammer/local celebrity Jan Lewan. Black brings all the rock star panache you need to understand how Lewan could be so beguiling to the Pennsylvanian retirees who surrendered their savings to the self-proclaimed Polka King.

Screenwriters Maya Forbes and Wallace Wolodarsky gave this story of fraud and showmanship shape. But they wisely trusted in the local flair of the terrific 2009 documentary The Man Who Would Be Polka King to provide some of the wildest lines of dialogue. Plucking directly from interviews with the friends, family, and victims of Jan Lewan, this comedy feels stranger than fiction but is jaw-droppingly real. Salty supporting turns from Jenny Slate, Jacki Weaver, and Jason Schwartzman bring added fun.* — K.P.

Starring: Jack Black, Jenny Slate, Jason Schwartzman, and Jacki Weaver

How to watch: The Polka King is now streaming on Netflix.

This hidden gem comes from New Zealand, the fertile comedy ground that gave us Taika Waititi, Flight of the Conchords, and What We Do in the Shadows. Waititi collaborators Jackie van Beek, James Rolleston, and Jemaine Clement team up for a deeply quirky buddy comedy about two long-time besties with a bonkers — but brilliant — business model.

Need someone to dump your partner so you can avoid a messy confrontation? Call on Jen and Mel (co-writers/co-directors/co-leads van Beek and Madeleine Sami). For a reasonable fee, these fearless Breaker Upperers will impersonate police officers, play pregnant, or even fake your death to help you ghost an ex. Whatever the shenanigans, van Beek and Sami sparkle. Booming with wild humor and big heart, this comedy is guaranteed to leave you cackling.* — K.P.

Starring: Madeleine Sami and Jackie van Beek

Where to watch: The Breaker Upperers is now streaming on Netflix.

Sir Ben Kingsley, Helen Mirren, Pierce Brosnan, and Celia Imrie bring to life the brilliant pensioners at the heart of Richard Osman's best-selling novel The Thursday Murder Club.

In the charming retirement village of Cooper's Chase, a club made up of a former M16 agent, a once-feared union activist, a wise therapist, and a cheerful retired nurse use their lifetimes of skills to crack cold cases. But when fresh blood is spilled in their social circle, the Thursday Murder Club hunts for clues, leading them to spooky settings and dangerous people.

Director Chris Columbus offers a cozy crime thriller that's sure to delight fans of the book series. And we suspect it'll hook newcomers too, as his cast dazzles with every quip and daring deduction. — K.P.

Starring: Helen Mirren, Pierce Brosnan, Ben Kingsley, and Celia Imrie with David Tennant, Jonathan Pryce, Naomi Ackie, Daniel Mays, Henry Lloyd-Hughes, Richard E. Grant, Tom Ellis, Geoff Bell, Paul Freeman, and Ingrid Oliver

How to watch: Thursday Murder Club is now streaming on Netflix.

Richard Linklater and Glen Powell reunite for Hit Man, a rom-com that is killer in all sense of the word.

Powell plays Gary Johnson, a college professor whose side gig as an undercover hit man leads to an unexpected meet-cute with would-be client Madison (Andor's Adria Arjona). The pair's connection (and electrifying chemistry) sparks a delightful game of false identities, reinvention, and twisted love that toggles between hilarious, thrilling, and sexy at a moment's notice. Oh, who am I kidding; sometimes it's all three at once!* — B.E.

Starring: Glen Powell, Adria Arjona, Austin Amelio, Retta, and Sanjay Rao

How to watch: Hit Man is now streaming on Netflix.

Our pals Wallace the inventor and his loyal pup Gromit have been running around for nearly four cracking decades now, but their latest adventure (and only their second feature-length film, after 2005's The Curse of the Were-Rabbit) proves they've lost not a whit of their vim and verve. Nominated for Best Animated Feature at the Oscars, Wallace & Gromit: Vengeance Most Fowl happily proves to be yet another gut-busting bit of British whimsy from Aardman Animation head Nick Park and his team of clay-playing creatives.

Vengeance Most Fowl sees our cheese-loving duo facing down a familiar foe — the diabolical penguin mastermind Feathers McGraw, who was last seen being sent off to imprisonment in the City Zoo in the classic 1993 short The Wrong Trousers. Hacking into Wallace's latest invention, a garden gnome robot named Norbot that he's created to do all of the yard work, Feathers builds himself an army of killer gnomes all programmed to do his bidding. And he wants nothing more than to bid adieu to his nemesis Wallace! — J.A.

#618. NASA’s Swift telescope falls to Earth after nearly 22 years in space

NASA's Neil Gehrels Swift Observatory has returned to Earth after spending nearly 22 years in space, bringing an extraordinary era of astronomical discovery to a close. The spacecraft re-entered Earth's atmosphere on Wednesday, ending a mission that lasted more than ten times longer than originally planned.

Launched in 2004, Swift was a pioneering space observatory equipped with three telescopes designed to study some of the most powerful explosions in the universe. Its instruments could detect different types of light, allowing scientists to investigate energetic cosmic events that would be difficult to understand using a single telescope.

NASA's Swift Observatory Re-Enters Earth's Atmosphere

The U.S. Space Force confirmed that Swift entered the atmosphere at 7:39 a.m. EST on Wednesday above North Sumatra, Indonesia. The reported location was approximately 3.4 degrees north latitude and 98.6 degrees east longitude.

NASA anticipated that most of the spacecraft would burn up as it plunged through the atmosphere. However, officials acknowledged that some components might have survived the intense heat of re-entry.

A Two-Year NASA Mission That Lasted Nearly 22 Years

Swift was originally developed to investigate gamma-ray bursts, extraordinarily powerful flashes of high-energy radiation associated with some of the most extreme events in the cosmos. These bursts can occur when massive stars collapse or when certain compact objects, such as neutron stars, merge.

Although NASA initially planned for Swift to operate for just two years, the observatory continued making scientific contributions for more than two decades.

One of its most important capabilities was its ability to respond quickly to sudden cosmic events. When Swift detected an energetic outburst, it could rapidly redirect its telescopes toward the source and notify observatories around the world. This coordinated approach allowed astronomers to study brief astronomical phenomena before they faded from view.

Swift also observed multiple wavelengths of light, giving researchers different ways to examine the same event and better understand the physical processes behind it.

Over its remarkably long lifetime, Swift's scientific contributions expanded far beyond its original focus on gamma-ray bursts.

The observatory helped astronomers investigate active galaxies powered by supermassive black holes, stars that produce powerful X-ray flares, and supernovae, the enormous explosions that mark the deaths of certain stars.

Its observations also extended much closer to home, including studies of comets and asteroids within our own solar system.

By combining rapid observations with the ability to detect different forms of radiation, Swift became a valuable tool for exploring both distant cosmic explosions and nearby objects. Its re-entry marks the end of a mission that greatly exceeded its planned lifespan and helped transform scientists' understanding of some of the universe's most energetic phenomena.

#617. Newest Sample, Oldest Rock?

Written by Tasha Coelho, Mastcam-Z Student Collaborator at Arizona State University

Perseverance’s most recent abrasion “Pumbe” bears a striking resemblance to an earlier abrasion, “Badger Peak.” That’s no coincidence. As previously reported, that rock had a hardness too high to core. However, the team felt strongly that it was worth the effort to add a gabbro, an igneous rock made mostly of calcium-rich plagioclase feldspar and pyroxene crystals, to Perseverance’s sample collection. In search of a new local gabbroic candidate to sample, Perseverance traveled about 100 meters (330 feet) to a different patch of light-toned rocks. Indeed, visual and chemical data from the light-toned rocks in the new workspace revealed a composition similar to that of “Badger Peak.”

To improve the odds of collecting a sample this time, the team made prudent decisions: choosing a neighboring rock with a flatter surface better oriented for the drill stabilizers to press against, and using a coring bit with less wear. At last, in a display of the rover’s namesake quality, the drill made it about 3.1 centimeters (1.2 inches) into the rock. Sample acquisition activities followed, making “Singita” the rover’s first sample in more than a year. While Singita is the newest sample, it may be among the oldest rocks in Perseverance’s collection. Textures in the rock suggest that it likely formed deep in the crust and was modified and excavated by at least one, and possibly multiple, large impact events.

Perseverance has now completed its 66th successful abrasion and collected an intriguing new sample alongside it. The latest mission status report opens with “Perseverance remains healthy and stable on Mars” and lists “Spacecraft Status: Nominal.” Translation: the rover remains capable of continued fruitful exploration, with many more abrasions to come.

#615. I Let Pangram Loose on My Gmail Inbox. Here’s How Much AI It Found

AI-generated content is everywhere now: From restaurant menus and student essays to full-length movies and social media posts, AI is churning out huge amounts of text, images, audio, and video. In some digital spaces, it's overwhelming human-made content.

But what about your inbox? Just how many of your friends, family members, clients, and colleagues are using AI to compose their emails? Is that store newsletter you just signed up for written by a real person or a bot? It's not always easy to tell.

The Pangram AI text and image detector tool has now extended its reach into your Gmail inbox. The company makes a browser extension that sits in Gmail and runs incoming messages through its algorithms to try to detect AI writing.

You will need at least the $20-per-month individual plan on Pangram to use the Gmail integration—it's not available on the free tier. I tested it to see how accurate it appears to be based on my recent Gmail conversations.

You can let Pangram loose in your Gmail inbox by heading to the Gmail integration page and clicking Connect your Gmail. You can also click the Gmail Integration entry on the left-hand navigation pane in your main Pangram dashboard on the web. You'll need to go through the standard authorization screens to confirm you want to make the connection.

Pangram says the contents of your emails aren't stored after being assessed and won't be used to train models designed to look for AI text. There's still a level of trust here, but you can quickly remove the Pangram plug-in from your Gmail at any point.

After you connect your Gmail account, Pangram gets to work. It'll scan your 100 most recent email threads and then any new messages from then on—you can't get it to check all of the emails you've ever received. As far as I can tell, email scanning doesn't count against your scan credit limit for the month, so you're covered no matter how busy your inbox is.

After scanning, the Gmail Integration part of your Pangram dashboard changes. You can see at a glance how many of your emails are suspected to be AI, how many are most probably human, and how many have been flagged as a mix between the two. Inside Gmail, these three categories can be applied as labels, though this is optional. (If you decide to remove Pangram from your inbox later, any labels it's created will stick around, but you can delete these manually.)

You can also choose to send incoming AI-written emails straight to the archive or to spam if you want, which is handy for keeping AI content away from your eyeballs. You can also choose to exclude specific domains, Gmail labels, subject keywords, and senders from Pangram's scans, if you don't want emails from your boss or mom scrutinized.

So the moment of truth: Out of the 108 threads that Pangram scanned, eight of them were labeled as AI, 37 got the human label, and the remainder were marked as mixed. It's a little dispiriting to discover that way more than half of my incoming messages are probably AI-written, with press releases the most common culprits.

In terms of accuracy, none of the emails I know to have been written by flesh-and-blood people were flagged as fully or partly AI. Everything from friends and family and colleagues I know passed the human test, so for this small sample size at least, Pangram's detection algorithms seem to be working.

I sent a couple of emails to myself from different accounts to see what Pangram would make of them. One was written with Microsoft Copilot, which the detector correctly flagged as AI. The other was a section of this article, written with my own brain and keyboard taps, and again Pangram got it right—it was marked with the Human label.

There does seem to be a minimum email length, and if an email’s word count falls below that threshold, Pangram won't make a decision either way, which is understandable. (AI is harder to detect with a smaller sample size). Only the first email in each thread is scanned (and optionally labeled), so if your correspondent switches to AI halfway through a conversation, it wouldn't get spotted.

I like the way the scanner stays in the background and just gets on with its job, and I can see myself using it long-term. It really does affect your mindset if you know a message has been entirely put together using AI. Of course, Gmail being Gmail, you can quickly fire off an AI response as well, should you want to.

#614. Hurdle hints and answers for November 9, 2026

If you like playing daily word games like Wordle, then Hurdle is a great game to add to your routine.

There are five rounds to the game. The first round sees you trying to guess the word, with correct, misplaced, and incorrect letters shown in each guess. If you guess the correct answer, it'll take you to the next hurdle, providing the answer to the last hurdle as your first guess. This can give you several clues or none, depending on the words. For the final hurdle, every correct answer from previous hurdles is shown, with correct and misplaced letters clearly shown.

An important note is that the number of times a letter is highlighted from previous guesses does necessarily indicate the number of times that letter appears in the final hurdle.

#613. Don't miss: Meteor showers, fireballs, and a supermoon light up November's night sky

November 2026 is packed with celestial events worth watching, from brilliant meteors streaking across the sky to a striking encounter between Mars and Jupiter. NASA's Voyager 1 spacecraft is also approaching an extraordinary milestone, while a dazzling supermoon will close out the month.

Three meteor showers offer multiple opportunities to catch shooting stars, including occasional fireballs bright enough to stand out dramatically against the night sky. Meanwhile, two of the solar system's most recognizable planets will appear unusually close together just before sunrise.

As if that weren't enough, humanity's most distant spacecraft is reaching a point where radio signals from Earth take an entire day to arrive.

Here's what NASA recommends watching for in November.

Three Meteor Showers Could Light Up November's Sky

November gives skywatchers several chances to see meteors flash across the darkness. The first opportunities come from the Southern and Northern Taurids, two related meteor showers that reach their highest activity levels about a week apart.

The Southern Taurids peak around November 5, followed by the Northern Taurids around November 12. Both showers get their names from the constellation Taurus, where their meteors appear to originate when viewed from Earth.

Neither shower is especially prolific. Even under excellent conditions, each typically produces only about five meteors per hour. However, the Taurids are known for occasionally producing impressive fireballs, exceptionally bright meteors that can make even a relatively quiet night of skywatching memorable.

The Leonids provide another opportunity later in the month. Their best viewing periods are expected during the early morning hours of November 17 and 18, particularly after the Moon has disappeared below the horizon. Under ideal conditions, observers could see approximately 15 meteors per hour, although actual counts will generally be lower.

For the best chance of spotting meteors, travel somewhere away from bright city lights. Allow your eyes roughly 30 minutes to become accustomed to the darkness, then watch a broad section of the sky rather than concentrating on one small area.

Mars and Jupiter Appear Almost Side by Side

Meteor showers aren't the only reason to get outside before dawn. On November 15 and 16, Mars and Jupiter will create an eye-catching pairing in the southeastern sky.

The two planets will appear separated by only about 1.2 degrees, slightly more than the apparent width of two full Moons placed next to each other.

Jupiter will be the brighter of the two, making it relatively easy to identify. Nearby, Mars will stand out with its characteristic reddish appearance.

No telescope or binoculars will be necessary to enjoy the sight. Both planets should be readily visible to the unaided eye under suitable viewing conditions.

Although they will appear remarkably close from Earth's perspective, Mars and Jupiter remain separated by enormous distances in space. Their apparent proximity is simply a result of their positions along our line of sight.

Watching the planets over several consecutive mornings will reveal another interesting feature of their motion. They will gradually appear to move closer together before their positions shift and they begin separating again.

Voyager 1 Reaches an Extraordinary Deep Space Milestone

While November offers plenty to observe in Earth's skies, something equally remarkable is happening far beyond the planets.

NASA's Voyager 1 spacecraft is reaching a historic distance of one light-day from Earth, becoming the first human-made object ever to travel that far from our planet.

A light-day is the distance light travels through space in 24 hours. Because radio signals move at the speed of light, a message transmitted from Earth will require an entire day to reach Voyager 1 at that distance.

If the spacecraft sends a response immediately, the return signal will take another 24 hours to arrive. That means even the fastest possible round-trip communication will require approximately 48 hours.

The milestone illustrates just how far Voyager 1 has traveled since leaving Earth and how challenging it has become to communicate with a spacecraft exploring such distant regions of space.

To get a sense of the spacecraft's current direction, look toward the constellation Ophiuchus, also known as the Serpent Bearer. Voyager 1 is traveling through space in that general direction, far beyond anything visible to the unaided eye.

NASA is marking the achievement on November 17 with its Shine Bright for Voyager's Light-Day activation, inviting people to celebrate this extraordinary accomplishment in space exploration.

November's Supermoon Begins a Three-Month Celestial Show

The final major skywatching highlight arrives on November 24, when a full supermoon will rise into the evening sky.

A supermoon occurs when a full Moon coincides with a period when the Moon is relatively close to Earth in its orbit. Because the Moon follows a slightly elliptical path rather than a perfect circle, its distance from our planet changes throughout the month.

When a full Moon occurs near the closer portion of that orbit, it can appear somewhat larger and brighter than an average full Moon. Although the difference is real, it is often subtle enough that casual observers may not immediately notice it.

November's event is particularly noteworthy because it begins a sequence of three consecutive supermoons. Another will follow in December, with a third arriving in January.

For an especially memorable view, NASA recommends looking toward the eastern horizon around sunset on November 24, when the full Moon will be rising.

The Moon may seem unusually enormous when it first appears near the horizon. However, much of that dramatic impression comes from a familiar visual phenomenon called the Moon illusion.

This optical effect makes the Moon appear larger when it is close to the horizon than when it is high overhead, even though its actual apparent size changes very little. The illusion can occur during any full Moon, not just a supermoon.

With three meteor showers, a close planetary pairing, a historic Voyager 1 milestone, and the beginning of a supermoon series, November offers plenty of reasons to look up and explore the night sky.

Materials provided by NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#612. Rubies on Mars?

Written by Roger C. Wiens, SuperCam Principal Investigator for NASA’s Perseverance rover at Purdue University

Exploration on Mars turns up the unexpected. Perseverance is equipped with a fantastic set of instruments for studying the rock record of this ancient planet. Both PIXL and SuperCam have great capabilities for detecting all kinds of elements. SHERLOC, SuperCam, and PIXL can detect minerals using various techniques including x-ray fluorescence, Raman, and infrared spectroscopies. As a side benefit of the way SuperCam performs its Raman spectroscopy, it can also study time-resolved luminescence. Normally luminescence occurs over a broad wavelength range (most of Earth’s commercial lighting uses luminescence in some way or another). Two years ago, the SuperCam team was startled to find strong, narrow luminescence peaks at two wavelengths of 692.7 and 694.1 nanometers when they shot a light-colored rock with the laser. This luminescence comes from delayed atomic transitions in chromium atoms present in a corundum mineral structure. SuperCam can time the delay of the atomic transitions via the resulting photons, and so we could verify that this emission came from chromium in corundum. The results were published in Geophysical Research Letters by a team led by Ann Ollila earlier this year, so you can read all about it. The gemstone version of chromium-containing corundum is ruby. Prior to installing SuperCam on Perseverance, we placed a ruby in the laser beam and watched the entire room pulse a brilliant red as SuperCam’s laser beam hit the gemstone; it was extremely bright.

While we haven’t found collectable rubies on Mars yet, the little corundum crystals are helping us learn more about Mars’ past. Corundum is a metamorphic and pegmatitic mineral which we would not expect to find on Mars, a planet that lacks plate tectonics. However, large meteor and asteroid impacts can create local metamorphism due to long-term heating from the impact. Because we are in a very ancient terrain near the rim of Jezero crater, we can expect some evidence of localized metamorphism. As the first mission in such terrain, we are learning new things. Earlier in the mission we tried this technique only rarely; lately we have carried out the time-resolved luminescence observations frequently on light-toned rocks to look for more evidence of impact effects. It’s great to have this unexpected tool on board as we delve further into Mars’ deep past.

#611. Hidden climate superpower: Why some ocean “snow” carries six times more carbon

Far below the ocean's surface, a constant snowfall drifts through the darkness. But unlike the frozen flakes that fall from the sky, this underwater snow consists of tiny clumps of waste, dead organisms, and other debris. Known as marine snow, these particles contain everything from mucus and animal feces to silt and the remains of microscopic plankton.

As this material sinks, it carries enormous quantities of carbon from the upper ocean toward the seafloor. Researchers estimate that marine snow removes more than 10 gigatons of carbon from surface waters each year. Yet only about two gigatons reach depths where that carbon can remain stored for hundreds or even thousands of years.

Why so much carbon fails to make it into the deep ocean has long puzzled scientists. Now, researchers from UC San Diego's Scripps Institution of Oceanography and the University of Lincoln have identified a surprisingly important factor: how sticky the marine snow is.

Their findings, published October 8 in the journal Science, show that the viscosity, or thickness and gooeyness, of marine snow influences how quickly bacteria break it apart and how far it can sink. According to the researchers, the stickiest particles can transport more than six times as much carbon to the deep ocean as particles with a thinner, less viscous consistency.

Marine snow plays an important role in Earth's carbon cycle, the natural movement of carbon among the atmosphere, oceans, land, and living organisms. By transporting carbon into deep water, sinking particles can keep some of it isolated from the atmosphere for centuries or longer.

However, not every particle survives the journey. Bacteria feed on marine snow as it descends, gradually breaking down the material and affecting how much carbon reaches the ocean's depths.

The new research provides the first detailed look at how living bacteria interact with the physical structure of marine snow and, more broadly, mucus itself. These interactions are important well beyond the ocean's carbon cycle. Similar relationships between bacteria and mucus help sustain coral reefs, kelp forests, plant root systems, and the digestive systems of humans and other animals.

Until recently, scientists lacked the technology to observe these interactions at the microscopic scale needed to understand them.

A Four-Year Quest to See Inside Marine Snow

The project began in 2021, when Bryce Inman, then a postdoctoral researcher at Scripps Oceanography, set out to explore what he calls "the muscoscape."

"Observing individual bacteria interacting with the physical structure of mucus had never been possible," said Inman.

Together with colleagues including Farooq Azam, a microbiologist and professor emeritus at Scripps, Inman spent four years trying to overcome that limitation. Multiple attempts were unsuccessful before the team finally developed a working technique in 2025.

Their solution combined 3D confocal microscopy with specially designed fluorescent molecules. Confocal microscopy uses light to capture detailed images at different depths within a sample, allowing researchers to reconstruct its structure in three dimensions. The fluorescent molecules helped make features of the material visible.

For the first time, the scientists could examine the "physical terrain" of individual marine snow particles while watching how living bacteria moved through and interacted with them.

The images revealed a surprisingly intricate microscopic environment, complete with structural differences that affect where bacteria can settle and how easily they can reach the material inside.

Much like snowflakes in the atmosphere, marine snow particles vary considerably. Each contains its own mixture of organic and inorganic materials, creating differences in composition, structure, and viscosity.

The researchers' new imaging approach, described in a companion study published in Nature Communications, revealed that bacteria preferentially colonize areas near a particle's surface where the material is less viscous.

The denser, stickier regions deeper inside are much harder for bacteria to penetrate.

"Bacteria can latch on to less viscous portions of each particle, but cannot reach more viscous regions until they break it down," said Inman. "Think of it like a worm in an apple: the flesh is easy to penetrate, but the core is tough to chew through"

This difference has important consequences for how long marine snow remains intact.

In laboratory experiments, the scientists observed that particles with greater viscosity resisted bacterial degradation and fragmentation for longer periods. Computer simulations further suggested that these stickier particles can descend to greater depths before breaking apart.

Based on these results, the team estimates that highly viscous marine snow can deliver more than six times as much carbon to the deep ocean as less viscous particles.

The findings help explain why some sinking material transports carbon much farther into the ocean than other particles, even though both begin their journeys near the surface.

"How far marine snow can sink is a surprisingly complicated problem, and we have added another - very gooey - piece to the puzzle," said Inman.

A Discovery With Implications Beyond the Ocean

Although the research focuses on marine snow, its implications extend to many environments where bacteria interact with mucus.

Mucus is often regarded as a relatively simple, slippery substance. But the new imaging technology reveals that it has a complex internal landscape, with microscopic structures that can influence how bacteria behave.

Understanding these physical features could help scientists investigate biological processes in settings ranging from human digestive health to the recovery of damaged coral reefs.

The method also opens opportunities to explore interactions that previously could not be observed directly, providing researchers with a new way to study the microscopic environments inhabited by bacteria.

Mucus, slime, whatever you want to call it, "has a surprising amount of structure," Inman said. "There are bulges, crevasses, all this sort of structure to it that we really had no idea about at a scale smaller than bacteria. This is the first time we're really examining this physical habitat."

The discovery adds an important missing piece to scientists' understanding of how carbon travels through the ocean. It also demonstrates that something as seemingly ordinary as the thickness of underwater slime can have far-reaching consequences for the planet's natural systems.

Additional funding for the research was provided by the Simons Foundation and the Engineering and Physical Sciences Research Council.

#608. Curiosity Blog, Sols 5050-5055: It’s Nazca, Our 49th Drill Hole

Mars Science Laboratory Mission Team Members

Written by Susanne P. Schwenzer, Professor of Planetary Mineralogy at The Open University, UK

Earth planning date: Friday, Oct. 23, 2026

As regular readers of this blog will know, we returned to the area where we found disc shaped inclusions in the rocks, which we interpreted as potential crystals. If they are indeed crystals, and we compare them to Earth, they could be very interesting indicators of the environmental conditions. Hence, the excitement in the team is high that we found a good block to drill. Good means first and foremost that it contains abundant potential crystals, but of course there is more before we drill. We passed the rover’s stability test, as all wheels have to be on solid ground before we extend the arm. Then we passed the preload test, which tests the stability of the rock and the forces the arm experiences when the drill bit presses into the rock.

With all those tests passed, we successfully drilled our 49th drill hole, called “Nazca.”

The drill campaign comes with many investigations before and after the drilling itself. Before the drill, we investigated the block with APXS, MAHLI and ChemCam – all on the target Nazca. After drilling, the sample was dropped off to CheMin, with the results still making their way from Mars to Earth. They are eagerly anticipated to reach us over the coming weekend. ChemCam obtained both passive spectral and active LIBS of the tailings and a LIBS measurement down the drill hole wall.

Other ChemCam activities this week included a LIBS investigation of “Palpala,” which is a dark pebble. We are on the lookout for those pebbles for a while and find that not all of them have the same composition. To make sure we understand their origin, we are measuring them when there are some in reach that are big enough. If a pebble is too small, it can move when it is being hit by the laser. Hence, we need to look out for some that are big enough.

Another such pebble was the target “Puna” identified in Friday’s planning. ChemCam LIBS on the target “Llama” is to get another depth profile to check whether our observations in the first such depth profile are consistently observed or whether they vary between locations.

The target “Pacaya” is another measurement of the disc shaped forms that drew us back to this region. In addition to the LIBS measurements, ChemCam has several long distance RMIs in the plan. They are all focused on the two buttes that have drawn a long-standing interest of the team for the various sedimentary structures and textures they reveal: Mishe Mokwa and Cordillera.

#607. These Robotic Arms Are Powered by Algae. Watch How They Move

What if a robot could be squishy? Soft, flexible robots could be useful for a wide variety of applications, including artificial muscles and wearable devices—or really any application where a robot must be especially delicate.

This is what has fueled development of “soft actuators” that can flex like a bicep and have a fine-tuned sense of touch as in the human hand. Like your stereotypical robot, soft actuators convert energy—such as electricity, heat, or light—into mechanical motion, but they look nothing like the metal and wire constructs that the word “robot” most often evokes.

One variety of these soft robots, known as bioactuators, utilizes the functions of living cells to generate motion. These biological machines have attracted particular attention in recent years, and now a research team at Delft University of Technology in the Netherlands has developed a soft robotic arm colonized by algae on its surface, and when the microbes photosynthesize, the arm moves.

“We didn’t start with a specific application. Our goal has always been to bring the research of living materials to new levels,” Marie-Eve Aubin-Tam, who led the research, explained in a press release. “We wanted to explore what becomes possible when living cells are integrated into functional materials.”

The tiny robot arms—about three-quarters of an inch long and about as thick as a credit card—are constructed of two layers of hydrogel that the algal cells live on. One of these layers responds to changes in pH, which are mediated by the metabolism of the algae. When light strikes the algae, they begin to photosynthesize. By absorbing carbon dioxide and fixing nitrogen in this process, the algae make the local pH more basic, making the responsive hydrogel swell. Conversely, when it becomes dark, the algae release carbon dioxide through respiration, returning the pH to the acidic side and the hydrogel shrinks.

The difference in the volumes of the two hydrogel layers controls the bending. According to the researchers, the pH-responsive hydrogel layer changed significantly with the algae’s activity. In acidic conditions, such as between pH 3 and 7, the layer was approximately 37 percent of its original weight, but it had quadruple its original weight at pH 11.

Other researchers have constructed soft robots that respond to pH, but typically the chemistry of all the surrounding liquid has to be altered. This is not the case for the algal bots. The photosynthetic activity has an effect that’s much more local, meaning it would be better suited to applications where minimal environmental impact is essential.

These aren’t, however, the speediest of robots. The chemical shift begins quickly, about 20 seconds after light is applied and about 10 seconds after it becomes dark, but it takes several hours for the hydrogel to bend significantly.

After their first proof of concept, the research team constructed several more algal devices. One was a four-fingered gripper that closes when exposed to light. It successfully grasped and lifted a hollow metal sphere, releasing it when the light was turned off. Another hook-shaped gripper could pick up and put down a rubber ring. These items were extremely light, weighing far less than a small paperclip, but the arms were able to grasp them with great dexterity.

These soft robots also require no external energy source other than light and no complex mechanical parts. “Microalgae are plantlike organisms that are largely self-sustaining,” Aubin-Tam said. This “gives the actuator potential for long-term operation with minimal external input. In theory, it wouldn’t require a lot of energy to run a system based on this technology.”

In the long term, the research team hopes this will contribute to the development of more autonomous, environment-responsive soft robots that could be used as automated agricultural equipment or for fluid control in lab-on-a-chip devices or really as part of any underwater robotics system.

Since the algae do not need to be genetically modified, there are none of the biosafety concerns often associated with microbe-based technologies. If anything, these are exceptionally environmentally friendly machines, since these organisms can absorb carbon dioxide from the atmosphere while deriving energy from sunlight.

This story originally appeared on WIRED Japan and has been translated from Japanese.

#606. Stronger flu shot linked to nearly 55% lower Alzheimer's risk

A higher dose of the annual flu vaccine may offer older adults an unexpected benefit: a significantly lower risk of developing Alzheimer's disease.

In a large study led by researchers at UTHealth Houston, adults ages 65 and older who received a high-dose influenza vaccine were less likely to develop Alzheimer's than those given the standard-dose version. The findings, published in the medical journal Neurology, suggest that the strength of a flu vaccine could matter for more than just protection against influenza.

The discovery grew out of an unexpected conversation between a UTHealth Houston physician and local public health officials. What began as a visit to discuss earlier vaccine research eventually led scientists to investigate whether different flu vaccine doses might be associated with different levels of Alzheimer's risk.

A Surprising Connection Between Flu Vaccines and Alzheimer's

Alzheimer's disease is the leading cause of dementia and an increasingly serious public health concern. In 2025, more than 7 million Americans ages 65 and older were living with the disease, representing approximately 1 in 9 people in that age group. That figure is projected to more than double by 2050.

Researchers have been exploring whether common vaccines might influence the likelihood of developing Alzheimer's. In a 2022 study, a team led by Paul Schulz, MD, found that flu vaccination was associated with a reduced risk of Alzheimer's among adults 65 and older.

Schulz is a professor of neurology at McGovern Medical School at UTHealth Houston and director of the Neurocognitive Disorders Center at UTHealth Houston Neurosciences.

Three years after that earlier investigation, Schulz and his colleagues published new research examining a more specific question: Could receiving a stronger flu vaccine be associated with an even greater reduction in Alzheimer's risk?

The idea came from public health officials who had learned about the team's previous findings and invited Schulz to discuss them.

"The public health department had seen our vaccine research and asked if I could come down to talk to them about it," said Schulz, who is the senior author of the study and the Rick McCord Professor in Neurology and Umphrey Family Professor in Neurodegenerative Diseases at McGovern Medical School. "We went through the findings, and they asked if there was a difference with different dosages; I was confused."

Why Older Adults Receive Stronger Flu Shots

As people grow older, their immune systems gradually become less effective at recognizing and fighting infections. This age-related decline can make influenza particularly dangerous for seniors.

To help address that vulnerability, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) recommends high-dose influenza vaccines as a preferred option for adults ages 65 and older. These vaccines contain four times as much influenza antigen as standard-dose shots. Antigens are the components of a vaccine that stimulate the immune system to recognize and defend against a virus.

The higher dose is designed to produce a stronger immune response in older adults, whose immune systems may respond less effectively to conventional vaccines.

Schulz had not previously realized that this stronger formulation was available.

"I was stunned that, as a physician, I didn't know a higher dose was offered," Schulz said.

Annual flu vaccination is recommended broadly, but preventing influenza is especially important for people with Alzheimer's disease. Flu infections and their complications can pose serious health risks for individuals already coping with cognitive impairment.

Before 2022, the CDC did not express a preference for one type of injectable influenza vaccine over another for people ages 65 and older. Instead, the guidance emphasized receiving a flu shot every year, regardless of the specific formulation.

Previous research from UTHealth Houston had already linked inactivated influenza vaccines, which use viruses that cannot cause influenza infection, to a 40% lower risk of developing Alzheimer's disease. However, researchers had not yet determined whether a higher vaccine dose might be associated with additional benefits.

Another practical issue made the question particularly relevant. As the population of older Americans continues to grow, demand for high-dose flu vaccines sometimes exceeds the available supply. Consequently, some seniors receive standard-dose vaccines instead.

This variation gave researchers an opportunity to compare Alzheimer's outcomes among older adults who had received different formulations.

"So we started looking, and sure enough, we were able to put together thousands and thousands of people over the age of 65 who got the high- or low-dose vaccine. They're more at risk for Alzheimer's than anybody else, of course, because it's age-related and we could look at high-dose versus low-dose and see if there was a difference," Schulz said.

Nearly 200,000 Adults Reveal a Significant Difference

To investigate the potential connection, the research team analyzed data from nearly 200,000 men and women ages 65 and older.

The results showed that people who received high-dose influenza vaccines had a significantly lower risk of developing Alzheimer's disease than those who received the standard-dose formulation.

The researchers reported that standard-dose influenza vaccination was associated with a 40% reduction in Alzheimer's risk, while high-dose vaccination was linked to a reduction of nearly 55%.

The association was particularly pronounced among women, who appeared to experience a greater reduction in Alzheimer's risk with the stronger vaccine.

These findings add to growing evidence of a possible relationship between influenza vaccination and brain health. They also raise important questions about whether the immune response triggered by vaccination might influence biological processes involved in Alzheimer's disease.

However, the findings demonstrate an association rather than establishing cause and effect. Additional research is needed to determine whether high-dose flu vaccination directly reduces the likelihood of developing Alzheimer's and to understand the mechanisms that might explain the connection.

What the Findings Could Mean for Alzheimer's Prevention

With Alzheimer's affecting millions of Americans and the number of cases expected to rise sharply in the coming decades, researchers are searching for practical ways to reduce risk.

The possibility that an existing vaccine could have benefits extending beyond influenza prevention is particularly intriguing. Unlike an entirely new treatment, flu vaccination is already widely used, and higher-dose formulations are specifically designed for older adults.

The new findings suggest that vaccine dosage could be an important consideration in future studies exploring the relationship between the immune system and Alzheimer's disease.

For now, the research provides another reason to investigate the potential benefits of influenza vaccination while emphasizing the need for further studies before drawing conclusions about Alzheimer's prevention.

Avram Samuel Bukhbinder, MD, a graduate of McGovern Medical School who is currently a clinical fellow in neurology at Massachusetts General Hospital and Harvard University, served as a corresponding author of the study.

Schulz is also a faculty member of The University of Texas MD Anderson Cancer Center UTHealth Houston Graduate School of Biomedical Sciences.

Other contributors from McGovern Medical School included Elizabeth He, visiting scholar in the Department of Neurology; Kristofer Harris, MPH, research coordinator in the Department of Neurology; Mya Rodriguez, graduate research assistant in the Department of Neurology; Kamal Phelps, MD, an alumnus of the Department of Neurology; Jenna Thomas; Gabriela Cruz, MD, resident in the Department of Neurology; and Lauren Jhin.

Researchers from the McWilliams School of Biomedical Informatics at UTHealth Houston also participated. They included Xiaoqian Jiang, PhD, associate vice president for medical AI, chair of the Department of Health Data Science and Artificial Intelligence, and Christopher Sarofim Family Professor in Biomedical Informatics and Bioengineering; Yejin Kim, PhD, assistant professor in the Department of Health Data Science and Artificial Intelligence; Luyao Chen, researcher; and Yaobin Ling, PhD, postdoctoral research fellow.

#604. NYT Strands hints, answers for November 9, 2026

Today's NYT Strands hints will be easy if you're a chatterbox.

Strands, the New York Times' elevated word-search game, requires the player to perform a twist on the classic word search. Words can be made from linked letters — up, down, left, right, or diagonal, but words can also change direction, resulting in quirky shapes and patterns. Every single letter in the grid will be part of an answer. There's always a theme linking every solution, along with the "spangram," a special, word or phrase that sums up that day's theme, and spans the entire grid horizontally or vertically.

By providing an opaque hint and not providing the word list, Strands creates a brain-teasing game that takes a little longer to play than its other games, like Wordle and Connections.
