#603. Scientists discover a surprising link between acorns and lyme disease

Tick-borne illnesses are becoming increasingly common across the United States, and Lyme disease remains the most frequently reported. Nearly 500,000 Americans are diagnosed each year. The infection is caused by the bacterium Borrelia burgdorferi, which typically reaches humans through the bite of an infected blacklegged tick. Antibiotics can treat Lyme disease, but without treatment, the infection may affect the joints, heart, and nervous system. Some patients also experience symptoms that persist over time.

Now, one of the longest-running studies of Lyme disease ecology is challenging several assumptions about what makes the disease more likely to spread. For more than 35 years, scientists at the Cary Institute of Ecosystem Studies in Millbrook, New York, have tracked the complicated relationships among ticks, mice, oak trees, predators, and weather. Their research has uncovered unexpected patterns that could help predict when people face greater exposure to infected ticks.

A new study published in Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences brings together nine of the project's most surprising discoveries. The findings show how decades of observations can reveal connections that shorter studies might miss.

"On balance, these surprises have enriched our knowledge of this system, helping us to inform management of tick-borne disease risk," said lead author Richard Ostfeld, a disease ecologist at the Cary Institute who leads the research program alongside Shannon LaDeau.

According to Ostfeld, the extensive data collected over the years have been essential for separating meaningful ecological patterns from coincidences and statistical fluctuations.

"Studies of this depth and length are incredibly rare," said LaDeau. "This research has followed an ecological community -- including oak trees, mammals, and microorganisms -- examining how interactions like predation, parasitism, and competition shape the system over time. The evolution of understanding summarized in this paper is something you simply cannot get without such a long-term and system-focused study."

Deer May Play a Smaller Role in Lyme Disease Risk Than Expected

When Lyme disease first gained recognition in the United States during the 1980s, white-tailed deer were considered a likely driver of rising tick populations. Hunters frequently encountered deer carrying numerous adult ticks, suggesting that areas with more deer might also produce more ticks.

The reasoning seemed straightforward. Although deer cannot pass the Lyme disease bacterium to ticks, they provide blood meals for adult female ticks. Scientists suspected that larger deer populations would allow more female ticks to reproduce, eventually increasing the number of young ticks capable of spreading the infection.

But decades of monitoring have produced a different picture. On the Cary Institute's 2,000-acre forested research property in Dutchess County, New York, researchers found no statistically significant connection between deer numbers and the abundance of nymphal ticks. Nymphs are immature ticks responsible for much of the Lyme disease transmission to humans.

"We had detected a weak positive relationship between deer and subsequent nymph abundance over short subsets of the data," said Ostfeld, "but the whole data set in its entirety shows no such thing."

Mice Are a Much Stronger Predictor of Tick Surges

While deer populations failed to predict the abundance of nymphal ticks, mouse populations showed a much clearer relationship.

Years with especially large mouse populations were followed by approximately 40% more nymphal ticks the next year. White-footed mice are particularly important because they provide excellent blood meals for tick larvae and are highly effective at transmitting Borrelia burgdorferi to those feeding on them.

Once infected as larvae, ticks can retain the bacteria as they develop into nymphs. When these nymphs take another blood meal, they can pass the infection to humans.

The findings suggest that tracking mouse populations could provide a valuable early warning of unusually high tick numbers.

How Acorns Can Predict Tick Populations Two Years Ahead

One of the most unexpected discoveries involves something that might seem entirely unrelated to Lyme disease: acorns.

Oak trees periodically produce exceptionally large crops of acorns in synchronized events known as acorn masting. These unusually productive years provide an abundance of food for forest rodents, allowing mouse populations to expand during the following year.

That increase in mice then supports larger numbers of young ticks. As a result, the researchers found that a particularly heavy acorn crop can reliably predict an increase in nymphal ticks two years later.

The relationship reveals a remarkable sequence of events in which the reproductive cycle of forest trees helps determine future tick abundance. It also offers researchers another potential tool for anticipating periods of greater Lyme disease exposure.

Why More Mice Don't Always Mean More Infected Ticks

Although mouse abundance helps predict how many nymphal ticks will appear, the researchers discovered that it does not consistently predict what percentage of those ticks will carry Lyme disease bacteria.

Initially, scientists expected both measures to rise together. Because mice are efficient transmitters of B. burgdorferi, an increase in mice seemed likely to produce not only more ticks but also a greater proportion of infected ticks.

Early observations appeared to support that idea. However, as additional years of data accumulated, the apparent relationship gradually disappeared.

"It kept me up at night," said Ostfeld, "because it meant that my understanding of how the system works was incomplete or wrong."

The explanation appears to involve the other animals that share the forest with mice. Skunks, squirrels, and opossums also serve as hosts for feeding ticks, but most are much less effective at passing the Lyme disease bacterium to them.

When these animals are abundant, more larval ticks may feed on hosts that are unlikely to infect them. This can reduce the percentage of nymphal ticks carrying the bacteria, even when mouse populations are high.

"So the infection prevalence in the nymphs really depends on how all the larval tick meals are distributed across all the hosts," explained Ostfeld. "It would have been convenient if one host predicted that for us, but that has not been the case."

This finding highlights an important distinction between the total number of ticks in an environment and the proportion carrying the pathogen. Predicting the risk of infection requires understanding the broader community of animals that ticks feed on, not simply counting mice.

Extreme Heat and Cold May Not Kill Ticks in the Wild

Weather has also produced some unexpected results.

Earlier laboratory experiments suggested that exposure to intense heat or severe cold could kill blacklegged ticks. Yet observations in natural habitats revealed that ticks are surprisingly capable of surviving these conditions.

Researchers believe ticks protect themselves from temperature extremes by retreating into deeper soil layers or hiding beneath fallen leaves, where conditions are less severe than at the surface.

However, the long-term data also revealed a more complicated relationship with temperature. Years that are warmer overall tend to be followed by fewer nymphal ticks than usual, despite the ability of individual ticks to withstand short periods of extreme heat.

At the same time, warmer conditions generally favor mouse populations, which can lead to more ticks. These opposing effects make it difficult to predict how changing temperatures will ultimately influence Lyme disease risk.

What 35 Years of Research Mean for Lyme Disease Prevention

Taken together, the discoveries show that Lyme disease risk depends on a network of ecological interactions that is complicated but not entirely unpredictable.

Mouse abundance, for example, provides a stronger indicator of future nymphal tick numbers than deer abundance at the study site. Similarly, unusually harsh winters may not reduce tick populations as dramatically as previously expected.

By understanding these patterns, public health officials may be better equipped to anticipate periods of increased tick exposure and develop more effective prevention strategies.

Still, important questions remain, particularly about how climate change will affect ticks, the animals they feed on, and the oak trees that influence rodent populations.

"You would expect that a warming climate, which makes life better for the mice, would eventually make life better for the ticks, but we have not seen that so far," said Ostfeld. "We need to know what to expect from the different manifestations of climate change, which are sometimes warmer and drier, and sometimes warmer and wetter. The time of year when it's very warm also matters to ticks, oaks, and acorn production."

The research site in Dutchess County has undergone significant environmental changes since monitoring began in 1991. Climate shifts, changes to the surrounding landscape, and the arrival of invasive species have all altered the conditions in which the forest's plants and animals live.

Researchers intend to continue following these changes to determine how ticks and their hosts respond over time. Their work could provide increasingly valuable insights into how future environmental changes will influence human exposure to Lyme disease and other infections carried by ticks.

#602. Curiosity Blog, Sols 5056-5062: Charging Back Up!

Mars Science Laboratory Mission Team Members

By Remington Free, Operations Systems Engineer, NASA-JPL

Earth planning date: Friday, Oct. 30, 2026

Today’s plan covered 3 sols, 5060, 5061, and 5062, as we take a few recharging sols before getting back to our normal drilling activities. As you may know if you have been following the blog closely, we are in the middle of a drilling campaign on our target called “Nazca.” We’ve already drilled the hole, characterized the tailings from the drill hole with ChemCam, and have now done several sample dropoffs, both to our SAM and CheMin instruments within the belly of the rover. We still have a number of activities left to do as part of the campaign, including another dropoff to SAM for a different kind of analysis, and drilling cleanup activities. One of those activities is called “RAGE” (Rotate to Agitate for Granule Expulsion, a very fun acronym) where we essentially shake the drill to get any remaining sample out of it. We’ll also make sure to take lots of imaging to continue document the drill hole and tailings.

In any case, those activities are very power-hungry for our rover! So today’s plan purposefully did not have a lot of activities in it so we could get a nice few power positive sols before we get back to drilling. Each of our three sols had a standard morning science block, with an extra exciting brand-new activity on 5061 (more on that shortly!).

Over the three sols, we conducted remote science with ChemCam active and Remote Micro-Imager mosaics on some interesting targets (“Titicaca,” “Pampas,” and “Salta”), some Mastcam imaging of our calibration target and our drill target, several Navcam dust devil studies and Mastcam tau observations, and twilight imaging. Despite this plan being recharging/waiting, we got in lots of exciting science to continue to study the Nazca crystal formations and the nearby layered rocks and bedrock.

On 5061, we also conducted a brand-new engineering activity to test parallelism over one of our downlink communications windows. As the rover gets older and our power output slowly decreases over the length of the mission, we are continuously looking for new ways to execute activities with less power use. One of our strategies is parallelism – having two activities execute at the same time will save power due to the rover being awake for less time. However, we need to vet pairs of activities ahead of time to ensure they are safe to run together. This is a test of one of those pairs – running our CheMin “Get Data” activity at the same time as we are sending data to one of our orbiters. CheMin Get Data is a high CPU activity which transfers science data from the instrument to our flight computers. The test seems to have gone well, but we’ll have to wait to hear back from our engineering team to analyze the results!

Overall, it was an exciting plan full of great remote science as we wait to be able to wrap up our drill campaign. Stay tuned to hear more about how the rest of the Nazca campaign went!

#601. Audioengine Color of the Year Kit Review: Bright Look, Good Sound

The artist Wassily Kandinsky said, “Color is a power that directly influences the soul.” But in the realm of home stereo gear, the color wheel gets minimal airtime, with equipment typically available in scant options: silver-faced classics, wood-grain boxes, all manner of modern reflective piano black, and not a whole lot else.

Not so with Audioengine, which has painted a neat little world with its annual Color of the Year kit: a suite of home audio components, including laptop speakers, a subwoofer, a turntable, and speaker stands. Each year, the brand drops a new edition of the series in a different color; in the launch year of 2024, a dusky matte blue; in 2025, a subtle metallic green; and now in 2026, a lustrous and smooth egg-yolk yellow.

Having a home audio system in such an unusual color palette is, to me, irresistibly joyful; whenever I look at these stereo components, I can’t help but smile. And the tech holds up, too. After months of testing the suite, I was colored impressed. Straight out of the box, it's a great option for music lovers who value the look and sound of their home stereo setup.

The anchoring component of the Color of the Year suite are the A2+ Gen 3 Wireless Speakers. At 6 inches tall by 4 inches wide, they’re sized like laptop speakers—and get high marks for that purpose as WIRED’s top pick for computer speakers—but that’s really just the start of what they can do. Alone, the speakers pack enough power for a small home theater. They're also easy to integrate into a bigger system, thanks to their included stereo mini-jack inputs, USB inputs, and both stereo RCA inputs and outputs. To make the sound quality more targeted, place the A2+ speakers atop the included DS1M Desktop Stands, and angle them 15 degrees up toward your ears.

This year, Audioengine expanded its Color of the Year suite to include a color-matched Bluetooth turntable in collaboration with Crosley. Called the C6, it's a manual two-speed machine with a nice, heavy steel platter and medium-density fiberboard (MDF) plinth. It's Bluetooth-enabled and features a built-in preamp that lets you directly connect your A2+ speakers—wired or wireless—without a need for additional components.

My favorite part of the Color of the Year suite is the S6 Powered Subwoofer. At 10 inches by 8.7 inches, the compact, front-firing form factor lets you snuggle it into various spaces that wouldn't work for a larger, bottom-firing subwoofer. This is a class D sub with a frequency response down to 33 Hz and an adjustable crossover down to 40 Hz that helps your system sound complete, lifting the bass and rounding out the listening experience.

The ease of setup is one of the major draws for the Audioengine suite. Plug in the A2+ speakers, link them together using the provided stereo cables, and turn them on. You can connect the speakers via Bluetooth, or plug in other components via the stereo inputs. It’s as basic as home hi-fi gets.

Linking the Crosley C6 turntable using Bluetooth is similarly seamless. The A2+s have an intuitive Bluetooth pairing mode that starts up when you turn on the speakers, indicated by a flashing Pair button on the back. From there, turn on the turntable, make sure it’s in the “line” position (by using the turntable’s built-in preamp), and the C6 instantly searches for the A2+s. Once the two units connect, the flashing button on the back of the speakers will turn blue, as will the Bluetooth indicator on the turntable.

Connecting the subwoofer was likewise a cinch: Connect an RCA cable to the back of the A2+s in the RCA output, then run that to the audio input on the back of the S6 subwoofer. Once you connect, you’ll want to tweak the sound and presence of the subwoofer, and once you’re happy with the dial-in, the subwoofers will go up and down in volume, in lockstep with the A2+ speakers.

Each Audioengine product also came with a QR code that launched a step-by-step setup process, which I found especially helpful for dialing in the sound for the woofer. (The Audioengine YouTube channel is also a wealth of information.)

After several colorful, music-filled months of testing the Audioengine kit, I found the system to be versatile, though not without its limitations or opportunities for upgrades and tweaks. Right out of the box, the speakers, subwoofer, and turntable looked fantastic and sounded pretty nice. The A2+ speakers were shockingly nuanced and detailed, considering their small size, with Primus and Frank Zappa records showcasing slashing guitars and Paul Chambers and Bill Evans jazz albums providing a sophisticated soundstage. These speakers also worked great for home gaming and laptop use. The subwoofer did wonders when connected to the A2+ for elevating the home-theater experience. The growling lion and car-chasing monkeys in 1995's Jumanji were vivid and lifelike.

Being able to transition from home stereo to computer to home theater is versatile and fun. It means there’s always a way to incorporate these products into your home.

If there’s a weak link in the Color of the Year kit, it’s the turntable—but even that is pretty strong. The aluminum tonearm leaves something to be desired if you’re accustomed to higher-end units, but the C6 worked well for me and will suit the needs of most casual listeners. There are several similarly priced Bluetooth-enabled turntables on the market, so out of curiosity I swapped in a Sony PS-LX3BT and noticed no significant difference in terms of sound. What those other turntables lack is the aesthetic unity and the satisfaction of having your entire system kitted out in a unique colorway.

Mostly, I just love how this canary-yellow gear looks together. It offers equal parts interior-design pleasure and home audio performance. The price is incredibly competitive across the entry-level home stereo space, and the ease of use and setup feels intuitive and user-friendly for novices. If color influences the soul, then the union of color and music stands to be a source of deep satisfaction—and I’ve found it here.

(If you don't want the entire suite, you can buy the components à la carte without a penalty—there's no price break for the bundled suite.)

#599. Mysterious gas leaks suggest Mars may still be volcanically alive

Mars may not be as geologically inactive as it appears. New evidence from mysterious gas emissions near an enormous Martian volcano suggests that heat and volcanic processes could still be present deep beneath the planet's surface.

Researchers have identified unusual concentrations of hydrogen chloride gas above fractures in the Alba Mons volcanic region. The findings suggest that gases from the planet's interior may be escaping through cracks in the surface, potentially revealing volcanic activity that has remained hidden until now.

An international team led by Alexander Trokhimovskiy believes the gas could originate from hot magma underground. If confirmed, this would indicate that Mars still has dormant volcanic systems capable of releasing gases to the surface.

The European Space Agency's ExoMars Trace Gas Orbiter (TGO) first detected hydrogen chloride in the Martian atmosphere in findings reported in 2021. On Earth, hydrogen chloride is frequently released through openings connected to magma, raising the possibility that similar processes could be occurring on Mars.

"Our findings suggest that Mars has retained enough internal heat to support volcanic activity until recently, and possibly still does," says Daniel Mège, Professor at the Space Research Centre of the Polish Academy of Sciences and co-lead author of the study published in Science Advances.

Mysterious Gas Emissions Defy Mars' Seasonal Pattern

The researchers noticed something unusual during early summer in the Martian northern hemisphere. Hydrogen chloride concentrations were unexpectedly elevated in a particular region, even though the gas normally becomes scarce at that time of year.

Hydrogen chloride levels on Mars generally follow a seasonal cycle. They typically peak during northern winter before declining to very low levels in summer. Scientists believe much of this behavior is associated with the planet's dust and water cycles.

However, the observations revealed a striking exception. Elevated hydrogen chloride repeatedly appeared in early summer over volcanic fractures on the slopes of Alba Mons. The unusual timing and consistent location suggested that something beyond ordinary atmospheric chemistry might be responsible.

Mars is home to the largest volcanoes in the Solar System, but scientists still do not know whether volcanic processes remain active beneath their surfaces.

Images taken from orbit indicate that some Martian volcanoes experienced eruptions within the past few million years. Instruments on the surface have also recorded marsquakes, demonstrating that the planet continues to experience seismic activity.

Together, these observations suggest that Mars may retain some of its geological energy. However, scientists have never directly observed a new volcanic eruption or fresh lava flow on the planet, leaving the question of present-day volcanism unresolved.

Fresh Cracks Reveal Clues to Hidden Volcanic Heat

To investigate the unusual gas concentrations, the research team combined atmospheric measurements with detailed observations of the Martian surface.

The Atmospheric Chemistry Suite aboard TGO uses infrared spectrometers to identify gases in the atmosphere. These instruments detected hydrogen chloride concentrated in a relatively small area shortly after the disappearance of winter frost.

Researchers then examined the same region using the orbiter's CaSSIS camera. The images revealed narrow fractures that appeared relatively fresh, with some extending hundreds of meters across the surface. Unusual changes in surface color were also visible around the cracks.

"The Trace Gas Orbiter was able to find the smoking gun. It was like looking for a needle in a haystack. It's truly amazing that it spotted a handful of molecules from a perhaps intermittent source, whilst moving continuously along its orbit to monitor the whole Martian atmosphere," explains Daniel.

One possible explanation is that gases originating from magma deep underground travel upward through these fractures. On Earth, comparable volcanic openings are known as fumaroles.

The researchers propose that extremely cold Martian winters, when temperatures can fall to -120°C, allow ice to seal the fractures and temporarily prevent gases from escaping. As temperatures rise in early summer, the ice sublimates, changing directly into vapor and potentially opening pathways for underground gases to reach the atmosphere.

The discoloration observed around the fractures could also be significant. It may result from minerals or other substances deposited by escaping gases, similar to the deposits commonly found around fumaroles (volcanic gas vents) on Earth.

Although the connection between the gas emissions and volcanic fractures appeared promising, the researchers needed to rule out other explanations.

"Looking at the spatial distribution of the seeping, it became instantly obvious to me that there was something volcanic behind it, but it had to be demonstrated," concedes Daniel.

The team carefully evaluated the chemical reactions that could produce hydrogen chloride in the Martian atmosphere, including processes unrelated to volcanism.

"A huge part of the work consisted of examining all the possible chemical processes going on in the Martian atmosphere. We needed to be rigorous, critical and creative to explain the presence of hydrogen chloride. The data were complex, but also excellent brain food," adds Daniel.

After examining the atmospheric and geological evidence together, the researchers proposed that the gas could be escaping from a dormant magma chamber beneath Alba Mons. Such a chamber would form part of an underground volcanic system that could continue releasing gases even without producing visible eruptions.

Hidden Volcanic Activity Could Help Mars Support Life

The implications may extend beyond understanding the planet's geological history. Volcanic gases could also provide clues about how Mars evolved and whether it ever supported environments suitable for life.

In the distant past, gases released from magma, including hydrogen chloride, may have helped create a denser atmosphere and warmer conditions. Those conditions could have allowed liquid water to remain across large portions of the Martian surface.

Volcanic activity might also create habitable environments underground. If rising magma or lava comes into contact with groundwater, the interaction can produce warm hydrothermal systems. Such environments could provide both chemical energy and protection from the harsh conditions on the surface.

"Looking for Martian life in the seeping fissures of the Alba Mons region would make a lot of sense. If hydrothermal systems are active, they could contain the ingredients and energy needed for microorganisms to develop, and shield microorganisms from the hostile and changing environment above the surface," ponders Daniel.

Although the findings do not establish that Mars is currently erupting or that life exists beneath its surface, they offer an intriguing new reason to investigate the volcanic fractures around Alba Mons.

"These findings may prove to be TGO's greatest success yet," highlights Colin Wilson, ESA's project scientist for the ExoMars Trace Gas Orbiter.

Materials provided by European Space Agency. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#598. Autumn Mornings Bring Valley Fog

Low clouds trace the branching valleys of the Appalachian Plateau and the Cumberland River, then burn off by afternoon.

On a cool November morning, low clouds traced the branching valleys of the Appalachian Plateau and the Cumberland River. By afternoon, they had faded, leaving clear skies over the autumn landscape.

The change is visible in this pair of images from November 8, 2026. The MODIS (Moderate Resolution Imaging Spectroradiometer) on NASA's Terra satellite acquired the left image that morning. In this natural-color view, low clouds appear white as they fill many of the valleys. Autumn vegetation appears in shades of brown and green.

The VIIRS (Visible Infrared Imaging Radiometer Suite) on NOAA-21 acquired the right image that afternoon, after the clouds had dissipated and exposed the rivers and lakes beneath them. This false-color view makes it easier to distinguish water (dark blue) from vegetation (bright green).

The clouds appear to be fog, a type of cloud that forms near the ground. Foggy mornings in the region are common this time of year. As nights lengthen, the ground has more time to radiate away the day's heat, and the air just above it cools to the dew point—the temperature at which air becomes saturated and water vapor begins condensing into the tiny droplets that make up fog.

Because cold air is denser than warm air, it flows downslope and pools in valleys, so fog tends to form there first. Notice the fog filling the branching valleys across part of the Appalachian Plateau in eastern Kentucky and southwestern West Virginia. Rivers and streams that run along many of the valley floors add to the effect by supplying extra moisture. The same pattern is visible along the Cumberland River and its reservoirs in north-central Tennessee and south-central Kentucky.

According to the National Weather Service, temperatures in many places on the morning of November 8 dipped into the 30s Fahrenheit before giving way to a mild, sunny afternoon. Forecasters reported locally dense valley fog that morning. Dense fog had also blanketed a wider part of the region earlier in the week, prompting advisories for hazardous travel conditions.

NASA Earth Observatory images by Lauren Dauphin, using MODIS data from NASA EOSDIS LANCE and GIBS/Worldview, and VIIRS data from NASA EOSDIS LANCE, GIBS/Worldview and the Joint Polar Satellite System (JPSS). Story by Kathryn Hansen.

#596. Moon phase today: What the Moon will look like on November 9, 2026

There's barely any of the Moon visible tonight as it approaches the New Moon. With less than a sliver illuminated, there is nothing for us to see in the way of lunar features.

As of Monday, Nov. 9, the NASA's Daily Moon Guide tracker tells us the Moon is in the New Moon phase, with 0% of its surface visible.

The next Full Moon will take place on November 24.

It takes the Moon around 29.5 days to pass through a complete lunar cycle. As it travels around Earth, the angle at which sunlight reaches its surface changes what we can see from here, producing the different phases, from the New Moon through to the Full Moon and back again. The cycle then starts over, as explained by NASA.

New Moon - The Moon is between Earth and the sun, so the side we see is dark (in other words, it's invisible to the eye).

Waxing Crescent - A small sliver of light appears on the right side (Northern Hemisphere).

First Quarter - Half of the Moon is lit on the right side. It looks like a half-Moon.

Waxing Gibbous - More than half is lit up, but it's not quite full yet.

Full Moon - The whole face of the Moon is illuminated and fully visible.

Waning Gibbous - The Moon starts losing light on the right side. (Northern Hemisphere)

Third Quarter (or Last Quarter) - Another half-Moon, but now the left side is lit.

Waning Crescent - A thin sliver of light remains on the left side before going dark again.

You can find bylines in publications including Fit&Well, Metro, and Coach magazine, usually covering deals on everything from earbuds to TVs, or guides on how to beat your half marathon time.

Lois also holds a Master's degree in Digital Journalism from Strathclyde University and obtained a Master of Arts in English Literature at the University of Aberdeen.

#595. Ancient Siberian tomb reveals surprising secrets of cannabis use 2,800 years ago

A remarkable discovery in a nearly 2,800-year-old Siberian tomb has provided the oldest directly dated archaeological evidence that people used cannabis for its intoxicating effects. By examining ancient cannabis seeds and chemical traces preserved in human hair, researchers have uncovered new clues about the role of the plant in prehistoric burial rituals.

Cannabis is among the most widely used psychoactive plants in the modern world, but tracing its use thousands of years into the past presents a major archaeological challenge. Flowers, leaves, and resins tend to decompose over time, leaving little physical evidence behind. Ancient communities also relied on cannabis for practical purposes, including producing fiber and oil, making it difficult to distinguish these uses from medicinal or recreational consumption.

An international team led by Dr. Gino Caspari, affiliated with the Institute of Archaeological Sciences at the University of Bern and the Max Planck Institute of Geoanthropology, investigated an elaborate tomb in the Republic of Tyva in southern Siberia. The burial site belonged to prominent members of the Scythians, a culture known for its skilled horseback riders who inhabited the vast Eurasian steppe.

Ancient Siberian Tomb Contains 1,000 Cannabis Seeds

During the investigation, researchers recovered approximately 1,000 cannabis seeds from the tomb. They used radiocarbon dating to determine the age of six individual seeds. Combined with other dating evidence, the results indicate that the seeds were deposited in the burial site during the early eighth century B.C.

"The seeds show that cannabis played an important role in the rituals surrounding the burial," says Caspari, an archaeologist and the study's lead author.

The researchers also analyzed human hair preserved in the tomb's central burial chamber. Chemical evidence in several samples pointed to cannabis consumption, providing a crucial connection between the plant remains and the people buried at the site.

"This study is particularly insightful because it combines the analysis of plant remains with molecular traces in the human body," says Caspari.

The findings were published in the journal Science.

Chemical Traces in Ancient Hair Reveal Cannabis Use

Although the cannabis seeds were remarkably well preserved, their presence alone could not establish whether the plant was used for its mind-altering effects. The seeds probably came from wild cannabis plants, and researchers could not determine whether people had deliberately cultivated them for their psychoactive properties.

The strongest evidence came from the preserved human hair. Using high-resolution mass spectrometry and other analytical techniques, the team identified clear chemical signals consistent with cannabinol (CBN) in several samples.

CBN can form when tetrahydrocannabinol (THC), the primary compound responsible for cannabis intoxication, undergoes oxidation. The researchers also detected weaker signals that matched the mass of THC, further supporting the possibility that the individuals had consumed cannabis.

While these chemical findings indicate cannabis use, they cannot reveal how much was consumed or exactly how the compounds entered the body. The individuals may have inhaled cannabis smoke or consumed the plant in another way, but the available evidence cannot establish a particular method.

Other archaeological discoveries from later periods in Central Asia provide clearer evidence of cannabis inhalation. At those sites, researchers have identified cannabinoid residues on heated stones and inside vessels, pointing to the use of heat to release compounds that could be inhaled.

"The molecular traces in the hair and the cannabis seeds come from the same grave and suggest that cannabis was used for its intoxicating properties," says Caspari, who conducts research at the Institute of Archaeological Sciences at the University of Bern and the Max Planck Institute for Geoanthropology in Jena (Germany).

Cannabis, Horse Sacrifices, and Ancient Burial Rituals

The cannabis evidence was discovered within an elaborate ceremonial burial site, suggesting that the plant may have been connected to important Scythian funerary traditions.

"In this princely tomb, the burials were accompanied by extensive horse sacrifices and, most likely, human sacrifices as well," says Gino Caspari.

Earlier research at the site identified the remains of 18 horses that had been deliberately positioned on the burial mound. Continued excavations have since uncovered evidence of several dozen more sacrificed horses, along with scattered human bones.

These discoveries reveal the extraordinary scale of the burial ceremonies and provide important context for understanding why cannabis may have been included among the materials deposited in the tomb.

Oldest Directly Dated Evidence of Cannabis Intoxication

Taken together, the seeds and chemical traces in human hair represent the oldest directly dated archaeological evidence currently known for cannabis use associated with intoxication.

However, several questions remain unanswered. The researchers cannot determine how much THC the ancient cannabis contained, whether the plants were particularly potent, or whether Scythian communities had begun deliberately selecting or cultivating cannabis varieties with higher concentrations of psychoactive compounds.

To investigate these questions, the team is now studying genetic material recovered from the ancient plant remains. The unusually good preservation of the cannabis DNA could provide valuable insights into how humans domesticated the plant and how it spread across different regions over thousands of years.

"We have already begun sequencing some of the plant samples," says Gino Caspari. "DNA preservation is excellent in this case. This will help us learn more about the history of the domestication and spread of this crop soon."

Materials provided by University of Bern. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#594. Authors Can Defend Against AI Allegations With ‘Paper Trail’ Tools. Is It Enough?

Andrea Bartz is a prolific novelist with an impressive résumé. Since 2019, she has published five thrillers, become a New York Times bestselling author, had her work optioned by the likes of Netflix and Hulu, and picked up an endorsement from Reese Witherspoon’s influential book club.

She also has a legal claim to fame: Bartz was the lead plaintiff in a class-action suit against Anthropic that alleged the company had downloaded millions of copyrighted books from pirate sites to use as training data for its models. Anthropic last year agreed to pay $1.5 billion in the largest copyright settlement in US history.

But while Bartz and many other writers were on guard against this plunder of their work, another AI threat was brewing. What happens when authors are accused of relying on artificial intelligence themselves?

This past March, the publishing industry found out: Following widespread speculation on social media that a horror novel titled Shy Girl was largely AI-generated, and a Times article about those claims, Hachette Book Group canceled an upcoming US publication run for the novel and discontinued it in the UK, where it had already been released. According to Pangram, a robust but by no means infallible AI detection tool, some 78 percent of the text was AI-generated.

Mia Ballard, the author of Shy Girl, has denied allegations that she used AI to write the novel, claiming that an editor was responsible for AI-assisted changes to a previously self-published version of the book. She has also said that she is pursuing legal action. Since that scandal broke, several more fiction authors have run afoul of AI suspicions, one after winning a short story prize, another after publishers bid into the millions for his crime novel. Both have denied these allegations as well.

But Bartz notes that once the accusations start, the damage is done. As the Shy Girl scandal erupted, she says, “I kind of immediately felt like this was going to lead to a potential witch hunt.” The unavoidable emergence of AI in publishing, she explains, will “have the unintended effect of leading anyone to be able to accuse anyone of using AI,” and at the moment, authors have little recourse in defending themselves against these charges. “There’s no agreed upon standard,” Bartz says, in proving human authorship.

The developers of a host of new software tools hope to change that. Platforms including Workings, Blindproof, Wintertext, Human Ink, and ObraVera promise the means to track and document an author’s writing process, in some cases encrypting and fingerprinting the written work or typing rhythms with unique codes, in order to produce a record of authorship (whether entirely human or AI-assisted). The idea is not just to create a paper trail that writers can point to if someone questions whether they used an LLM but to demonstrate provenance when, for example, text is scraped into AI training sets without attribution or other identifying metadata.

Mary Rasenberger, CEO of the Authors Guild, a professional organization focused on free expression and copyright protection, says that the guild can’t recommend any one of these tools over another but wants writers to know they exist as something of a security net. (Many of the companies have approached the Authors Guild to pitch their services, according to Umair Kazi, the guild’s director of policy and advocacy.)

At the same time, Rasenberger tells WIRED, it’s “insulting” and a “burden” to have to jump through this hoop, and “we don't feel it should be required” for authors to preserve receipts.

“If what you're selling are your words and your voice and your imagination, your creativity, using AI doesn't make sense,” Rasenberger says, noting that authors’ pride in their own work means AI slop is far less common in publishing than one might think. “I've talked to editors, particularly editors of literary nonfiction and fiction,” Rasenberger says, “and some of them will say, ‘I never see AI-generated submissions, I'd know it if I did.’”

Rasenberger worries that authors are up against a climate of distrust—they should “refrain from accusations of other authors because it just does not support a healthy literary community,” she says—and overconfidence in AI detection tools, which can be fooled and have been known to produce false positives. While Pangram claims an “industry-leading” false positive rate of 0.01 percent when used on creative writing, Rasenberger said the Authors Guild has “heard of lots of instances where people have put their own human-authored work in Pangram and gotten a result back saying it was AI-generated.” There are a variety of factors, she adds, that can “make an AI detector think something's AI-generated when it's not.”

By the same token, a process-tracking app isn’t foolproof; the AI arms race means there’s an ongoing cycle of vulnerabilities and fixes on both sides.

“We offer a product that [tracks writing] in Google Docs, and many users find it helpful,” says Max Spero, cofounder and CEO of Pangram. “With that said, ‘paper trail’ tools are not a perfect catch-all. For example, someone could hand-type AI text, or use an auto-typer.” Spero points out that the messiness of writing itself creates further problems. “The average writer doesn't just sit down and type out a book,” he says. “They work with multiple files, send drafts back and forth, use voice notes while on the go. Not all of this gets captured in writing process tools.”

The tools, meanwhile, may be more geared toward copyright concerns than confirmation of human or AI authorship. Brady Ridgway, technical founder of ObraVera—which can cryptographically “fingerprint” patterns in writing as well as audio voice recordings—says his platform is designed to trace reuse of material you have stored in their database as a registered human user. With a time stamp and a crypto hash, he explains, if the content “crops up later somewhere it's not supposed to be, or somebody else claims it as theirs, we can search the database and say, ‘These words were created at this time, by this person.’”

That may demonstrate provenance and ownership, Ridgway says, yet it’s up to the registered individual to declare whether they used AI or not in the creation of the text. He refers to the kerfuffle in May over the writer who was accused of winning a Commonwealth Short Story Prize with an AI-generated submission. “Everybody seems to agree that he's the person who submitted the short story,” Ridgway says. “That's all that the software would do. It would say that somebody on this day made a declaration that it was human only or not AI-assisted.” It can’t confirm whether that’s a true statement or not.

Some argue that trying to institutionalize vetting systems for human authorship is simply impractical—and may soon be a moot point anyway. Jane Friedman, an author and publishing business expert, recently argued in her industry newsletter The Bottom Line, that the era of AI scandals is likely to be short-lived, given the improvements of Pangram and similar detectors. Moreover, she wrote, authors have now seen what may happen if they attempt to pass off AI-generated writing as their own.

Friedman tells WIRED that agents and publishers are unlikely to implement any method of verifying human authorship that “costs extra money or extra time,” not least because it would be impossible to get them to commit to a shared standard. She predicts that “it will be like herding cats to get agreement on this, because publishing is slow to adopt new technologies, especially new technologies developed by industry outsiders.”

Even if diverse publishers with highly variable needs and resources were to coalesce around some theoretical form of authentication, hurdles remain. Friedman alludes to the logistical problems of integrating this benchmark into existing production models, not to mention the lingering problem of “faked or manipulated” certification. “If any of this costs extra money or extra time, forget it,” she concludes. And while authors may be willing to pay for peace of mind, Friedman says, they won’t be able to trust that agents and publishers will accept proof of authorship from any given app.

In the absence of a clear solution, writers are left to fend for themselves. Bartz says that she’s alarmed by how zealous readers are responding to the prospect of being deceived by AI. “I have heard of people getting review-bombed on Goodreads because some stranger felt that the book sounded like AI to them, and everyone else piled on,” she says. “I've seen readers saying, ‘I'm not gonna read anything published after 2022, because then I can be sure that it doesn't have generative AI in it.’ That's a great way to tank an entire industry all at once.”

It so happens that Bartz’s forthcoming 2027 book, Delete Your Account, tackles generative artificial intelligence head-on. Her protagonist is a blocked writer who opts for an unfortunate shortcut, prompting an AI chatbot to imitate her favorite thriller—only to find herself trapped in that original story, facing certain death.

Bartz wonders if the very choice to satirize AI has put her in a precarious spot. “I've written portions that are very much meant to sound like LLMs,” she says. “Transcripts I made up, and annoying, AI-sounding marketing materials from this made-up AI company. And I don't know—if any of that comes back as AI-written, what will I do?”

#593. APOD: 2026 November 10 – Saturn Behind the Moon

Discover the cosmos! Each day a different image or photograph of our fascinating universe is featured, along with a brief explanation written by a professional astronomer.

Explanation: Sometimes Saturn disappears. It doesn't really go away, of course -- it just disappears from our view when Earth's Moon moves in front. Such a Saturnian eclipse, more formally called an occultation, was visible in early 2025 along a small swath of Earth -- from Greenland through central Europe and into northern Africa. The featured image is a digital fusion of three images, the first of just Saturn before it disappeared, and then two more of the Moon with different exposure lengths. The images were taken from Sardinia, Italy, after sunset. The bright dot near Saturn is not a moon of Saturn, but rather the star 85 Aquarii, which is further behind Saturn than Saturn is behind the Moon. The next time the Moon moves in front of Saturn, as visible from some place on Earth, will be in 2031. Tomorrow's picture: small but mighty

#592. Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang admits affair with alleged Chinese spy

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has admitted to cheating on his wife with a suspected Chinese spy. The company's board announced that it is launching an independent investigation into the matter, with Huang's cooperation.

"I owe my family and all of you an apology," Huang wrote in an unexpected press release on Sunday. "I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry."

Huang has been married to his wife since 1985. They have two children together.

Allegations of Huang's affair arose in a New York Times report last month. According to the report, U.S. spy agencies determined that a Chinese intelligence asset had been sharing information with the Chinese government. Specifically, the New York Times identified the alleged agent as Alice Huang (no relation), an executive at Singapore-based data centre company Megaspeed who had reportedly developed a close relationship with Nvidia's CEO.

In a statement to the New York Times, a spokesperson for the Chinese embassy in Washington, D.C. said that the U.S. was conducting an operation of "groundless smears and slanders" against China.

Megaspeed gained the U.S. government's attention last year due to suspicion that it was smuggling Nvidia chips to China. If true, this would be in circumvention of U.S. restrictions, which have blocked the export of many Nvidia chips to China due to national security concerns. The Singaporean government also opened an investigation into Megaspeed for potentially violating local laws.

Megaspeed denied these allegations at the time, stating that it had already been investigated and cleared by multiple institutions, including both the U.S. Commerce Department and Nvidia.

"We operate fully within the bounds of all applicable export control regulations and maintain the highest standards of legal and ethical conduct," read a statement from Megaspeed from last December.

It's unclear whether Ms. Huang is alleged to have been operating independently or as an agent of Megaspeed. Huang previously denied an inappropriate relationship with Ms. Huang in an interview with Bloomberg late last month, and claimed she was no longer with Megaspeed.

"Our relationship, our interactions, have been related to Megaspeed, and it was all completely normal and not unusual," said Huang. #591. A nuclear blast could save Earth from an asteroid, but there's a catch

A nuclear explosion could potentially protect Earth from an incoming asteroid by releasing powerful X-rays that heat its surface and push it onto a safer trajectory. However, new research suggests that the success of such a mission could depend heavily on which side of the asteroid receives the blast.

Unlike the smooth, round objects often used in computer simulations, real asteroids have uneven surfaces and irregular shapes. Those differences can dramatically influence how an asteroid moves when exposed to intense radiation.

A new study published in Icarus found that changing the direction of an X-ray burst could produce vastly different results, even when targeting the same asteroid. In one simulation, the asteroid's speed changed by 3.9 millimeters per second when irradiated from one direction, compared with only 0.8 millimeters per second from another. That represents a nearly fivefold difference in deflection velocity, the change in the asteroid's forward speed caused by the burst.

"We expected aim to matter," said Nathan Moore, a physicist at Sandia National Laboratories who led the research. "What surprised us was how much."

The effects on an asteroid's rotation could be even more dramatic. For another simulated asteroid, the change in how quickly it spun varied by a factor of 17 depending on the direction of the X-rays.

These findings suggest that understanding an asteroid's shape and carefully selecting where to direct a nuclear blast could be essential to protecting Earth from a future impact.

The research received funding through Sandia's Laboratory Directed Research and Development program.

A New Way to Simulate Asteroid Deflection

To investigate how different asteroid shapes respond to nuclear blasts, Moore and his colleagues developed what they describe as the most accurate asteroid deflection model yet.

Earlier computer models faced a major limitation. Researchers typically had to choose between representing an asteroid's complex shape and performing highly detailed calculations of the physical forces involved. Combining both approaches demanded more computing power than was practical.

The new system overcomes that obstacle, allowing scientists to study realistic asteroid shapes while maintaining the precision needed to calculate their response to an X-ray burst. The result is the most detailed simulation of asteroid deflection produced so far.

"Our new algorithm is over 1,600 times faster, which was critical to this breakthrough," Moore said. "With the old models, computers just couldn't handle it."

Moore's interest in improving these calculations grew out of an earlier experiment using Sandia's Z machine. That experiment provided the first demonstration that X-rays could deflect a small piece of asteroid material.

The next challenge was determining how the same physical process might affect an entire asteroid.

"I started wondering how to scale up the calculation to more accurately predict deflection of a full-scale asteroid."

A major problem was the widespread practice of treating asteroids as spheres. Although this simplifies the mathematics, it overlooks the irregular shapes of real asteroids, which can have ridges, depressions, and uneven surfaces.

"You can make estimates that way, but asteroids come in all kinds of odd, lumpy shapes," he said. "That's when I latched onto some work one of my colleagues was doing related to outgassing of objects in space. I think we had an aha moment when we realized we could do this. No more spheres. We would be able to model deflection for any asteroid."

The solution involved bringing together two sophisticated simulation systems already used at Sandia for very different types of physical problems.

"The ALEGRA code is normally used to model fast, high-energy experiments, like magnetically imploding wires on the Z machine, while SPARTA, a direct simulation Monte Carlo code, is usually used to model comparatively weak, slowly evolving forces, like the drag on a satellite from the solar wind," Moore explained.

"The key innovation here was figuring out how to combine the two simulation approaches."

Predicting What Happens After a Nuclear Blast

The new computer model allows researchers to reconstruct an asteroid using detailed 3D images captured during spacecraft flybys. They can then simulate a nuclear X-ray burst while adjusting the explosion's strength, its distance from the asteroid, and the direction from which the radiation arrives.

The software calculates how much material would vaporize across different parts of the asteroid's surface and displays the results as a heat map. Using that information, it estimates the resulting push, changes in the asteroid's rotation, and the extent of surface damage.

These calculations are important because a successful planetary defense mission would ideally change an asteroid's trajectory without breaking it into multiple dangerous fragments. Redirecting one large object could be preferable to creating several pieces that might still threaten Earth.

However, the simulations cannot yet predict every possible consequence of a nuclear blast.

"Right now, our computer model only considers breakup of rocks on the surface. We can't predict breakup of the entire asteroid body accurately yet," Moore said.

Developing that capability will require additional research, investment, and more advanced computational tools. An intense X-ray burst can trigger numerous physical effects, some of which influence one another. Accounting for all those interactions makes it exceptionally difficult to predict exactly how an asteroid would behave during a real deflection attempt.

Choosing the Right Blast Could Save Earth

Despite these remaining challenges, the findings offer valuable guidance for future planetary defense efforts. The research shows that the direction of a nuclear blast is not simply a minor detail. It can substantially change how effectively an asteroid is redirected.

"Our study shows how important it is to aim carefully," Moore said. "Pick the right side and you win. Guess wrong and it could be a bad day."

The new simulation tools could eventually help mission planners identify the most effective way to deflect a threatening asteroid. In principle, researchers could enter detailed information about its shape, then calculate the ideal explosive strength, distance, and direction needed to move it away from Earth.

Turning those calculations into a real mission would still be extremely difficult.

For one thing, the software requires reasonably accurate information about the asteroid's shape from every direction. If an asteroid were discovered on a collision course with Earth, there might not be enough time to gather the necessary observations.

Another challenge would be delivering and positioning a nuclear device at precisely the right location relative to an asteroid traveling through space. Even if scientists could identify the ideal blast configuration, executing it would require extraordinary precision.

Nevertheless, the improved simulations could eliminate much of the uncertainty surrounding one possible method of asteroid defense. By better understanding how nuclear X-rays interact with the complex surfaces of real asteroids, scientists are moving closer to predicting which strategies would work best.

For Moore, that progress is an encouraging step toward ensuring humanity has a better chance of preventing a catastrophic asteroid impact.

#589. An ancient ocean full of giant predators reveals a surprising twist in evolution

An extraordinary collection of fossils in central Colombia is revealing a surprising chapter in Earth's evolutionary history. Researchers have uncovered evidence of a time when ancient animal groups that were approaching extinction lived alongside newer groups that would eventually flourish. The discovery suggests that major evolutionary transitions can occur without the widespread destruction associated with mass extinctions.

Scientists from McGill University and the Centro de Investigaciones Paleontológicas (CIP) identified this unusual pattern while studying fossils from Colombia's Paja Formation. The ancient marine ecosystem preserves an exceptional mixture of surviving Jurassic lineages and emerging Cretaceous groups, offering a rare glimpse into a period of profound biological change.

Drawing on these findings, McGill biology professor Hans Larsson and PhD graduate Dirley Cortés have proposed a new concept called a "Biotic Inflection." The term describes an evolutionary turning point when older groups nearing extinction coexist with newer lineages that are beginning to expand and diversify.

A Perfect Storm for Ancient Ocean Biodiversity

The researchers believe an unusual combination of environmental conditions helped make this remarkable coexistence possible. Warm greenhouse temperatures, elevated sea levels, and coastal waters rich in nutrients may have supported an unusually diverse range of marine life.

Another important factor was the emergence of new ocean connections between the eastern Pacific and the early Atlantic through the region that is now Colombia. Together, these conditions likely created opportunities for different groups of animals to thrive within the same ecosystem. Many of these environmental influences continue to shape biodiversity around the world today.

"The Paja Formation records an extraordinary process in the history of life, when a 'perfect storm' of environmental factors we know to promote ecological complexity coincided with ancient lineages that were about to disappear. At the same time, new groups were beginning to flourish," Larsson said.

A Lost World of Giant Marine Reptiles and Predators

The evidence comes from the Early Cretaceous Paja Formation near Villa de Leyva, Colombia. This fossil-rich region has preserved an astonishing variety of prehistoric marine animals, providing scientists with an unusually detailed picture of an ancient ocean ecosystem. Most of the fossils collected from the formation are housed at the CIP.

"This geological formation has produced an exceptionally rich fossil record that includes giant marine reptiles like long-necked plesiosaurs and marine crocodile relatives, as well as turtles, sharks, crustaceans and others," Cortés explained. "Together, these animals formed the Paja Biota, one of the most ecologically complex marine communities found to date."

In a previous study, the researchers reconstructed the feeding relationships among animals in the Paja Biota. Their analysis revealed an exceptionally intricate food web, with an unusually large variety of apex predators occupying the highest levels of the ecosystem.

This remarkable complexity makes the Paja Formation especially valuable for investigating how ecosystems change as older evolutionary groups decline and new ones become established.

Could This Discovery Change How Scientists Understand Evolution?

The proposed Biotic Inflection offers a potentially important new way to understand major changes in Earth's biodiversity. Rather than being driven by a mass extinction, some evolutionary transitions may involve periods of extraordinary diversity, when declining lineages and emerging groups coexist.

However, the researchers emphasize that more evidence is needed to establish how common this pattern may have been. Additional fossil discoveries, more accurate estimates of fossil ages, and comparisons with geological formations in other parts of the world will help determine whether similar transitions occurred elsewhere in Earth's history.

"I think we've discovered a new model of global biodiversity change. Now we'll need to see just how widespread this could be in Earth's history and how we can quantify its impacts," Larsson said.

The research received funding from the Natural Sciences and Engineering Research Council of Canada, the Smithsonian Tropical Research Institute, the Anders Foundation, the 1923 Fund, Gregory D. and Jennifer Walston Johnson, the Fonds de recherche Nature et technologies Québec, and the McGill Biology Department Writing-Year Award 2024-2025.

#588. Scientists May Have Discovered a Massive Starless Galaxy

Some galaxies appear to contain all the ingredients needed to make stars, yet have failed to produce almost any.

Scientists have puzzled over why for years. Recent findings suggest that these “dark galaxies”—that is, galaxies that contain gas and dark matter but few or no stars—may be lurking throughout the cosmos. Finding them could help astronomers understand why some galaxies form stars while others remain dark, and test the best models of galaxy formation.

Researchers’ most successful model of the cosmos, known as Lambda-CDM, assumes the existence of two invisible ingredients: dark energy, the mysterious property thought to be driving the accelerating expansion of the universe, as well as cold dark matter, a hypothetical substance that physicists think makes up about 85 percent of the universe’s matter.

Clumps of dark matter, called haloes, provide the gravitational scaffolding for galaxies to form and grow, according to the model. Gas collects inside these haloes, eventually cooling and collapsing to form stars. But astronomers are still trying to understand why this process appears to happen in some haloes but not others.

One possibility is that some contain vast quantities of hydrogen gas, yet form very few stars. That would make them extremely difficult to spot.

Now, researchers have found a promising candidate for such a dark galaxy and published their findings in the Monthly Notices of the Royal Astronomical Society. The object, known as AGC 322753, contains roughly 2 billion times the sun's mass in hydrogen, yet appears to have no stars, though the researchers note that it is too big to be totally starless. However, if confirmed, it would be an unusually massive example of a starless galaxy.

Researchers at Swinburne University of Technology in Australia made the discovery by comparing observations from two existing radio surveys, one conducted by the Arecibo Observatory in Puerto Rico and the other by China's Five-Hundred-Meter Aperture Spherical Radio Telescope. Both telescopes had picked up a massive cloud of hydrogen.

Although other researchers have identified several dark galaxy candidates over the past decade, this one stands out for another reason: It may be rotating.

Hydrogen gas emits radio waves at a signature wavelength, which shortens when the gas moves toward Earth and stretches when it moves away. The signal from AGC 322753 shows two distinct peaks, suggesting that one side of the gas cloud is moving towards us while the other is receding.

This could mean that the gas has settled into an organized, rotating structure, much like the discs in ordinary galaxies, despite apparently having formed no stars.

"The theoretical models and the simulations show us that dark galaxies shouldn't rotate, so this is extremely interesting," says Marco Monaci, an author of the study at Swinburne University of Technology.

If AGC 322753 really is a rotating disc with no stars, astronomers will have to explain how the gas organized into such a structure without producing them.

That isn’t the only puzzle. Most models of galaxy formation predict that a cloud holding this much hydrogen should have formed at least a few stars, as its densest patches collapse under their own gravity and ignite.

The paper’s coauthors suggest it may be very unlikely that AGC 322753 contains absolutely no stars. So, instead, they think it may contain stars too faint to see–an “almost dark” galaxy. If so, they must be very faint indeed, because even deep optical images from the DESI Legacy Imaging Surveys, which search for starlight rather than the hydrogen gas detected by radio telescopes, reveal none.

From this, the team estimates that AGC322753 contains at least 40 times more mass in hydrogen than in stars, raising questions about what exactly suppressed stars from forming.

But further observations, such as ones made by the Very Large Array, a network of radio antennas in New Mexico, will be needed to confirm whether AGC 322573 is truly a dark—or almost dark—galaxy.

"Finding out more about it really is an essential next step at the moment. It's intriguing, and I want to know more," says Robert Minchin at the National Radio Astronomy Observatory.

Minchin knows how misleading such signals can be. He was involved in the discovery of VIRGOHI21, a dark galaxy candidate found in the early 2000s that was later reinterpreted as a long stream of hydrogen gas pulled from a nearby galaxy by gravity, known as a tidal tail.

Gas torn away like this can keep some of its original motion and mimic the signature of rotation, so Minchin says more observations are needed to rule out the same explanation for AGC 322753. Yet there is no obvious nearby galaxy it could have come from.

Rhys Taylor, an astronomer at the Astronomical Institute of the Czech Academy of Sciences in Prague, says tidal debris is unlikely to explain the observations. Reproducing the candidate dark galaxy’s distinctive double-peaked profile through such a process “is technically possible, but it’s fantastically unlikely,” Taylor says.

Taylor and Minchin both flag another concern, though. The two radio surveys that the researchers used placed the hydrogen at noticeably different positions in the sky. That suggests AGC 322753 may be an extended cloud of gas rather than a compact galaxy, another possibility that follow-up observations could help untangle.

Whether AGC 322753 turns out to be completely dark or merely exceptionally faint, understanding objects like it could still help astronomers work out why some galaxies form stars while others apparently do not, says Elisabeth Adams at the Planetary Science Institute.

One possible reason is a galaxy that spins so fast that its gas stays too spread out to collapse. Another is that ultraviolet light from the universe’s earliest stars heated the gas, keeping it too warm to condense. Studying such almost-dark galaxies could help tell these phenomena apart, revealing how the conditions of the early universe shaped the galaxies we see today.

“Once it’s no longer a dark galaxy, people seem to stop caring, and I actually think that’s not the right approach,” says Adams.

But if AGC 322753 truly is starless, it would be one of the most massive dark galaxies ever identified, potentially helping explain where the missing galaxies predicted by Lambda-CDM have been hiding. It could even help astronomers understand why some gas-rich galaxies form stars while others don't, providing a valuable test for models of galaxy formation—and perhaps even offering new clues about the nature of dark matter itself.

#586. A hidden limit of space and time could unlock electronics 100,000 times faster

Quantum physics places fundamental limits on what scientists can know about the behavior of particles. Werner Heisenberg's famous uncertainty principle states that certain properties, such as a particle's position and momentum, cannot both be measured with unlimited precision. This restriction is not caused by flaws in scientific instruments. It is built into the laws of nature.

But position and time are different. Heisenberg's uncertainty principle does not impose the same restriction on these quantities. Now, researchers have experimentally demonstrated another fundamental limitation: an electron's location and its behavior over time cannot both be determined with arbitrary precision. Known as the "space-time limit," this newly observed relationship could have major implications for the future of quantum technology and ultrafast electronics.

The discovery comes from an international collaboration involving research groups at the Regensburg Center for Ultrafast Nanoscopy (RUN), led by Profs. Jascha Repp, Rupert Huber, Franz Giessibl, and Klaus Richter, along with a team at the Max Planck Institute in Hamburg led by Angel Rubio.

Watching Electrons Move at Incredible Speeds

Understanding how matter behaves at the smallest scales is essential for developing the next generation of technology. Advances in clean energy, quantum computing, and powerful electronics for artificial intelligence all depend on knowing how chemical reactions unfold, how light interacts with materials, and how electrons travel through electronic devices.

However, even the sharpest microscopic photographs cannot reveal the full picture. Scientists also need to observe how these processes change over time, effectively creating slow-motion movies of events occurring at the atomic scale.

At RUN, researchers are developing ultrafast microscopes capable of recording the movements of electrons, atoms, and molecules with extraordinary precision in both space and time. About ten years ago, scientists in Regensburg used ultrafast scanning tunneling microscopy to resolve the movement of a single molecule across space and time for the first time.

Tracking individual electrons presents an even greater challenge. At these microscopic distances, electrons can move roughly a thousand times faster than atoms and molecules, with important changes taking place over just attoseconds.

To appreciate how extreme these scales are, consider that an atom is approximately ten million times smaller than a millimeter. An attosecond is one-billionth of a billionth of a second. The difference between an attosecond and a second is comparable to the difference between one second and the entire age of the universe.

Making these measurements is especially difficult because electrons do not behave like ordinary objects. Their motion follows the unusual laws of quantum mechanics rather than the familiar rules of classical physics.

Ultrafast Lasers Reveal Quantum Tunneling

To observe electron behavior at these extraordinary speeds, the researchers built a new laser system that provides much finer time resolution than earlier experiments. The system allows them to control and study the quantum motion of individual electrons directly.

In the experiments, carefully timed laser pulses drive electrons from an extremely sharp metal tip toward a silver surface. The distance they travel is only a few atomic diameters.

The researchers detect this movement by measuring the resulting electrical current. To determine exactly when the electrons move, they use two pulses of light separated by a precisely controlled interval.

Simon Maier, the lead author of the paper, explains: "By varying the time interval between the two laser pulses, we can directly observe how the electrons respond."

This approach revealed clear evidence of electron motion occurring on attosecond timescales. It showed that the laser pulses could transfer electrons within these incredibly brief intervals while allowing scientists to observe the process as it happened.

What makes the result particularly remarkable is how the electrons travel. Instead of moving like conventional particles, they behave as quantum waves. This allows them to pass through an energy barrier separating the metal tip from the silver surface, even though classical physics predicts they should not have enough energy to cross it.

This phenomenon, known as quantum tunneling, is somewhat like an object passing through a solid wall without breaking or damaging it.

"Our measurement can be understood as a high-speed camera for the electron wave packets, since you can see at what point in time the tunneling process takes place," explains Katharina Glöckl, a doctoral student and co-author of the publication.

To understand the measurements more deeply, researchers working with Prof. Angel Rubio performed sophisticated quantum simulations of the electrons' behavior near the "space-time limit."

Their calculations closely reproduced the experimental findings and uncovered another remarkable detail. The electrons did not respond to the light field instantaneously. Instead, their motion lagged behind it by approximately 500 attoseconds.

At such extreme scales, several fundamental features of quantum physics become important simultaneously.

For example, the researchers found that the laser pulses could not be described entirely by treating light as a wave or entirely by treating it as individual particles called photons. Their effects displayed characteristics of both descriptions. This combination was essential for exploring electron behavior so close to the "space-time limit."

The team also discovered that controlling electron motion over extremely short intervals changes how the electrons are distributed in space. Quantum mechanics describes these electrons using wave packets, which represent how their wave properties extend across different locations.

The relationship between the timing of electron motion and the spatial extent of these wave packets revealed the central limitation behind the discovery.

Raffael Spachtholz, another co-author of the study, explains: "The more precisely we want to pin down the electron's position in time, the more energy we need to provide. And as a result, the electron wave packet spreads out more spatially."

In other words, improving the precision of an electron's timing comes with a cost. The more tightly researchers define when its motion occurs, the more broadly its quantum wave packet spreads through space.

To examine this relationship experimentally, the team placed a single atom on the surface to confine the electron wave packets to an extremely small region immediately before the laser pulses arrived.

This setup allowed the scientists to directly measure the connection between the electrons' spatial distribution and the timing of their motion.

Importantly, even when the electrons received substantial energy from the laser pulses, their wave packets remained sufficiently localized for microscopy with atomic spatial resolution and attosecond time resolution.

The findings provide experimental insight into a fundamental space-time constraint that scientists had previously only suspected. They also make it possible to systematically investigate how changes in electron motion over time affect the spatial structure of quantum wave functions.

Could Quantum Electronics Become Hundreds of Thousands of Times Faster?

Beyond revealing a new aspect of quantum behavior, the discovery could open the door to technologies that operate on timescales far beyond the capabilities of today's electronics.

One especially promising possibility involves using precisely controlled electron wave packets to influence chemical reactions.

Transferring a single electron to a molecule represents the smallest possible transfer of electric charge. But when that electron is concentrated within an exceptionally small region of space and time, the resulting local peak current density can reach as much as 1 trillion amperes per square centimeter.

Such extreme conditions could give scientists new ways to control chemical bonds and observe how molecules change during reactions.

"In the future, we want to use such wave packets to specifically trigger chemical reactions and observe, on the relevant length and time scales, how chemical bonds can be broken or altered," explains Prof. Jascha Repp enthusiastically.

The implications could extend well beyond chemistry. Greater control over electron motion may eventually help engineers develop electronic devices and quantum information systems that operate at speeds approaching the fundamental limits of electron dynamics.

"In the long term, the insights gained could also contribute to operating electronics and quantum information processing at the intrinsic speed limit of electron motion itself -- hundreds of thousands of times faster than the currently dominant CMOS technology," adds Prof. Rupert Huber.

That possibility remains a long term technological goal rather than an achievement demonstrated in the current experiments. Nevertheless, the findings provide a new foundation for exploring how electrons can be manipulated at the smallest distances and shortest timescales.

For Repp and Huber, the results suggest that the range of potential applications is extraordinarily broad. They believe that future advances in controlling electrons at the space-time limit may ultimately depend more on human creativity than on the constraints imposed by nature.
