#583. DNA on the Shroud of Turin reveals centuries of hidden secrets

Scientists have uncovered a remarkable collection of DNA hidden within the Shroud of Turin, revealing a surprisingly complex biological history behind one of the world's most mysterious and controversial religious artifacts. The genetic evidence includes traces of humans, bacteria, fungi, plants, animals, and even Mediterranean red coral, offering new clues about the environments and people that have come into contact with the famous cloth over the centuries.

Published in Scientific Reports, the study used advanced DNA sequencing technology to examine samples collected from the Shroud in 1978. For the first time, researchers applied a sophisticated technique (rigorous PCR-free next-generation metagenomic sequencing) to these officially collected samples, allowing them to identify genetic material preserved within the linen fibers in unprecedented detail.

Unlike traditional DNA testing, which often focuses on genetic material from a single organism, metagenomic sequencing can detect DNA from many different species within the same sample. This makes it particularly useful for studying historical objects that have been handled, stored, and exposed to changing environments over long periods.

By examining this genetic material, the international research team assembled what they describe as the most comprehensive picture yet of the biological traces accumulated on the Shroud throughout its history.

Human DNA and a Hidden World of Microorganisms

Among the discoveries were multiple lineages of human mitochondrial DNA, suggesting that genetic material from different human populations has accumulated on the cloth. Mitochondrial DNA is a small portion of our genetic material that is inherited through the maternal line, making it especially useful for studying ancestry and population history.

The researchers also identified an unexpectedly diverse microbiome, the collection of microscopic organisms associated with a particular environment or object. These included bacteria commonly found on human skin, various fungi, and microorganisms adapted to living in salty environments.

Some of these organisms belong to a group known as archaea, which are single-celled microorganisms distinct from bacteria. Certain archaea can thrive in extremely salty conditions that would be difficult for many other forms of life to tolerate.

Together, these findings reveal that the Shroud carries a complicated biological record shaped by repeated human contact and exposure to different environmental conditions.

DNA From Plants, Animals, and Mediterranean Coral

The discoveries extended far beyond human DNA and microorganisms. Scientists also detected genetic traces from numerous plant and animal species, adding another layer to the Shroud's biological history.

Plant DNA included cultivated wheat, carrots, maize, bananas, and peanuts. Genetic material from domesticated animals was also present, including cattle, pigs, chickens, dogs, and cats.

One particularly unusual finding was DNA associated with Mediterranean red coral.

The wide variety of biological material suggests that the cloth has encountered a diverse range of living organisms and environmental influences. These genetic traces may reflect its long history of handling, storage, and movement through different geographical regions.

Importantly, the presence of DNA from a particular species does not establish when that material reached the cloth. Some traces could have accumulated relatively recently, while others may reflect much older interactions. This makes the findings valuable for reconstructing the Shroud's environmental history, but also highlights the difficulty of interpreting DNA recovered from an object that has been exposed to human activity for centuries.

Can DNA Solve the Mystery of the Shroud of Turin?

Despite the remarkable diversity of genetic material, the researchers emphasize that their findings cannot establish the Shroud's age or authenticity.

The Shroud of Turin is a linen cloth bearing the faint image of a human figure and has long been associated by some believers with the burial of Jesus. Its origins have been debated for generations, making it one of the most extensively investigated historical and religious artifacts in the world.

Because the cloth has been touched and exposed to different environments over many centuries, its DNA represents a mixture of biological material accumulated at different times. This makes it impossible to reliably separate any original DNA that might have been associated with the fabric from the many genetic traces deposited afterward.

Instead, the research demonstrates how modern forensic genomics and proteomics can reveal information that traditional methods might miss. Forensic genomics uses genetic evidence to investigate biological origins and interactions, while proteomics examines proteins, which can also preserve valuable clues about biological materials.

Together, these approaches offer powerful new ways to investigate how historical objects have been preserved, handled, and exposed to different environments.

Modern DNA Technology Reveals New Historical Clues

Professor Noemi Procopio, Professor of Forensic Science at the University of Lancashire, contributed to the international research collaboration. She emphasized that advances in forensic technology are making it possible to recover information from historical samples collected decades earlier.

Professor Noemi Procopio, Professor of Forensic Science at the University of Lancashire, said: "The Shroud of Turin continues to fascinate historians, scientists and the public, and we're thrilled to reveal new information about the biological complexity preserved within samples collected almost 50 years ago by applying new cutting-edge forensic DNA technologies.

"The Shroud represents a rich archive of genetic information that has accumulated over centuries of human interaction and environmental exposure. While the DNA evidence cannot answer all the questions about the age or authenticity of the cloth, it provides novel insights into its biological history and demonstrates how advances in forensic science can unlock new information from historical artifacts."

Materials provided by University of Lancashire. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#582. Most of Gen Z isnt turning to porn, Feeld report finds

More than a third of men say romance novels, erotic fiction, or fan fiction have shaped what turns them on. Most of Gen Z isn't turning to porn when dating gets hard. And eight in 10 adults say the buildup is a huge part of what makes sex exciting. (So much for instant gratification.)

That's according to the new State of Dating report from Feeld, the dating app “for the curious” (including those interested in ethical non-monogamy, kink, and unconventional relationships), and honestly? I'm not the least bit surprised.

The report, titled Holding Out For a Slow Burn, was developed in collaboration with Dr. Justin Lehmiller, a senior research fellow at the Kinsey Institute, and Feeld's senior product data analyst, Sy Dusk.

AdultFriendFinder — readers’ pick for casual connections

Tinder — top pick for fast hookups

Hinge — popular choice for regular meetups

Pure — best for anonymous hookups

OkCupid — best app for inclusivity

Here’s what the report, with insights from 3,310 adults in the U.S. and UK, revealed about Gen Z’s dating habits and modern dating in general.

Across the survey, 80 percent of respondents said it was difficult to meet their sexual and romantic needs through IRL connections, and 73 percent agreed that “modern dating often feels like it’s missing something.” What’s missing? For 58 percent, it was authenticity and clear intentions.

Dating has been “optimized,” maybe too much. We have so many apps and algorithms, but is anyone really taking the time to put themselves out there and meet organically? Or are we retreating to our “romantasy” novels and TV shows just to feel something again?

“Dating apps can certainly make it harder for slow-burn attraction to develop because they encourage us to make snap judgments based on limited information,” Dr. Lehmiller tells Mashable. “And since there’s always another potential match just around the corner, it can be tempting to just keep swiping in search of an immediate spark instead of giving attraction time to grow.”

The survey also found that nearly 60 percent of Gen Z men and more than 70 percent of Gen Z women do not turn to porn when real-world intimacy feels difficult. And in a separate 2026 Kinsey survey conducted in collaboration with Playboy and Julie Care, 34 percent of young men and 53 percent of young women said porn is something they “never watch.” Guess those “goon caves” aren’t as popular as the internet wants us to think.

Digital intimacy is taking over the world one sad little phone screen at a time. According to the latest survey, 81 percent of respondents said getting close to someone through a screen plays a role in their intimate lives. That’s not all AI — texting, sexting, and video calls count, too. But for one in six, digital intimacy is their main way of exploring intimacy, and about one in 10 turn to AI companions when connecting in real life gets difficult.

Some respondents said they believed digital intimacy could help their real-world relationships (29 percent), and 30 percent said it offers a way to explore their desires and vulnerabilities.

So, about that slow burn. The survey found that eight in 10 adults agreed that anticipation and buildup are "crucial to erotic excitement."

There are several reasons we might need more time to get there, including financial stress, the lingering effects of living through a pandemic, political turmoil, and general uncertainty — it’s enough for anyone to say, I’m not in the mood.

The idea of love at first sight is, well, lovely. But attraction doesn’t always happen right away. Only 13 percent of people surveyed said attraction develops instantly, while 86 percent agreed that “attraction typically becomes stronger when two people create a shared story together through conversations, experiences, and future plans.”

Research cited in the report suggests that getting to know someone can give that spark a chance to grow. For example, when someone turns out to be “funnier than you expected,” that can be a turn-on for your brain — it can trigger what’s called a positive prediction error. Basically, you’re experiencing something even better than your brain expected, which leaves you wanting more.

Mistakes, or the little things that make us human, can also give us brain boners. But you usually have to like the person already for it to work. Seeing them spill their coffee or make an embarrassing admission can make them more endearing, not less. You get to see a little of who they are, unfiltered. And in a world where “looksmaxxing” is a thing, we could stand to be a little less polished.

“Our desire for a slow burn isn’t inherently incompatible with dating apps. But we need to be more intentional in how we use them,” says Dr. Lehmiller. “Instead of fast swiping in search of instant chemistry, we need to give people opportunities to surprise us and reveal new sides of themselves. That’s where the unexpected rewards lie, and where attraction has an opportunity to go beyond the surface.”

And so, we’ve been getting our fix through books, streaming series (Bridgerton, Heated Rivalry, and Off Campus, just to name a few), and even audiobooks and audio erotica like Dipsea and Quinn. Which I’m totally here for.

Media has become so entwined in our intimate lives that one survey respondent described his most intimate moment as “Watching TV and crying in my partner’s arm because the episode struck chords I was feeling because of a recent familial loss.”

Men are turning to romance media that “taps into fantasies of being desired, understood, and truly seen,” which usually (not always) isn’t shown in porn. In some surveys, men reported fantasizing about “passion, intimacy, and romance” more than the women did.

Before COVID, a Kinsey Institute survey found that 6 percent of men said romantic or erotic novels had shaped their sexual fantasies. In Feeld’s new survey, 37 percent of men said romance novels, erotic fiction, or fan fiction had shaped their desires. These aren’t identical comparisons, but the romance genre clearly isn’t just for women.

And “romance” doesn’t necessarily mean the same thing to everyone. Men in the survey were more likely to associate romance with physical affection and touch, while women were more likely to associate it with emotional openness and vulnerability.

That said, when asked about their most intimate non-sexual moments, men and women described similar things: cuddling, talking, being vulnerable, and really listening to one another.

We can’t all live in a romantasy novel. But surely we can make time for a little sexual tension…

“It is quite funny to think about translating epic fantasy romance arcs into daily life filled with heading to work, taking out the bins, remembering to floss and reply to friends, and being generally unglamorous,” Ruby Rare, a sex educator and Feeld’s intimacy expert, tells Mashable. “But there are ways of weaving this in, and it all comes down to intentionality.”

Her advice? Tell the person you’re seeing that you want to take your time.

“Once you've connected with someone and felt that shared glimmer of excitement for what might come next, be vocal about your desire for a slow burn as an antidote to the self-gratification that we’ve become so used to,” Rare says.

“This isn't about going full Victorian and making courtship chaste and timid; instead, lean into activities that will deepen connection and intrigue between you. Write love letters, go for long walks where you challenge each other to stay off your phones and be present with each other, make out for as long as possible before things escalate.”

Tabitha Britt is an award-winning freelance journalist, editor, and SEO/AEO strategist. Aside from reviewing dating apps and sex toys for Mashable, Tabitha is also the founding editor-in-chief of DO YOU ENDO — a digital magazine by individuals with endometriosis, for individuals with endometriosis. She has a Master's degree in Creative Publishing and Critical Journalism from The New School for Social Research and is a grad of Sextech School. You can find more of her work in various online publications, including National Geographic, Glamour, Better Homes & Gardens, and Everyday Health.

#580. After 40 years, CERN finds evidence of a one-in-10-billion particle decay

After more than 40 years of searching, physicists at CERN have reached a major milestone in their hunt for one of the rarest known particle decays. Researchers working with the LHCb experiment have detected evidence of a process expected to occur only once in approximately 10 billion beauty particle decays.

The finding, announced during a seminar at CERN, marks the first time a single experiment has produced sufficient evidence for this extraordinarily rare event. Beyond its rarity, the decay is particularly valuable because it could help scientists investigate particles that are too massive to be created directly in the Large Hadron Collider.

At the center of the discovery is a particle called Bd, which contains a beauty quark and a down antiquark. Scientists found evidence that this particle can decay into a pair of muons (μ+μ-). Muons are similar to electrons but have greater mass. The results agree with predictions from the Standard Model, the leading theory describing fundamental particles and their interactions, and help narrow the possibilities for new particles beyond it.

Why Rare Particle Decays Could Reveal New Physics

One reason physicists are so interested in these unusual decays is that quantum effects allow other particles to influence how they occur. Even particles too heavy to be produced in the Large Hadron Collider can potentially leave subtle traces in the rates of these processes.

By measuring extremely rare decays with high precision, researchers can look for differences between experimental results and theoretical predictions. Such discrepancies could provide indirect evidence of previously unknown particles. Generally, the rarer a decay is, the more sensitive it can be to these hidden influences.

The decay known as Bd → μ+μ-belongs to a special category that physicists have long considered 'golden channels' for investigating physics beyond the Standard Model. During this process, a beauty quark and an antiquark with the opposite electric charge annihilate each other. Their energy is transformed into a muon and its antiparticle.

Scientists have previously observed a related process involving a different beauty particle, called Bs. In 2015, a joint analysis by the LHCb and CMS collaborations reported the first confirmed observation of Bs → μ+μ-, along with initial evidence for the much rarer Bd decay.

However, subsequent analyses incorporating data collected through 2018 could not confirm the earlier evidence for Bd → μ+μ-. The difference in rarity is substantial. The Bs decay occurs approximately three times per billion beauty particle decays, while the Bd process is predicted to happen about 30 times less frequently.

Upgraded CERN Detector Makes the Breakthrough Possible

A significant improvement to the LHCb experiment helped researchers overcome the enormous difficulty of detecting such a rare event.

Following a major upgrade, the experiment began collecting data in 2024 at five times the rate achieved between 2010 and 2018. The upgraded detector also became better at identifying different kinds of particles, giving researchers a clearer picture of the activity produced during collisions.

The team combined these improvements with machine learning techniques to distinguish the exceptionally rare signals from the vast number of other particle processes. Without these advances, the events scientists wanted to study would have been overwhelmed by the background.

"Physicists have been chasing this decay for more than forty years," said Marco Santimaria, who co-led the international analysis team of 19 scientists. "Extreme precision is required to pick the muons out of the overwhelming background."

Four Decades of Searching Finally Produce Strong Evidence

In particle physics, scientists use a statistical measure called standard deviations to evaluate how likely an apparent signal is to result from random fluctuations rather than a genuine physical process.

A result must reach three standard deviations to qualify as evidence of a new phenomenon. At that level, the probability of random fluctuations producing a signal at least as statistically extreme is approximately 0.3%.

Using data collected during 2024 and 2025, the LHCb researchers identified evidence of Bd → μ+μ- at a significance of 3.0 standard deviations. When they combined those measurements with earlier data gathered between 2011 and 2018, the statistical significance increased to 3.2 standard deviations, exceeding the widely accepted threshold for evidence.

The same investigation also produced the most precise measurements ever obtained for the related Bs → μ+μ-decay, providing additional information about these exceptionally uncommon processes.

Although the new findings represent a major achievement, physicists have not yet reached the threshold required to declare a confirmed observation. That milestone requires a significance of five standard deviations.

If future measurements achieve that level of certainty, Bd → μ+μ-would become the rarest beauty particle decay ever observed.

Future Large Hadron Collider Upgrades Could Uncover Hidden Particles

Researchers expect upcoming improvements to the Large Hadron Collider to make these measurements even more powerful.

The High Luminosity LHC, also known as the HiLumi LHC, is scheduled to begin operating in 2030. Another major upgrade to the LHCb experiment is planned for the middle of the following decade. Together, these developments could eventually increase the experiment's data collection rate by another factor of ten.

Collecting much larger datasets would allow physicists to detect more of these extremely rare decays and measure their properties with greater precision. That increased sensitivity could make it possible to identify subtle effects associated with particles that remain beyond the direct reach of existing accelerators.

"Seeing evidence of this rare decay is incredibly exciting, opening up new possibilities to probe beyond the direct reach of the LHC," explained Harry Cliff, LHCb's Rare Decays Convener and Analysis Co-lead.

"The future LHCb upgrade will allow us to observe many more of these decays, providing even greater sensitivity to undiscovered particles."

For physicists who have pursued this elusive signal for more than four decades, the new evidence represents a long-awaited advance. It also offers a promising way to investigate whether the known laws of particle physics are part of a much larger picture.

Materials provided by CERN. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#579. The Midterms Were a Pretty Good Sign for the Right-to-Repair Movement

Right-to-repair policies weren’t the headline issue in the midterms, but lawmakers across party lines ran on fixing the systems that lock buyers out.

Iowa senator-elect Josh Turek and presumptive Minnesota state representative Jake Johnson both centered repair policies in their platforms, focusing on issues that affect farmers and their equipment. (As of publication, Johnson’s race has not yet been called and may be headed to a recount.) In St. Paul, Minnesota, voters also passed a measure ensuring renters' right to repair with overwhelming support.

The right-to-repair movement, propelled by advocates in the US and internationally, pushes lawmakers for rules that prevent manufacturers from locking owners out of their own devices. Repair efforts tend to be bipartisan, with support ranging from consumers to farmers to wheelchair users.

“A political climate where people want policies that stand up for the little guy, help reduce unnecessary costs, and work in a bipartisan way—that’s a very good environment for right-to-repair,” says Nathan Proctor, senior director of PIRG’s right-to-repair campaign, in a text to WIRED.

Advancing right-to-repair legislation would be a step toward the larger ownership battles taking place in the US. People are angry that companies are turning everything into a subscription service or using software locks to restrict people from modifying or fixing their gear. That meshes with a long-simmering fury about the broader political climate, where tariffs, a memory shortage, and an extremely unpopular war with Iran have driven prices up on just about everything.

As Kevin O’Reilly, executive director of Fulu, an advocacy group focused on consumer digital ownership and product rights, points out, the efforts to claw back control over devices mimic the broader message voters sent at the ballot box.

“There’s a lot to be excited about,” O’Reilly tells WIRED. “People want things to be more affordable, politics to be less polarized, and Congress to solve problems that affect them.”

That discontent points straight to the root of the problem: Section 1201 of the Digital Millennium Copyright Act (DMCA). The 1998 federal statute made it illegal to circumvent digital locks, enabling companies to punish people who break them with possible prison time. Repair advocates have widely decried the legislation, as companies have used it to prevent people from fixing their devices without software access.

For advocates, the reshuffled Congress is the cleanest legislative target in decades. With voter frustration cutting cleanly across party lines, O'Reilly suggests the mandate is hard to ignore.

“If I were a new or returning member of Congress,” O’Reilly says, “I would see DMCA reform as a no-brainer.”

#575. NASA reveals bold new plan to return astronauts to the Moon in 2028

NASA has revealed new details about its accelerated effort to return American astronauts to the Moon in 2028. The updated strategy includes testing two competing lunar landers, streamlining rocket preparations, and introducing a lighter spacesuit to help make the first moonwalks possible sooner.

During an October 23 briefing, NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman and other agency leaders outlined the next steps for the Artemis program. The revised approach centers on two major missions: Artemis III, a test flight scheduled for 2027, and Artemis IV, which is intended to land astronauts on the lunar surface in 2028.

The announcement builds on priorities introduced earlier this year through NASA's Ignition initiative. Agency officials also highlighted recent progress in spacecraft development, mission preparations, and partnerships with private aerospace companies. These efforts are intended to reduce technical risks, accelerate the schedule, and establish the foundation for a lasting American presence on the Moon.

"This is a very energized NASA, and these updates are only a small part of that story. We are bringing extreme focus to the mission to ensure delivery of world-changing outcomes as quickly, safely, affordably, and successfully as possible," said Isaacman. "NASA is driving outcomes to make Moon missions a reality, and we're not waiting for others to bring their plans to us -- the campaign must move with urgency as strategic competitors pursue a permanent presence beyond Earth."

Artemis III Will Test Two Lunar Landers in Earth Orbit

Before attempting another crewed Moon landing, NASA plans to conduct a major rehearsal in Earth orbit. The Artemis III mission will bring together NASA's Orion spacecraft and experimental lunar landers developed by SpaceX and Blue Origin.

Four astronauts will travel aboard Orion, launching on NASA's powerful SLS (Space Launch System) rocket. During the planned 14-day mission, the crew will practice rendezvous and docking procedures with both companies' lander test vehicles. Rendezvous involves guiding two spacecraft to meet in orbit, while docking allows them to connect so astronauts can move between vehicles.

These demonstrations are designed to verify important spacecraft capabilities before Artemis IV attempts to carry astronauts to the Moon's surface.

The tests will take place in a carefully planned sequence. Blue Origin will first launch a test version of its Blue Moon lander into Earth orbit. NASA will then send Orion and its four astronauts into space aboard the SLS rocket, where Orion will rendezvous and dock with the Blue Moon vehicle.

Next, SpaceX will launch a test version of its Version 3 Starship lunar lander. Orion will separate from Blue Moon and perform another docking maneuver, this time with Starship. By testing both systems during a single crewed mission, NASA aims to gather essential operational experience while reducing uncertainties before the 2028 landing attempt.

NASA Cuts Rocket Preparation Times and Costs

The new mission details follow other recent Artemis progress reports. According to NASA, the hardware needed for Artemis III remains on schedule, and improvements in processing have reduced preparation timelines by approximately 50%.

The agency also reported a significant engineering achievement involving a critical rocket fuel-line component. Rather than relying on an outside supplier, NASA engineers redesigned, manufactured, and tested the component themselves in just five months. The work cost roughly one-tenth of an earlier estimate from an external vendor.

"NASA should be expected to do this kind of work, and we have some of the best technical knowledge in the world on rocket hardware and operations to do it," said Jeremy Parsons, the agency's Artemis program manager. "It's an example of how we are rebuilding our core competencies and developing centers of excellence in technical functions instead of continuing to outsource expertise."

The improvements reflect NASA's broader effort to strengthen its internal engineering capabilities, shorten development schedules, and reduce the cost of preparing missions for launch.

SpaceX and Blue Origin Offer Different Paths to the Moon

For Artemis IV in 2028, NASA intends to launch astronauts aboard Orion using the SLS rocket from Kennedy Space Center in Florida. Once in space, the crew will rely on a lunar landing system developed by either SpaceX or Blue Origin to reach the Moon's surface.

Both companies have revised their original mission designs to make operations simpler, more efficient, and more adaptable. Although they share the same objective, their approaches to transporting astronauts to the lunar surface differ considerably.

SpaceX plans to use its Starship spacecraft, which will take on additional propellant while orbiting Earth. After refueling, Starship will meet Orion and carry the spacecraft toward the Moon. Refueling in orbit is an important part of SpaceX's strategy because it allows the vehicle to receive the propellant needed for its journey after leaving the launchpad.

Blue Origin is developing a different approach using its Blue Moon Mark 2 lander. Its plan calls for additional propulsion stages in Earth orbit to send the lander toward the Moon. The lander will then meet Orion in a relatively low lunar orbit before carrying astronauts down to the surface.

Both companies are working toward having their systems ready to support a crewed Moon landing in 2028. Maintaining two potential landing systems gives NASA greater flexibility as development and testing continue.

NASA Explores New Rocket Options for Artemis IV

NASA is also considering two possible upper stages for the SLS rocket used on Artemis IV. An upper stage is the portion of a rocket that provides additional propulsion after the earlier stages have completed their work, helping send a spacecraft toward its destination.

One option is the interim cryogenic propulsion stage already used by SLS. The other is an extended version of United Launch Alliance's Centaur V upper stage, which would hold additional propellant.

By developing both possibilities, NASA hopes to preserve flexibility in its launch plans and avoid becoming dependent on a single technical solution. Having alternatives could help the agency respond to engineering challenges without jeopardizing the broader Moon landing schedule.

A New Lightweight Spacesuit Could Speed Up Moonwalks

Another important change involves the spacesuits astronauts will wear while exploring the lunar surface.

NASA is working with Axiom Space on a second, lighter design known as the "Sortie suit." The new suit is intended specifically for the first Moon surface excursions and weighs approximately half as much as Axiom's larger primary spacesuit.

The lighter design could simplify preparations for the earliest landing missions and help astronauts begin exploring the lunar surface sooner.

However, the Sortie suit is not intended to replace the primary design. Axiom's more capable suit will remain part of NASA's plans for future missions that require longer periods outside the spacecraft and more demanding surface activities.

Building a Permanent Human Presence on the Moon

NASA's ambitions extend well beyond returning astronauts to the lunar surface for a single mission.

As the Artemis program advances, the agency intends to send crews on increasingly challenging expeditions near the Moon's South Pole. This region is an important focus of future lunar exploration, and the missions are designed to expand how much of the Moon humans can investigate directly.

NASA describes the effort as part of a new Golden Age of innovation and exploration. The agency expects its lunar missions to support scientific discoveries, generate economic benefits, and encourage the development of technologies needed for more ambitious space exploration.

Ultimately, the goal is to establish an enduring human presence on the Moon rather than repeat the brief visits of earlier lunar expeditions. The experience gained from operating spacecraft, supporting astronauts, and carrying out increasingly complex missions on the lunar surface will also help NASA prepare for another historic objective: sending the first astronauts to Mars.

#573. Scientists finally discover why we learn to love bitter coffee

A first taste of black coffee can be surprisingly unpleasant. Its sharp bitterness is the kind of flavor our brains are naturally inclined to avoid. Yet millions of people eventually come to enjoy that same taste, often looking forward to their morning cup. What changes?

Scientists at UC San Francisco may have uncovered an important part of the answer. Their research reveals a little-understood dopamine pathway linking the digestive system and the brain, helping explain how we develop a liking for certain flavors through repeated experience.

Dopamine is a chemical messenger involved in motivation, reward, and learning. Researchers have long known that it helps the brain recognize experiences worth repeating. However, most studies have examined rapid dopamine signals that appear within seconds of encountering something rewarding, such as the sight of a favorite meal.

The new research, published November 4 in Nature, examines a different system. This slower pathway responds to information coming from inside the body, allowing the brain to learn about the benefits of food after it has been consumed.

"We've shown that this slow dopamine system is what drives us to learn to like certain foods over time, and we can see where and how that happens," said Zachary Knight, PhD, professor of Physiology and senior author of the study.

The findings offer a possible explanation for why an initially unpleasant flavor can gradually become enjoyable. Once the brain connects the taste of coffee with caffeine's stimulating effects, Knight explained, "that coffee starts to taste a lot better."

How the Gut Sends Reward Signals to the Brain

Scientists have spent decades investigating dopamine and its influence on behavior. Its familiar, rapidly acting signals help us respond to opportunities for reward. But dopamine also operates on a slower timescale, through a system that has received far less attention.

According to Knight, this slower process largely unfolds outside our conscious awareness.

When food enters the digestive tract, specialized sensors detect nutrients, including sugar and fat. Information about those nutrients travels toward the brain, where it can produce a dopamine response that develops gradually and continues for several minutes.

To investigate this process, Knight worked with James Grove, PhD, who was a graduate student at the time. They introduced nutrients directly into the stomachs of mice while monitoring dopamine activity throughout their brains.

The researchers used either sugar water or a liquid resembling a milkshake. Delivering these substances directly into the stomach allowed them to separate the effects of nutrients from the experience of tasting food.

The results revealed a powerful, prolonged dopamine response in a specific brain region called the anterior basolateral amygdala, or BLA.

The amygdala is involved in emotional processing and learning, including forming associations between experiences and their consequences. Scientists already knew that dopamine was released in this particular region, but its purpose had remained unclear.

"We could see that this dopamine is part of a different system and begin to understand its role," he said.

Scientists Find a Brain Circuit That Changes Food Preferences

Finding the dopamine signal was only the beginning. The researchers next wanted to determine whether it actually influenced how animals learned to prefer different flavors.

They first interrupted the newly identified pathway in mice. The animals continued eating normally, indicating that the disruption did not prevent them from consuming food. However, they lost the ability to learn that an unfamiliar flavor was associated with receiving calories.

The team then conducted the opposite experiment.

After mice tasted a flavor containing no calories, the researchers artificially activated dopamine fibers in the BLA. Remarkably, the animals developed a preference for that flavor even though it provided no nutritional reward.

In effect, stimulating the pathway reproduced the learning signal that nutrients would ordinarily generate.

Together, the experiments showed that this dopamine system plays a critical role in teaching the brain which flavors predict rewarding nutritional effects.

A Surprising Discovery in the Left Side of the Brain

The researchers also looked for evidence that a comparable process occurs in humans. Brain imaging studies conducted by Knight's collaborators revealed a strikingly similar pattern.

Dana Small, PhD, a psychologist at Yale University, revisited data from an earlier positron emission tomography (PET) study involving 13 men. Participants had consumed either a milkshake or a drink without calories.

Her analysis found that dopamine release following nutrient delivery was especially concentrated in the left side of the BLA.

A separate study led by Marc Tittgemeyer, PhD, and his colleagues provided additional evidence. Using functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI), which measures changes in brain activity associated with blood flow, the researchers examined 38 adults after they consumed glucose, a simple sugar.

They observed increased activity in the same brain region.

One of the most unexpected findings was that the response was not evenly distributed between the brain's two hemispheres.

In both mice and humans, nutrients produced a considerably stronger dopamine response in the left BLA than in the corresponding region on the right.

That imbalance surprised Knight because brain networks responsible for essential functions, such as feeding, are generally expected to work in a relatively symmetrical fashion.

Why the left side plays such a prominent role remains unanswered. Knight's laboratory is now investigating what causes this unusual difference.

Could This Discovery Help Scientists Change Food Cravings?

Identifying the pathway provides researchers with a clearer picture of how the digestive system influences the brain's learned responses to food. It also raises an intriguing possibility: Could scientists eventually use this knowledge to change which foods people find appealing?

One area of interest involves GLP-1 medications, a class of drugs that mimic a hormone naturally released by the gut. These medications can substantially alter appetite and eating behavior.

Knight's team did not investigate GLP-1 drugs in the new study, so it remains unknown whether they influence the dopamine pathway the researchers identified.

Still, Knight suggests that one possible explanation for some of their effects could involve reducing the dopamine signals generated when nutrients reach the digestive system. If that happens, foods might gradually become less rewarding because the brain would receive weaker signals reinforcing their flavors.

For now, this remains a hypothesis rather than a demonstrated effect of the medications.

The broader implications extend beyond hunger and fullness. Many approaches to changing eating behavior focus on reducing appetite or increasing feelings of satisfaction after a meal. The newly mapped pathway suggests that the brain's process of learning food preferences could eventually become another target.

By influencing how the brain connects flavors with their consequences, future treatments might someday help people develop different preferences rather than simply making them feel less hungry.

"By understanding the steps between nutrient detection in the gut and learning in the brain, it might be possible to fine-tune them to subtly change our preferences for different foods," Knight said.

The discovery offers a new perspective on why our tastes can change over time. From learning to enjoy bitter coffee to developing a preference for calorie-rich foods, what we love to eat may depend partly on a quiet conversation between the gut and brain that continues long after the first bite.

The research was supported by the National Institutes of Health (grants R01-DK106399, R01-DK138127, R01-NS116626, and R01-DK145100 and F31-NS120468).

Materials provided by University of California - San Francisco. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#570. Apple might turn Siri AI into an agent next year

Apple's intelligent assistant Siri has been an early success for the company, but in recent years it became a pain point as it lagged behind far more capable large language models such as OpenAI's ChatGPT. The company significantly improved Siri this year, rebranding the whole system Siri AI, but it's still not quite there yet.

This might change next year. According to Bloomberg's Mark Gurman, Apple's new CEO John Ternus is "pressing" Apple's software chief Craig Federighi to turn Siri into an AI agent that can take actions autonomously and perform a variety of tasks for users.

Bloomberg's report goes into the intricacies of the relationship between Ternus and Federighi, with the latter described as being an AI skeptic, while Ternus pushes for a more progressive approach to AI.

Bottom line for users is that Siri AI, while far more capable than last year's Siri, still isn't the type of assistant that you can just throw a bunch of (complex) tasks at and get back results. But next year's version should get a couple of advanced capabilities that will make it a powerful rival to other AI agents. The most important among these is the ability to tap into apps (both Apple's and third-party apps) and perform actions within them. Examples include asking Siri to book a table or order food, with the assistant autonomously performing the task through a service such as OpenTable or Uber Eats. Siri should also get more precise control over Apple's own apps; for example, it could go into the Photos app, edit a photo, and then share it with someone, all on its own.

The part in which Siri gets more capable of performing tasks in Apple's apps might come "as early as December," with the public version of iOS 27.3 (the company recently launched the first iOS 27.3 beta). More advanced Siri capabilities inside third-party iOS apps won't arrive until spring 2027, and is likely to be launched with either iOS 27.5 or iOS 27.6, with a possible launch ahead of Apple's WWDC conference in June.

All of this still isn't enough to make Siri as capable as an agent like ChatGPT's Dots or Meta's Muse, which you can give a task (such as creating a website) and have them work on various tasks as they build and improve the project. Gurman believes Apple intends to launch such capabilities for Siri as early as next year. One example of that has already been announced during WWDC 2026: Siri's ability to find weak passwords tied to a user's Apple account and changing them autonomously on the appropriate websites and in the user's Passwords app.

Ultimately, Gurman argues, Apple can allow itself to be a bit more cautious about this as it controls the hardware and software of the devices itself, but rivals could start developing hardware of their own, which could take away Apple's most important market advantage.

#569. Alzheimer’s may have early warning signs outside the brain

Alzheimer's disease is best known for its effects on memory and thinking, but new research suggests that some symptoms may develop in a surprising place: outside the brain.

Scientists at the University of Central Florida (UCF) have discovered that genetic mutations associated with Alzheimer's can interfere with the nerves that control muscle movement, even without involvement from the brain or spinal cord. The findings could help explain why some people with Alzheimer's experience changes in walking, balance, or coordination long before noticeable memory problems emerge.

The discovery raises the possibility that movement difficulties could provide earlier warning signs of the disease and that treatments focused exclusively on the brain may overlook important problems elsewhere in the body.

The research was led by James Hickman, a professor at UCF's Nanoscience Technology Center, and Research Professor Xiufang "Nadine" Guo, working alongside scientists at the healthcare technology company Hesperos. Funded by the National Institutes of Health's National Institute on Aging, the study used laboratory systems built from human cells to investigate how inherited Alzheimer's mutations affect movement. The findings were published in Alzheimer's & Dementia: The Journal of the Alzheimer's Association.

"Motor deficits may be an earlier indication [of Alzheimer's]," Guo says. "If we can detect those changes and intervene earlier, that could help delay the onset of central nervous system symptoms."

Why Alzheimer's Symptoms May Appear Before Memory Loss

Alzheimer's is generally associated with progressive memory loss, confusion, and declining cognitive abilities. However, doctors have also observed that some patients develop physical symptoms, including changes in balance, coordination, and gait (manner of walking), years before clear cognitive difficulties become apparent.

These observations have raised an important question: Are such movement problems simply a consequence of damage occurring in the brain, or could Alzheimer's also interfere directly with nerves elsewhere in the body?

To investigate, the UCF team focused on familial Alzheimer's, a rare inherited form of the disease that typically develops earlier in life (from 40 to 65 years of age) than the more common form.

Their experiments revealed that motor neurons carrying mutations linked to familial Alzheimer's could disrupt the connections responsible for controlling muscles, even when the brain and spinal cord were completely absent from the experimental system.

Motor neurons are specialized nerve cells that transmit signals to muscles, telling them when to contract and produce movement. The new findings suggest that Alzheimer's mutations can interfere with this process directly, rather than affecting movement only through damage to the brain.

"This is the first time it's been demonstrated that deficits in the peripheral nervous system can arise directly from these mutations," Hickman says. "It means drugs that target the brain may not fix problems in the rest of the body."

The peripheral nervous system includes the nerves located outside the brain and spinal cord. These nerves carry signals between the central nervous system and the rest of the body, helping control movement, sensation, and other essential functions.

Guo also points out that preserving the ability to move could have benefits beyond physical health. Regular physical activity is associated with better cognitive well-being, so protecting motor function may also help support brain health.

How a Miniature Human Cell System Revealed the Discovery

To examine the relationship between Alzheimer's mutations and muscle movement, the scientists used an innovative laboratory platform known as "human-on-a-chip" technology.

Developed and manufactured through Hesperos, a company co-founded by Hickman, these miniature systems bring different types of human cells together under controlled conditions. They allow researchers to recreate specific biological functions and observe how diseases interfere with them.

Unlike conventional laboratory experiments that examine isolated cells, these systems can reproduce important interactions between different tissues. They also provide an alternative to animal models, which do not always accurately reflect how human diseases behave.

For this study, the team created a miniature model of the neuromuscular junction, the connection where a motor neuron communicates with a muscle cell.

The model was deliberately designed without a brain or spinal cord. This was a crucial part of the experiment because it allowed the researchers to investigate whether Alzheimer's-related changes could cause movement problems independently of the central nervous system.

The scientists combined healthy muscle cells with motor neurons derived from stem cells that carried genetic mutations associated with familial Alzheimer's disease. Stem cells are cells that can develop into specialized types of cells, making them valuable tools for studying human diseases in the laboratory.

The researchers then examined how effectively the altered nerve cells communicated with otherwise healthy muscle tissue.

The results showed that the Alzheimer's-associated mutations could interfere with normal nerve-to-muscle function. This suggests that at least some movement difficulties linked to the disease may arise directly in the peripheral nervous system rather than being caused entirely by degeneration in the brain.

Why Damaged Nerve Connections Can Affect Walking and Balance

Every voluntary movement depends on successful communication between the nervous system and the muscles.

At the neuromuscular junction, an electrical signal traveling along a motor neuron triggers chemical communication that causes a muscle to contract. This process makes everyday activities possible, from walking and maintaining posture to lifting objects and performing precise movements.

When the connection becomes impaired, muscles may respond less effectively to nerve signals. Depending on the severity and location of the problem, this can contribute to weakness, reduced coordination, and difficulty sustaining physical activity.

To evaluate these effects, the research team measured how consistently nerve signals produced muscle contractions and how long the muscles could continue contracting before becoming fatigued.

These measurements provided a direct assessment of how well the nerve and muscle cells worked together. They also resemble aspects of the functional testing physicians use when examining patients with movement disorders.

"You can't move unless the motor circuit works," Hickman says. "When a doctor taps your knee to check your reflex, they're testing that exact connection."

By demonstrating impaired nerve-to-muscle communication in a system containing no brain or spinal cord, the study provides evidence that mutations linked to familial Alzheimer's can produce effects beyond the central nervous system.

However, the experiments do not establish that these changes necessarily occur before memory loss in patients. Further research will be needed to determine how closely the laboratory findings reflect the progression of Alzheimer's in people, particularly those with the more common forms of the disease.

Could This Discovery Lead to Earlier Alzheimer's Detection?

The findings suggest a potentially important new direction for Alzheimer's research: looking beyond memory and other cognitive symptoms when investigating the disease.

If future studies confirm that changes in motor function are detectable early in certain patients, tests of walking, coordination, or nerve-to-muscle activity might eventually help identify disease-related changes before more obvious neurological symptoms develop.

The results also raise questions about treatment. Many Alzheimer's therapies are designed to address processes occurring in the brain, but medications that target the central nervous system may not necessarily correct abnormalities affecting peripheral nerves and muscles.

Understanding these separate effects could help researchers investigate treatments that protect physical function alongside cognitive health.

Human-on-a-Chip Technology Could Transform Disease Research

Beyond the implications for Alzheimer's, the researchers see considerable potential in using human-on-a-chip systems to investigate other diseases and evaluate possible treatments.

Because these platforms contain human cells and reproduce measurable biological activities, they can help scientists observe disease-related changes in conditions that more closely resemble human physiology than some conventional experimental models.

This approach may be particularly useful in drug development, where researchers need to understand not only how a potential treatment affects individual cells but also how it influences interactions between different tissues.

Such systems can also uncover biological effects that may be difficult to detect in animal experiments, potentially helping researchers identify promising therapies and recognize limitations earlier in development.

For Hickman, the progress represents the culmination of three decades of work devoted to understanding disease through more realistic laboratory models.

"These systems let us study disease in a way that's closer to what actually happens in the human body, and that's what we need to develop better treatments," he says.

#567. New Hulu Doc ‘Get Huge’ Shows the Deadly Toll of Looksmaxxing

Before Clavicular, before Androgenic, before the Tate brothers, before bonesmashing and frame-mogging and the manosphere and looksmaxxing entered the online lexicon, there was Tony Huge.

A 44-year-old former model turned lawyer turned bodybuilder turned supplement impresario, Huge fled the United States for Thailand to escape legal pressure on his company from the Food and Drug Administration and to build out a “harem” of young, nubile Thai women. With his bulky frame, his sex-and-drugs-fueled lifestyle, and his motto “a day natty [natural] is a day wasted,” Huge became an icon for what was then the fledgling biohacking community. He amassed a huge following of disaffected, lonely young men, some of whom moved to Thailand to live with him. Then, some of them started dying.

Huge is one of the main focuses of Get Huge: Looksmaxxing to Death, a bone-chilling documentary series hitting Hulu on November 10. Directed by Stacey Lee and executive-produced by the journalist Jennings Brown, Get Huge kicks off with the untimely death of “Leo and Longevity,” aka Laith Abdallah Algaz, a biohacker and penis enlargement influencer who was found deceased in Huge’s home in January 2023.

What follows, however, is not a typical true-crime documentary. It’s a dizzying investigation into the hedonistic, self-destructive, and often deadly world of self-optimization, in which dudes inject themselves with pig’s brains and liquified gold, alliances shift at the drop of a hat, and you’re only as good as your last high-performing Instagram Reel. (A warning, spoilers for Get Huge follow.)

Since 2023, Huge has opened up his Pattaya, Thailand, home to the Get Huge team, offering “full transparency” into what Lee refers to as “this 24/7 performance of being a man.”

“It was, ‘Here’s my harem, here’s my other men that I’m leading, here’s my muscles, here’s my drugs,’” she says. But as her crew got further and further into the filmmaking process, it became clear that what was going on behind the scenes—not just the turmoil within Huge’s inner circle but also the hangers-on dropping like flies around him—was far more interesting.

“Therein lies the problem for the young kid watching at home who is watching this as a blueprint for their life. They can't discern what's real and what's not,” Lee says. “Even I was having trouble discerning what was real and what’s not.”

In some respects, Get Huge is about the rise of looksmaxxing, the wildly popular and dangerous online self-optimization movement popularized by manosphere influencers such as Clavicular and Androgenic. (The filmmakers say Clavicular participated in initial talks with the producers to participate in the documentary but ultimately declined; Androgenic plays a small but key role in the series.)

Tony Huge in the new docuseries, Get Huge.

But Jennings Brown, a journalist and podcast producer, started delving into Huge’s world in 2023, long before Clav became a household name. Brown and his producing partner, Brett Neitchin, were obsessed with the manosphere and how the internet has ushered in extreme and rigid views of masculinity. “I went to school in Kansas and was short and felt insecure about that and always thought someday I’d save up for leg extensions,” he said. “So I’ve always had a horrified fascination with this world.”

Lee came on board as director a year later, just as the manosphere was starting to become a major talking point with the 2024 election. “It was such an absurd story—I mean, a penis influencer dying in Thailand,” she says. “But what was really interesting beyond the subject matter was the deeper story—why are men going to Thailand, and why is this kind of masculinity becoming more and more prevalent in online spaces?”

In some respects, Get Huge is a relatively straightforward true-crime story. Algaz was found deceased in Huge’s home with his pants around his ankles in January 2023, and Thai police ultimately concluded he died of blunt force head trauma. (The case is still open.) Internet sleuths in the looksmaxxing community immediately started pointing fingers at Huge, who had also been linked to two other prominent looksmaxxers, Nick Weisser and Josh Brooks, who died under equally mysterious circumstances in 2018 and 2021, respectively.

But as the filmmakers delved deeper into Huge’s world, they realized that was not the appropriate tack. “True crime became a vehicle through which to actually explore and understand this world, the stakes of it, the responsibilities of it, what keeps men there after these tragedies happen,” Lee says. “We wanted to know not only what draws them in but what keeps them there.” (That was particularly true for Algaz, who had recently abandoned his wife and child in Los Angeles before coming to live with Huge in Thailand.)

In some ways, the question of what keeps Huge’s acolytes in Thailand is the driving force of the series, particularly after the bodybuilder and influencer Connor Murphy (who one of the men in Huge’s circle refers to as “the ultimate chad”) joins the inner circle. As the men resort to increasingly extreme looksmaxxing methods, with Hughes even undergoing a psychotic break as a result of his drug use, they remain entrenched in Huge’s circle until Huge essentially decides to cast them out.

Huge did not respond to WIRED’s request for comment on the documentary.

Though the men frequently cite the importance of male friendship in their lives, they are constantly bickering and enabling each other, filming themselves engaging in increasingly self-destructive behavior to increase their viewership. “They don’t take care of each other,” one of Hughes’ former acolytes, Elon Muscular, says in Get Huge. “They don’t uplift each other. They find ways to fucking fuck each others’ lives up with bad habits.”

There’s also, it must be said, an obvious strain of queerness throughout the series, with the men regularly taking copious amounts of drugs before having group sex with each other and a bevy of Thai sex workers, who Huge calls his “harem.”

“Bodyhacking and looksmaxxing have a very homoerotic side,” says Lee. “It’s a bunch of dudes oiled up, posing with each other in little Speedos, and many of the drugs that began circulating within the community came from gay culture. [And] I think there is a lot of closeted behavior and tendencies to be explored.”

Huge uses his “harem” to burnish his macho image, tattooing his name on one and paying for another’s breast implants. In one shocking scene, he shoots some members of the harem up with drugs to increase their sexual attraction to him. Huge is clear throughout that he is paying these women for their time, raising uncomfortable ethical questions for the audience and the filmmakers alike.

“As a woman, it was incredibly challenging to witness” the way Huge treats his girlfriends, says Lee. “I think he would rationalize what he's doing is giving these women the best versions of their lives. Same with the men—he finds them at rock bottom, and he thinks he can improve them with his chemical fixes.”

One of the most shocking moments of the series, however, happened after the crew had stopped shooting in Pattaya, when Murphy was found dead in a lake in his backyard in July 2026. Murphy had been engaging in increasingly erratic behavior, with Thai media outlets reporting that two syringes and unidentified pills were found in his vehicle after he died, and according to Get Huge, he had recently started shooting up liquid gold. Authorities attributed his cause of death to accidental drowning.

To the team behind Get Huge, however, Murphy was one more casualty associated with an increasingly dangerous movement, as well as yet another loss in Huge’s inner circle. “I think we’ve always had this feeling that this extreme form of masculinity they all believe in was a huge contributing factor” to the deaths, Lee says. In one eerie scene, Huge appears to foreshadow Murphy’s death, addressing critiques that he may be enabling his drug use by callously saying, “It’s not the end of the world if something bad happens. People also learn from that.”

Perhaps more haunting than Murphy’s death after the cameras stopped rolling, however, is that looksmaxxing has only become more popular since Get Huge started filming.

Since production wrapped four weeks before Murphy’s death, Clavicular’s public profile has only grown, with the 20-year-old influencer garnering headlines for his “Camp Looksmax” workshop and walking in a major New York Fashion Week show. The Hulu team was even hit with a cease-and-desist from Clavicular after Mitchell Jackson, Clavicular’s publicist, says they attempted to get footage from the Elena Velez fashion show without his consent, leading to him withdrawing from the project.

“Hulu needs Clavicular. Clavicular doesn’t need Hulu,” Jackson tells WIRED. “That’s why they’re using his name to promote their stupid documentary.” (Brown, who was present, refutes this, saying he was not there to film and was only attending the fashion show in a personal capacity.)

Even as Huge gets older and his audience share is dwarfed by those of the younger generation of looksmaxxers—“relevancy is everything in this world, and looksmaxxing is a young man’s game,” says Lee—the movement he has spawned continues to gain traction. The deaths of Murphy and Algaz have served as little deterrent.

“No one starts out wanting to become one of these extreme characters, and I think there’s something we can all relate to about just trying to be the best version of yourself that you can be,” says Lee. “[But] when Leo died, did anybody stop? When Connor died, did anyone stop? And I think that for me, the question is, when the goalposts keep shifting and the nihilism and the looksmaxxing and the living for today becomes more and more extreme, where does it end?”

#566. Rosalía drops 1,000-song Spotify playlist to celebrate LUX

If you're spinning the same songs on Spotify over and over again, Rosalía has a monster playlist for you.

Marking the one-year anniversary of her wildly popular, critically lauded, Grammy-nominated album LUX, the Spanish artist shared a 1,000-track Spotify playlist for fans in a Substack post over the weekend.

Titled "1.000 TEMAS QUE TE DAN LUX" ("1,000 SONGS THAT GIVE YOU LUX"), the playlist is a fittingly eclectic, global mix of artists including icons Kate Bush and Patti Smith, Spanish singer Camarón de la Isla, famed opera singer Maria Callas, Icelandic queen Björk (who featured on LUX), Bulgarian ensemble Bulgarian State Television Female Vocal Choir, Egyptian reciter Abdul Basit Abdul Samad, Ethiopian composer Emahoy Tsege Mariam Gebru, as well as German experimental electronica, Spanish flamenco, Ukranian folk songs, Czech polka, Austrian and Italian classical, Japanese Buddhist chants, medieval para-liturgical songs, and Black Sabbath. It's deeply LUX-inspired.

"It's been a year since I gave Lux a try and I still can't believe it, how daring of me, haha," Rosalía wrote on Substack on Saturday.

"I love acts of service; there's something about preparing something for someone else that fascinates me. That's why I've dedicated myself to making a playlist of 1,000 songs that I listened to during the years I conceived, composed, and shared Lux," she added, sharing the anniversary playlist.

Released Nov. 7, 2025, Rosalía’s chart-topper LUX became the most-streamed album in a single day in Spotify history by a Spanish-speaking female artist — a level of popularity echoed in her extremely sold-out LUX world tour. The album was nominated for Album of the Year at the 27th Latin Grammy Awards, with track "La Perla" nominated for Record of the Year, Song of the Year and Best Pop Song.

You can listen to Rosalía's LUX playlist on Spotify.

#563. Wayfair sales jump 10% as furniture retailer says it's winning share of sluggish market

Wayfair on Wednesday said it posted its strongest quarter of sales growth since the pandemic as the furniture retailer continues to win over customers and take market share.

The company said it saw double-digit revenue growth in the U.S., reaching about $3 billion, an 11.4% increase from the year prior. Still, international net revenue for the quarter decreased 2.3% year over year to $380 million.

Wayfair's stock closed about 11% higher on Wednesday.

The report comes as the housing market remains sluggish. But the furniture and home goods retailer has reported consistent growth over the past year. CFO Kate Gulliver told CNBC that Wayfair's momentum is building as it takes market share.

"We see the overall consumer in the U.S. in this category, we think it's roughly flattish, and so that's our [market] share momentum continuing to build," Gulliver said.

She added that shopper loyalty, the opening of new stores, the company's luxury furniture business and an emphasis on value has allowed Wayfair to win more consumers. Customers are also continuing to spend, whether at the company's value prices or on the luxury side, she added.

"We've been highly promotional for the last few years, and the promotional activity this year is actually very consistent with last year," she said. "It maintains this sort of momentum on promotional activity this year."

Here's how the company performed in its third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

For the period ended Sept. 30, Wayfair reported net income of $62 million, or 45 cents per share, compared with a loss of $99 million, or 76 cents per share, the year prior.

Total revenue grew 9.7% year over year, reaching $3.42 billion.

The company said its active customers grew 4.7% year over year, reaching 22.2 million. It also saw a 12.2% increase in orders delivered during the quarter to about 11 million.

As the company looks ahead to the holiday season and the fourth quarter, Gulliver said it's maintaining promotional activity similar to last year. She added that Wayfair is confident that its range of prices and products will appeal to a broad base of consumers.

"We've seen that growth play out," Gulliver said. "We've seen that flow through to EBITDA, ultimately to net income positive this quarter, which is really another key piece in the story and the momentum here, so we feel very good about the quarter of the performance."

#562. King Arthur Baking Sourdough Sidekick Review: Rising to the Occasion

Like many people, I enjoy baking my own bread—especially this time of year. I baked sourdough on and off for nearly a decade, even having a recipe tested for a James Beard-winning bread cookbook, but I’m not proud to admit it’s been over a year since I baked a proper sourdough loaf.

As a full-time working parent, maintaining a sourdough starter sometimes feels like bringing home another pet. (You know, the kind your child swears they will care for before abandoning all tasks after three days.) You can certainly keep your starter in the fridge for around a week between bakes, but reviving it may take a day or two of feedings, and let’s be real, Will you remember to do this every week? Even the pet snake my son begged for only needs feeding every other week.

It is for people like me that King Arthur Baking Company and FirstBuild, GE Appliances’ product development lab, created the Sourdough Sidekick, a gizmo that automatically feeds and maintains a sourdough starter for you. We wrote about the Sourdough Sidekick when it was first announced in 2025, and now, after trying it for three weeks, I can say that my sourdough-baking dry spell has finally come to an end, perhaps even permanently.

Sourdough Sidekick, pictured here with the optional Crock Grip Band ($15)

The Sidekick is a Mr. Coffee-sized appliance with a built-in water reservoir, flour hopper, and glass crock with a stirring-paddle lid. I deal with an awful lot of apps in my job, and the Sidekick uses GE’s generic SmartHQ for GE appliances, which isn’t specific to this device and seemed optional. I admit I couldn’t be bothered with an unnecessary app, so I didn’t download it.

King Arthur Baking the Sourdough Sidekick

Instead, I followed the directions in the included Quick Start Guide booklet, which indicated that I simply needed to fill the water reservoir and flour hopper, set the current day and time (and the day and time I wanted the starter to be ready—you can choose up to seven days ahead; I chose seven), add a little bit of existing starter, and press “Auto Mode.” Almost immediately, a torrent of flour released from the hopper and began mixing into my seed starter with the stirring paddle. (King Arthur recommends keeping a portion of your starter in the fridge in case things go wrong, and I second this suggestion.)

Oddly, no water was dispensed in this process, so post-mixing, my starter was now in the form of an under-hydrated ball. Over the next few days, it became a larger, drier ball until I finally decided to remove the crock to add a couple of tablespoons of water; as I did, I noticed the reservoir was still at its maximum fill line.

I decided I should probably download the app, where I noticed the Sidekick indicated it needed a firmware update. After the update, the Sidekick began dispensing water during mixing, but I realized I really should have followed the instructions in the main instruction booklet, which calls for using a scale to properly calibrate the amounts of water and flour the machine dispenses.

Theoretically, you should only have to do this once, which is good because it’s an irritatingly lengthy and fiddly process. However, my next attempt (remember when I mentioned saving some starter in the fridge?) went much better, properly dispensing 30 grams each of water and flour each day and initiating a stirring cycle every two hours. It starts with a loud beep, then the paddle begins a loud mechanical whir, and for the first few days this can be quite startling, especially if you’re in the same room when it happens.

You can bypass the every-two-hours stirring cycle via the onboard controls, but since the stirring’s purpose is to knock down the starter’s volume and reincorporate any unconsumed flour, I kept it on. (For what it’s worth, I did think the paddle, an update from the Sidekick’s first design, did a pretty good job of incorporating all of the flour and water each time it mixed.)

Note that, in part due to the regular mixing and the lack of discard (Auto Mode will prompt you to discard after a week, while Ratio and Custom modes, detailed below, will have discard reminders), your finished starter in the Sidekick may not look as traditionally puffy or bubbly as you may be used to. You can disable stirring, or you can go on scent—it should smell tangy and indisputably fermented. As the many online Sidekick adherents often say, “Trust the process.” Some online users recommended adding a few teaspoons of more nutritionally dense rye flour to the (presumably all-purpose) flour in the hopper, which is supposed to supercharge the starter’s growth, but I admit I didn’t notice much of a difference in appearance, smell, or performance between an all-purpose starter and a rye-boosted starter.

Boules made with end-of-week starter maintained via the Sourdough Sidekick

Other than enabling firmware updates, the app does little beyond displaying the machine’s current settings and sending a push notification when the starter is finished. You can’t adjust settings or control the machine from it, but the most recent firmware update supports power-loss recovery up to 12 hours, which I tested and found successful after my husband tripped a breaker one day. The display shows the ambient temperature, but unlike other starter-babysitting gadgets like the Goldie or Brod & Taylor’s Sourdough Home, the Sourdough Sidekick has no way to adjust the starter’s temperature, which could be an issue if your house or kitchen runs cold. It does, however, offer “temperature-aware” feedings in Auto Mode, adjusting amounts based on detected room temperature.

In addition to Auto Mode, there’s Ratio Mode (where you can indicate starter-flour-water feedings by ratio—1:1:1, 1:1:2, et al.) and Custom Mode, which is what I ended up using the most, as I like to switch between flour types for different kinds of bread—you can dictate the exact flour and water quantities, as well as timing of the feeding (once a day, or even twice a day). In a typical week, the crock was about two-thirds full by the end, with more than 400 grams of starter—enough for two boules and a sandwich loaf with some seed left over for the next batch—ready to use right out of the crock.

The resulting bread turned out no differently than it had with traditionally maintained starter. If you’re unable to bake at that time, you can just discard most of the starter, leaving some “seed” behind for the next batch, or keep it in the refrigerator for a few days before reviving it the traditional way. (The crock comes with a metal lid for this purpose.)

King Arthur Baking the Sourdough Sidekick

If you’re only baking one loaf, you’ll have excess, but you can use the discard in recipes or give it away. Just note that if you intend to make a starter with a different flour, like whole wheat or full rye, you will need to calibrate the machine again as you did at the beginning. All removable components are top-rack dishwasher safe.

The Sourdough Sidekick is one of the most compelling kitchen-tech gifts I’ve ever seen for someone who bakes a lot or is interested in sourdough baking but intimidated by the process of keeping a starter. (The Sidekick also includes directions for making your own starter right in the machine.) Is it a necessary device? Of course not—especially since it costs about $175 more than a spoon and a Mason jar. But I can tell you I wouldn’t have baked bread this year without it.

King Arthur Baking the Sourdough Sidekick

#558. Kraft Heinz to divest its North American coffee business in $650 million deal

Kraft Heinz announced Wednesday that it will divest its North American coffee unit in a deal that values the business at $650 million on an enterprise basis, as it works to refocus on its core brands.

The deal with private equity firm One Rock Capital Partners includes brands such as Maxwell House and Gevalia and is expected to close in mid-2027, the company said in a news release. Kraft Heinz will retain a 30% noncontrolling equity stake in the business.

Kraft Heinz said it will use the $500 million of post-tax cash proceeds from the transaction to pay down its debts as it works to prioritize resources where it can accelerate growth.

"This transaction is a meaningful step in unlocking value from our portfolio and will further accelerate the momentum behind our transformation," Steve Cahillane, CEO of Kraft Heinz, said in the release.

Cahillane joined the company in January and paused work on plans to split the company in two, a move that would have reversed the blockbuster $46 billion merger from a decade ago. He said the problems Kraft Heinz faced were "fixable and within our control."

The turnaround efforts have included a $700 million investment back into the business and a sharpened focus on some of its core brands.

Also on Wednesday, Kraft Heinz reported third-quarter earnings that beat expectations and raised its 2026 outlook. It now expects organic net sales to be down 0.5% to 1.25%, an improvement from its second-quarter report, which projected that sales could decrease as much as 2%.

"Our 2026 operating plan to turn around the U.S. business and accelerate growth internationally is on track," Cahillane said. "Relative to last year, our market share, sales, and volume trends are improving in every region. We are increasingly meeting consumers where they are, and giving them more reasons to choose our brands."

Shares of the company were up about 5% Wednesday morning.
