#556. Jensen Huang Hired a PR Firm Known for Handling Sex Scandals Last Year

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang began working with a crisis communications firm known for handling public scandals a year before he admitted to having an extramarital affair with a woman suspected by US intelligence of being a Chinese spy. The firm, Heller, has handled Huang’s behind-the-scenes communications since at least October 2025, according to two sources familiar with the matter.

Risa Heller, the firm’s founder, has built a reputation as a fixer for powerful men facing allegations of sexual misconduct and other personal controversies, according to a 2023 New York magazine profile. Her past clients and people who have sought her advice include former congressman Anthony Weiner, chef Mario Batali, and writer Jeffrey Toobin. Heller also handles corporate communications and issues unrelated to personal controversies for its clients.

Heller’s public relations consultants were initially believed to be hired for matters related to Huang’s personal life, including his family’s philanthropic endeavors, according to people familiar with the arrangement. The firm frequently communicated with Huang and his daughter Madison, a product marketing director at Nvidia who has become an increasingly prominent figure within the company, sources tell WIRED. It’s not clear whether Heller’s early work with the Huang family involved Huang’s affair.

In 2024, Huang began appearing in public with a woman who goes by Alice Huang, a former executive at the Singapore-based chip buyer Megaspeed, according to The New York Times. Megaspeed was created when it split off from a Chinese gaming and cloud computing firm in 2023, a few months after the US government began restricting exports of advanced semiconductors to China, including those developed by Nvidia. A subsidiary of Megaspeed later became a major buyer of Nvidia chips.

In early October 2025, Jensen Huang’s business dealings with Megaspeed and his connection to Alice became public in a lengthy New York Times report, which alleged that the US Commerce Department was investigating whether the company was helping Chinese firms evade American export controls through its subsidiaries.

The report caused tensions to rise within Nvidia’s top ranks, and Jensen was rankled by the story, according to sources. By the end of that month, Nvidia’s internal communications teams had been instructed to include Heller’s firm on any press inquiries about Huang, whether personal or professional, according to people familiar with the matter.

When reached by email, a representative for Heller referred WIRED to Nvidia. Nvidia did not immediately respond to requests for comment.

US intelligence officials have determined that Alice (who has no known relation to Jensen) is a spy working for the Chinese government, according to the Times. Nvidia initially said that Jensen’s interactions with Alice were part of routine business with clients. But on Sunday, Huang acknowledged that his relationship with her was romantic in nature. In an email to Nvidia staff, he said he owed them and his family an apology. “I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry,” Huang wrote.

Nvidia’s board of directors announced it is opening an independent investigation into Jensen’s activities. It said it has no reason to believe that national security or confidential Nvidia information has been compromised.

Before founding her firm, Risa Heller worked for Jane Harman, then a Democratic congresswoman from California, and later served as Democratic senator Chuck Schumer’s New York City press secretary. Nvidia’s head of public policy communications, John Rizzo, also previously worked for Schumer, serving as his deputy communications director.

#552. The 9 Best TV Shows to Stream This Month (November 2026)

With temperatures dropping, the holidays approaching, and relatives preparing to invade the sanctity of our homes, November is the perfect time to find a little peace and quiet in front of the TV.

Fortunately, this month’s lineup of must-watch shows offers plenty of excuses to escape, including a dramatization of one of the biggest scandals in cryptocurrency history, a return to the dystopian world of Blade Runner, and a dark comedy about desperate parents who turn to surveillance technology to find their missing daughter.

There’s also a return to Middle-earth, a deadly game of international espionage, and a mysterious archaeological discovery that sets off a globe-trotting conspiracy. Plus, we're revisiting two groundbreaking television series that helped shape our fascination with government secrets, surveillance, and conspiracy theories.

As always, we’re not just chasing the biggest names or the shows everyone is already talking about (as much as we do enjoy The Gilded Age). Instead, these picks reflect the kinds of stories WIRED covers every day, from technology and science to politics and culture, and the unexpected ways they often intersect.

Whether you’re in the mood for a mind-bending sci-fi thriller, a twisty international conspiracy, or a classic worth rediscovering, these are the TV shows worth making time for this month.

Ben Whishaw (Q in Daniel Craig’s Bond movies) is one of the finest actors working today, even if his name isn’t quite as recognizable as it should be. Fortunately, Black Doves gives him plenty of opportunities to remind us why.

The deliciously twisty British spy thriller stars Keira Knightley as Helen Webb, a seemingly ordinary wife and mother who just happens to be secretly passing government intelligence to a shadowy organization known as the Black Doves. Whishaw plays Sam, her longtime friend and a professional assassin who gets pulled back into her dangerous double life. Then there’s the always-brilliant Sarah Lancashire (Happy Valley) as Helen’s formidable handler, Mrs. Reed.

Season 2, which arrived November 5, raises the stakes considerably as Helen’s husband prepares to become Britain’s prime minister, putting her at the very heart of government. It’s a wickedly entertaining mix of political intrigue, espionage, dark humor, and double-crosses, with a stellar cast that makes even the most murderous characters hard to resist.

If you thought things were looking bleak in Middle-earth at the end of Season 2, just wait. Amazon's spectacularly ambitious—and wildly expensive—prequel to J.R.R. Tolkien’s The Lord of the Rings returns for a third season, picking up five years after the fall of Eregion. Sauron (Charlie Vickers) has been busy building his armies and conquering much of Middle-earth, leaving the Elves, Dwarves, Men, and Wizards scrambling to stop him. Meanwhile, the Dark Lord is working on his most dangerous creation yet: the One Ring.

The new season promises plenty of political intrigue, epic battles, and the kind of jaw-dropping fantasy world-building that makes the series such a visual feast. Even if you know where Tolkien’s story ultimately leads, watching the pieces fall into place is half the fun. The first four episodes arrive November 11, with the remaining four rolling out over the following two weeks.

Remember when cryptocurrency was going to change the world? For a while, Sam Bankman-Fried seemed poised to make that happen. The young entrepreneur became a billionaire seemingly overnight as the founder of FTX, a cryptocurrency exchange that promised to revolutionize finance. Then it all came crashing down in spectacular fashion, exposing an $8 billion fraud that landed Bankman-Fried in prison for 25 years.

The Altruists dramatizes that astonishing rise and fall, with Anthony Boyle (Say Nothing) playing Bankman-Fried and Julia Garner (Ozark) portraying Caroline Ellison (no relation to Larry Ellison), his on-again, off-again girlfriend and the CEO of his trading firm, Alameda Research. Inspired by reporting from New York magazine, the eight-part limited series explores how two young idealists who embraced the philosophy of effective altruism—using wealth and resources to do the most good—became central figures in one of the biggest financial scandals in recent history. All eight episodes premiere November 19.

If Amy Poehler and Mike Schur taught us anything with Parks and Recreation, it’s that even the most mundane workplace can be a breeding ground for brilliant comedy. Their latest collaboration takes that idea to an archaeological dig in Greece.

Based on Kate Myers’ 2023 novel Excavations, the 10-episode comedy stars Poehler as Elise, an ambitious archaeologist who has spent years waiting for her boss (Hugh Laurie) to retire so she can take over the operation. When she and three fellow excavators (Geraldine Viswanathan, Antonia Thomas, and Fina Strazza) uncover a mysterious artifact that could rewrite history, they make the questionable decision to keep it secret. Naturally, things spiral out of control, drawing the women into an international conspiracy that threatens their careers—and possibly much more. With a cast that also includes What We Do in the Shadows’s Kayvan Novak (aka Nandor the Relentless), Dig delivers an entertaining mix of workplace comedy, historical mystery, and archaeological intrigue. All 10 episodes premiere November 23.

More than 40 years after Ridley Scott’s Blade Runner first introduced audiences to a dystopian Los Angeles, where synthetic humans are almost indistinguishable from the real thing, the franchise is still daring to ask science fiction’s most unsettling question. In this case: What makes us human?

Set 50 years after the events of Denis Villeneuve’s excellent Blade Runner 2049, this eight-part limited series stars Hunter Schafer (Euphoria) as Cora, a fugitive who assumes the identity of a Blade Runner, a special operative tasked with hunting down rogue Replicants. She’s forced to team up with Olwen (Michelle Yeoh), a Replicant whose days are numbered, to track down another fugitive whose secret could unravel the very foundations of their society. With artificial intelligence advancing at a dizzying pace in the real world, the franchise’s questions about consciousness, identity, and the increasingly blurry line between humans and machines feel more relevant than ever. All eight episodes arrive November 25.

How far would you go to find your missing child? For Dedo (Axel Stein) and Carlotta Klatt (Heike Makatsch), the answer is: very far. And well beyond the boundaries of what’s legal. When their teenage daughter, Wanda (Lea Drinda), disappears without a trace, the desperate German couple takes matters into their own hands, enlisting their tech-savvy son to help them secretly install surveillance equipment throughout their seemingly sleepy suburban neighborhood. What they discover is that practically everyone has something to hide.

The truth is still out there. More than three decades after The X-Files premiered, Chris Carter's groundbreaking sci-fi series remains one of television’s most addictive explorations of government conspiracies, alien encounters, and things that go bump in the night (or creepily squeeze through air vents). It also made pop culture icons of David Duchovny and Gillian Anderson as FBI agents Fox Mulder, a true believer in the paranormal, and Dana Scully, a scientist and skeptic whose job was ostensibly to debunk his theories. Anderson’s character also helped to inspire a whole generation of women in STEM, a phenomenon known as “The Scully Effect.”

The pair’s chemistry, and seemingly endless will-they-or-won’t-they relationship, helped sustain the show through 11 seasons (including a two-season revival beginning in 2016), two movies, and countless monsters of the week. With Ryan Coogler’s (Sinners, Black Panther) reboot, starring Danielle Deadwyler and Himesh Patel, recently receiving a series order at Hulu, now seems like the perfect time to revisit the original.

Twenty-five years ago this November, Kiefer Sutherland introduced the world to Jack Bauer, a counterterrorism agent who could save the country from almost certain destruction in a single day—but never seemed able to find five minutes to use the bathroom. Premiering just two months after 9/11, 24 became a defining television series of the post-9/11 era, with its real-time format, ticking digital clock, and seemingly endless cliff-hangers making it nearly impossible to watch just one episode.

Each season follows Bauer through a particularly hellish day as he races to thwart terrorist plots, uncover government conspiracies, and navigate an increasingly murky moral landscape. The Emmy-winning thriller also gave us one of television’s most recognizable phone ringtones (courtesy of the fictional Counter Terrorist Unit) and some truly bonkers subplots—including a memorable Season 2 encounter between Bauer’s daughter, Kim (Elisha Cuthbert), and a cougar. Yes, a cougar. Some of the show’s politics and attitudes toward torture haven’t aged particularly well, but its influence on modern action television is undeniable. All nine seasons are available to stream.

This acclaimed British prison drama anthology explores the lives of inmates and correctional officers, with each season tackling a different part of the criminal justice system. The first season starred Game of Thrones’ Sean Bean as a guilt-ridden prisoner and Adolescence’s Stephen Graham as a morally conflicted prison officer, while the second shifted to a women’s prison, with Thirteenth Doctor Jodie Whittaker and The Last of Us’s Bella Ramsey leading the cast.

The third installment stars Doctor Who icon David Tennant as a veteran officer at a juvenile detention facility, where a violent incident raises questions about accountability, rehabilitation, and the psychological toll of incarcerating teenagers. Siobhan Finneran (Downton Abbey) returns as a prison chaplain, the only character to appear in all three seasons.

#549. McDonald's stock rises as earnings beat, U.S. restaurants show improvement

McDonald's on Wednesday reported mixed quarterly results as traffic to its U.S. restaurants fell, leading to a slight decline in domestic same-store sales.

The fast-food giant reported a 0.2% decrease in sales at its U.S. restaurants that have been open at least 13 months. Even so, the segment performed better than the 0.5% decline that Wall Street anticipated, according to a survey of analysts by StreetAccount.

"In the U.S., we're encouraged by improvement as the quarter progressed following our intervention on value," McDonald's CEO Chris Kempczinski said in a statement.

McDonald's same-store sales returned to growth in September after falling in July and August, CFO Ian Borden said on the company's earnings conference call. However, in response to an analyst question, he clarified that he did not say that U.S. same-store sales would be better in the fourth quarter.

Kempczinski said in August that the company's U.S. business was falling short on execution, particularly on the implementation of its value offerings. Like many restaurants, McDonald's has leaned into discounts to attract budget-conscious diners over the last two years.

To reverse same-store sales declines, McDonald's focused its marketing on its Extra Value Meals and restarted regional and national promotions on its mobile app, which are important to its most frequent diners.

As McDonald's tries to regain its footing in the U.S., the chain is also testing two new value menus, one with mix and match bundles and the other with set combinations of menu items, executives said on the earnings call. The offering that resonates the most with customers and franchisees will be rolled out across the U.S. in early 2027.

Shares of the company closed more than 6% higher Wednesday.

Here's what the company reported compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

The company reported third-quarter net income of $2.49 billion, or $3.51 per share, up from $2.28 billion, or $3.18 per share, a year earlier.

Excluding restructuring charges and other items, McDonald's earned $3.57 per share.

The company's same-store sales rose 1.5%, lifted by its international segments. That topped analysts' expectations of a 1.3% increase, according to StreetAccount.

McDonald's international developmental licensed markets business, which includes Japan and China, reported same-store sales growth of 4.2%. Wall Street was forecasting same-store sales to rise 2.1% for the division, based on StreetAccount estimates.

The company's international operated division, which includes top markets like Australia and Germany, saw same-store sales growth of 1.4%. Still, the business underperformed Wall Street's expectations. Analysts were projecting same-store sales would rise 2.1%, according to StreetAccount.

"In the majority of our large international markets, we're holding or gaining share," Borden said on the company's conference call. "It's a soft consumer environment, but we're doing well."

France was a weak spot, McDonald's said. Kempczinski said that franchisees in France recently voted on a strategy to turn the business around. In addition to a softer economy in the country, McDonald's has also seen short-term disruptions related to student protests.

#544. Trump hammers Fox News, blaming network for GOP losses as ratings slide

President Donald Trump turned against Fox News on Tuesday night in a string of posts on Truth Social while Democratic wins rolled in during a historic midterm election.

Trump blamed the network, which he's long championed, for Republican losses, saying in one post on his social media network that it "bears responsibility for these losses by constantly and consistently putting on the Dumocrat opposition."

Democrats took control of both the Senate and House of Representatives, flipping seats in states usually favorable to Republicans, including Texas, South Carolina and Kansas.

Trump also pointed to Fox's recent ratings as evidence of the network's decline.

"FoxNews Ratings are way down (27%!) because they're spending too much time covering the Trump Deranged Dumocrats! All of those people who used to watch Fox, and no longer do, get it. It's them, not me," he posted.

Fox News did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

In a press release last week, Fox News reported 1.1 million average viewers for October, down about 27% from the same month a year prior.

Fox Corp. CEO Lachlan Murdoch noted the decline during the company's quarterly earnings call on Monday, citing "viewer fatigue."

However, Murdoch said such a shift is expected in the lead-up to a midterm election with a second-term president and said it was not the result of viewers turning to competitors' coverage.

"Those viewers are not going to any other news channel. They're not switching to MS NOW or to CNN," he said. "They're just fatigued and watching slightly less television overall."

Versant-owned MS NOW and Skydance-owned CNN both reported primetime viewership was up by double digits in October compared with the year-ago period. However, both networks still had smaller total-day average audiences than Fox News.

MS NOW had 588,000 average viewers in October across all day parts, and CNN had 402,000, according to Nielsen data cited by Fox.

Despite Fox News' year-over-year drop in viewership, the company reported an otherwise strong quarter in what Murdoch called an "outstanding start to fiscal 2027."

Fox Corp., which includes the linear TV networks as well as entertainment and sports assets and a streaming business, reported revenue for its fiscal first quarter of $4.57 billion, up 22% year over year.

The company pointed to strength in advertising revenue, which increased 47%, buoyed by strong political advertising and the FIFA Men's World Cup, which aired in part on Fox platforms across June and July.

Trump is a frequent critic of media companies, especially broadcasters that he views as being biased against him. While he's repeatedly taken aim at NBC and Disney-owned ABC, the president has had a much friendlier relationship with Fox and its networks.

Trump appeared on Fox News' "Hannity" on Monday night ahead of the election.

Disclosure: CNBC and MS NOW are divisions of Versant Media.

#536. Pokémon card drops and restocks: Best deals from Amazon, Walmart, TCGplayer

Finding Pokémon card stock at market price hasn’t become any easier in 2026. Of course, you can help your odds by knowing where to look.

When shopping for the 30th Celebration collection, the new Delta Reign expansion, or the Ultra Premium Collection, finding actual stock can be tough. That said, by making the most of important go-tos like Amazon’s invitation system, Best Buy’s own store-only listings, and the occasional restocks elsewhere, you can add to your collection without paying inflated prices.

These are the stores we'd keep bookmarked to have the best chance of getting Pokémon TCG’s new releases. Keep in mind that some of the best options for products are through third-party sellers:

Amazon — One of the more convenient places to find sealed Pokémon products, especially Booster Bundles, ETBs, and Booster Display Boxes. In fact, Amazon recently brought back its $59.99 invitation-only 30th Celebration ETB offer. Select ‘Request invite’ on the Amazon-sold listing; an invitation isn't guaranteed, and the price will likely revert to much higher third-party prices once stock runs out.

TCGplayer — One of the first places to check for preorders on brand new releases, single cards, and listings close to market price. The dedicated trading card site’s "market price" estimations are helpful to gauge whether you’re overpaying for a Pokémon TCG product in the modern market.

Walmart — Regularly carries newly released collections and older expansions, with an extensive selection of products from marketplace sellers, usually at lower prices than Amazon. Not only can you find the in-demand Pokémon TCG 30th Celebration Ultra Premium Collection Night Edition for under market price, but Walmart+ members can sometimes receive early access to restocks.

Best Buy — Among the better options for avoiding reseller markup, thanks to its Pokémon listings that are actually priced at MSRP. For instance, its Prismatic Evolutions ETB is listed at $49.99, although this is another example where you need to request an invite and hope to be randomly selected. Meanwhile, other products sold at MSRP are available only in physical stores. Even if you’re not able to visit in-person Best Buy stores, it's worth registering for online stock drops at MSRP whenever that option becomes available.

Gamestop — Carries the latest expansions alongside older Booster Bundles, Elite Trainer Boxes, and single packs, with some surprising deals that can undercut other sellers. Its Delta Reign Booster Bundle is currently listed at $49.99, with Pro members eligible for a reduced $47.49 price. The retailer's store availability tools are particularly useful for finding nearby stock.

Target — Another useful destination for new collections, particularly if nearby stores have inventory available for pickup. As for online, anything at or near MSRP does unfortunately sell out instantly, and anything readily available comes with a massively jacked-up price. Target has also begun experimenting with invitation systems for certain Pokémon products, including the Destined Rivals Three-Booster Blister, priced at $14.99.

Pokémon Center — The go-to for finding market price and initial drops — plus getting exclusive Pokémon Center Elite Trainer Box editions with additional booster packs and promotional cards. Thanks to the massive imbalance between demand and supply, stock runs out within minutes.

The core issue that’s been plaguing the Pokémon TCG for years comes down to overall demand far outstripping supply; almost any product sells out at MSRP instantly. Between demand from collectors, competitive players, and most importantly, resellers (with many using bot accounts), any preorders or stock drops for new and popular expansions can disappear within minutes of being listed.

The Pokémon Company is printing more cards than ever to help combat the problem. It produced 10 billion cards worldwide during its latest financial year, but even that hasn't been enough to satisfy demand.

That said, the higher volumes during the 30th Celebration have at least affected scalpers' profit margins. More have been available, giving resellers less justification to drive prices above market levels. Moving forward, the company will continue to tackle the shortages while increasing production and promising additional reprints. At the same time, retailers like Amazon, Best Buy, Walmart, and Target have introduced invitation systems and randomized drawings to reduce the impact of scalpers.

With competition still fierce for the most desirable Pokémon Trading Card Game collections, a little preparation can make the difference between paying MSRP and settling for whatever a reseller is asking:

Sign up for invitations and drawings: Register for Amazon and Best Buy invitations whenever available, and check Target and Walmart for new product draws. These don't guarantee a purchase, but they remove some of the pressure of competing against other shoppers during a live restock.

Check local stores: Best Buy, GameStop, Target, and Walmart can all be worth visiting in person. Online stock may have disappeared, but that doesn't automatically mean every physical location has sold out.

Compare prices before buying: Use TCGplayer to establish the market price for whatever you're looking at, then compare it to the original MSRP.

Follow Pokémon restock trackers: Accounts like @PokemonDealsTCG and @pokemondealsuk on X (formerly Twitter) can help flag new drops, especially when the Pokémon Center opens its virtual queue. Plus, turning on notifications could save you from refreshing multiple store pages throughout the day.

Check who is actually selling the product: Amazon and Walmart regularly feature external sellers, so inspect the seller's details, shipping costs, and estimated delivery dates before committing to a purchase. Third-party sellers are fine to use if they’re selling at around market price and if their rating’s strong.

With Black Friday arriving on Nov. 27, there's a good chance that retailers like Amazon, Walmart, GameStop, Best Buy, and Target will have more Pokémon TCG stock available during their holiday sales.

However, there's currently no guarantee that high-demand products like the 30th Celebration Elite Trainer Box or Delta Reign collections will get additional inventory. If there is, these will very likely be very limited, invite-only drops — some even at MSRP if you’re lucky.

Instead, keep checking retailer listings throughout November, especially for older expansions like Perfect Order, Chaos Rising, and Pitch Black, which could see more competitive prices during the sales. It’s usually at these types of shopping events that third-party sellers undercut each other to clear old stock. Any discounts will sell out quickly, so be sure to keep Mashable’s Black Friday buyer’s guides bookmarked throughout the event.

#535. CVS to split off drugstore business in major breakup of healthcare giant; shares fall 10%

CVS Health plans to split into two separate public companies by the first half 2028 in what would be one of the largest corporate breakups in history, the company announced Wednesday.

The move would reverse the company's massive $78 billion acquisition of its insurer, Aetna, and dismantle the vertically integrated healthcare conglomerate it created in 2018. The plan would specifically separate CVS' roughly 9,000 retail pharmacies from its major insurance business and pharmacy benefit manager, Caremark, one of the nation's three largest PBMs.

The decision follows a "comprehensive strategic review" by the company's board of directors, according to a news release. Shares of CVS closed more than 12% lower on Wednesday.

It's a stunning reversal for the healthcare powerhouse, which has spent the last two decades working to turn itself into a one-stop health destination. The move also comes as CVS' aggressive turnaround plan under CEO David Joyner shows substantial progress, with the company pursuing $2 billion in cost cuts, store closures, leadership shake-ups and efforts to rein in Medicare Advantage costs.

But Joyner told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" on Wednesday that the decision to split is "out of a position of strength," pointing to recovery in Aetna's Medicare business and solid performance in the retail pharmacy.

"So it's all about the strength of the business that affords us the opportunity to make what we think are the right strategic decisions for the future of this company," he later added.

CVS shares have jumped roughly 70% since Joyner took the helm in October 2024, bringing the company's market value to $117 billion.

The two new companies would both be publicly traded.

The insurance and PBM company will retain the CVS Health name and be run by Joyner, while the other company will be called CVS Pharmacy and be led by Prem Shah, who is currently group president of CVS Health. Additional leadership positions and board members will be announced at a later date, CVS said.

Existing CVS shareholders will own stock in both companies after the split. The Financial Times on Tuesday first reported the breakup plans.

The company's pharmacy business raked in $37.66 billion in 2025 sales, while its insurance unit booked $36.29 billion in revenue and the healthcare services segment generated $51.24 billion.

CVS on Wednesday also posted third-quarter earnings and revenue that topped Wall Street expectations. Total sales climbed to $108.40 billion, from $102.87 billion in the prior-year period.

Sales were boosted by year-over-year growth in the company's healthcare benefits and health services segments. Meanwhile, revenue in its pharmacy services business slid from the previous year.

On Wednesday, CVS said it will lump its insurer and PBM with its primary care business Oak Street Health, home visit provider Signify Health and the company's specialty drug business.

CVS also said the split will allow the two companies to experience a "focused strategic agenda" and greater ability to "innovate, adopt technology, increase efficiency," among other benefits.

It's not the first time CVS has weighed splitting the company. In October 2024, CVS engaged advisors in a strategic review of its business and considered breaking up its retail pharmacy and insurance units.

But Joyner told CNBC that the company was "in a turnaround" at that time without the flexibility, cash position or business performance to execute a split.

In an interview, Leerink Partners analyst Michael Cherny said the split shows "the stability of the pharmacy," which has won market share from competitors and is seeing gross margins stabilize thanks to CostVantage, a new pharmacy reimbursement model introduced by CVS last year that the company bills as more transparent.

"This business is a much different business from the one that's been considered three years ago, five years ago, 10 years ago, where there had been a litany of headwinds that the pharmacy just couldn't overcome," he told CNBC on Wednesday.

One potential concern for investors is whether the retail pharmacy will lose its competitive advantage of being directly linked with a PBM, which negotiates drug prices and manages prescription drug benefits on behalf of insurers and employers.

But Joyner told analysts Wednesday that the synergy between the two businesses "are going to be preserved in commercial agreements between the two organizations," adding that "we don't need the integration of an operating structure."

Cherny said the rationale for the split could also extend to the state legislative scrutiny over the company's level of vertical integration.

The potential breakup also comes as pharmacy benefit managers face mounting bipartisan scrutiny in Washington, D.C., and across states. Republicans in Tennessee and Arkansas have passed laws that would ban co-ownership of PBMs and pharmacies. Those have been challenged in court by CVS and other companies.

PBMs have been criticized for opaque pricing practices, their growing market power and potential conflicts of interest when they are owned by companies that also operate insurers and pharmacies.

#534. Waymo Said It Fixed Its School Bus Problem. The US Government Says It Didn’t Work

The US government is examining whether Waymo’s recall of software involved with a series of school bus passing incidents last fall actually fixed the safety issue.

The formal investigation by the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration will determine whether the company’s supposed software fix succeeded in preventing its robotaxis from improperly passing stopped school buses with red lights on and their stop arms extended—indicators that the buses were picking up and dropping off students. It is illegal to pass these stopped school buses in all 50 states.

According to a filing, Waymo issued a fix for the software issue in mid-November and a voluntary recall a month later. But the government says that the majority of school bus issues occurred after that date.

Earlier this year, a report from the National Transportation Safety Board, the US’s independent safety agency, found that another school bus passing incident occurred in mid-January, several weeks after the supposed software fix. The NTSB is also investigating the school bus passing issue.

In a written statement, Waymo spokesperson Dana Gallagher said the company has “made significant improvements to vehicle behavior around school buses” and that it has not been cited for passing school buses since March—months after the mid-December software recall. She wrote that Waymo looks “forward to continuing [its] engagement on recall processes for AV software that continues to learn and improve.”

Late last year, WIRED reported, the Austin, Texas, school district even helped set up a half-day data collection event for Waymo’s vehicles, coordinating with the self-driving vehicle developer to bring school buses and stop-arm signals to an empty parking lot so that the company could collect information to help train its vehicles to avoid the illegal street maneuver. The data collection event occurred after Waymo had issued its software fix and voluntary recall. Still, the incidents continued.

In years past, recall queries like this one have led to civil penalties, some of them costing automakers and software developers tens of millions of dollars.

#531. Want to live and work on the moon? NASA is hiring.

Ever looked at the moon and thought, man, I'd love to work there? Well, NASA is hiring.

Announced over the weekend, applications are now open for the next batch of astronauts who will train specifically to work and live on the lunar surface.

The U.S. space agency is looking for the next class to be trained at NASA's Johnson Space Center in Houston, those "who will join NASA as America returns to the Moon, builds the Moon Base, and lays the groundwork for the first crewed missions to Mars." Applications close 11:59 p.m. ET Dec. 3 and NASA will make its selections in late 2027.

Saturday's announcement came with a "NASA wants you" recruitment video, featuring NASA astronauts Jessica Meir, Andre Douglas, Deniz Burnham, Randy Bresnik, Kayla Barron, Jack Hathaway, and Chris Williams.

Notably, remote work isn't available for astronauts, and relocation fees to Houston may be covered by NASA. But the salary is out of this world: between $164,395 to $164,395 per year.

Of course, you can't just spacewalk into a job on the moon. To apply, you'll need to be a U.S. citizen with a master's degree in a STEM (science, technology, engineering, and mathematics) field from an accredited institution. If you don't have the latter, NASA will also accept currently enrolled or completed Test Pilot School students (who also have a STEM bachelor's degree), people with medical degrees with professional experience or completed residency training, or people who have completed two years of a STEM-based doctoral program. Candidates also need at least three post-degree years in their field.

Applying pilots also need to have considerable flight experience and pilot-in-command time whether in high-performance jet, rotary-wing, or other fix-wing aircrafts.

Along with a dazzling CV, applicants will need to upload five references (not letters of recommendation), all STEM-related academic transcripts, and if necessary, a completed pilot qualifications form. Candidates can then track their applications online.

It's been two years since NASA opened astronaut applications in 2024, from which 10 candidates made it through from over 8,000 applications. Nicknamed "The Platypi," the class of 2026 are currently training at Johnson Space Center. When their training's complete, they'll join the 32 active astronauts at NASA in missions and tasks like spacecraft development and maintenance (including those all-important spacewalks), research experiments, Mission Control ground support, and more. Who knows? A future Artemis mission could be on your CV.

"This is an extraordinary time to join NASA’s astronaut corps," said NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman in a press statement. "The people we select could walk on the Moon, live and work at the Moon Base, and help pioneer the systems and operations that will ultimately carry crews to Mars."

So, if you think you meet NASA's requirements (and I certainly don't), you can apply to be an astronaut via the U.S. government's job website.

#527. Jensen Huang’s Troubles Leave the AI Industry Reeling

No executive has pulled the strings in the artificial intelligence era quite like Nvidia’s CEO, Jensen Huang. He parlayed the boom into incredible wealth, became a close confidant of President Donald Trump, and emerged as a prominent voice in US–China relations. That now all stands in the balance.

Over the past few days, Huang’s status as an AI titan has come under scrutiny amid an admission that he had an affair with Alice Huang, an executive at a Singapore-based company whom US intelligence services concluded was a Chinese intelligence asset.

Nvidia’s board of directors said the company would launch an investigation into the incident. Huang, the company said, “fully supports the review and has committed to cooperating with it.” Huang earned $36 million last year and owned about 3.5 percent of Nvidia as of March, according to a corporate filing—which also noted that Huang is an at-will employee who can be let go anytime without severance.

How exactly the investigation shakes out, and what it means for Huang’s future at the company, remains to be seen. But the incident at least puts his position at risk, and raises questions about how the industry will respond.

“He’s the person who decides who gets the chips and who doesn't,” says David Yoffie, a professor at Harvard Business School who tracks the chip industry.

Nvidia, which Huang cofounded in 1993, controls 90 percent of the market for chips used in training frontier AI models. With those chips in short supply, Huang has the power to determine which areas of AI get a boost next. Nvidia has also bankrolled much of the AI buildout, investing billions in the very companies buying its chips, such as OpenAI and Anthropic. When Elon Musk wants to build a new data center, Yoffie says, he can put in a call to Huang.

Internally, Huang closely manages even small details at Nvidia, responds to emails from any one of its roughly 60,000 staffers, and raises his voice to get points across, according to current and former employees. He’s a regular on cable TV and at tech conferences, where he’s been trying to deflate concerns about an AI market bubble and boost the positioning of his company.

But Nvidia has no other executive anywhere close to Huang’s prominence, and no apparent potential successor. “We have no idea who would replace him,” Yoffie says. “You put someone new into that position, if you're a customer, who knows what that's going to mean.”

Ryan Fedasiuk, a senior fellow at the think tank Foundation for American Innovation, says Huang has played such an important role—and become so close to the president—that he has become load-bearing for the entire US AI industry.

“On the one hand, Huang is irreplaceable. He built Nvidia into the juggernaut it is today, and he was the visionary behind the GPU, which has sustained the intelligence revolution,” Fedasiuk says. But he says that the company, which is indispensable to the AI industry, has to come first.

“He has to be replaceable as the CEO of the world's largest company, at $4 trillion in capex.”

Nvidia shares fell more than 4 percent on Monday, even as broader stock market indices rose.

The board’s investigation will most likely focus on whether Huang violated any company policies, Yoffie says. But the FBI could also investigate whether laws were broken, and Congress may decide to look into whether Huang’s affair influenced the US government’s policy on selling chips to China.

Nvidia didn’t immediately respond to WIRED’s request for comment.

The scandal also raises serious questions about whether China may have gained sensitive information about US AI companies and the government’s strategy on China, Fedasiuk says.

Huang’s affair appears especially damning given how outspoken he has been in recent years against calls for more aggressive technology export controls on China. His credibility on the topic could be permanently in doubt.

Both the first Trump administration and the Biden administration imposed export controls designed to limit China’s access to Nvidia’s chips, as well as to technology required to mint advanced semiconductors. In his second term, however, Trump loosened these controls, giving China access to older generations of Nvidia chips; it was better to make China reliant on US technology, the argument went.

Sam Bresnick, a research fellow at Georgetown University’s Center for Security and Emerging Technology, says the Huang scandal is likely to accelerate efforts to crack down on chip exports.

“There was going to be more of an impetus to control what chips are getting sent to China anyway,” he says. “I expect there to be more scrutiny on what's going on, what's getting sent to China. I think there's also going to be more scrutiny from the Commerce Department.”

#525. Meta announces global roll out of its age estimation system

Meta announced Monday that it will roll out its age-estimation technology globally. The move puts millions more teens around the world into Teen Accounts settings on Instagram, the company said in a blog post.

In April 2025, Meta began using AI technology to detect teens on Instagram, even if they'd used an adult age upon opening an account. Once identified, teens are proactively put into Meta's safest settings, which are designed to limit inappropriate content.

This month, parents in the U.S. will begin seeing a video in their Instagram feed explaining how teens are placed in age-appropriate protections.

"We’ll continue to do our part to proactively ensure teens are in age-appropriate experiences on our apps," the blog post said.

Still, Meta has spent the year under fire for its ability to protect young users.

In March, the company lost a case brought by the state of New Mexico, which alleged that Meta's design features helped pedophiles sexually exploit children on Meta platforms. A jury found Meta liable for misleading consumers about endangering children and the safety of its platforms. Meta, which said it would fight the decision, was ordered to pay nearly $950 billion in related penalties.  The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile Products available for purchase through affiliate links. If you buy something through links on our site, Mashable may earn an affiliate commission.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

In August, Meta made a $12 billion settlement with dozens of state attorneys general in a trial over whether the social media company had designed addictive platforms.

As part of the settlement, Meta agreed to implement new platform design features and recommit to industry-standard child safety measures, including daily time limits, screen time options like night mode, and stronger age assurance measures. The company also called on competitors TikTok, Snapchat, and YouTube to agree to the settlement terms, at which time Meta would pay another $5 billion. On Monday, Meta again asked TikTok and YouTube to adopt the same practices.

"We’ve set a strong standard for age assurance, but unless other companies follow suit, teens remain inconsistently protected online, defeating the purpose of these laws," wrote Meta.

Meta's proposed fixes were met with general approval from online child safety groups, despite continued criticism of the company's Teen Accounts.

Months prior to the settlement, Meta expanded Teen Accounts to all Meta platform users globally. At the time, the tech giant explained it was exploring additional ways to reinforce age-appropriate content moderation and repetitive, personalized algorithms.

Meanwhile, governments around the world have begun cracking down on social media access for teens, including Australia's complete social media ban for users under 16. Other countries — as well as some U.S. states — have passed laws reinforcing age-gaited platforms and requiring social media platforms enact age assurance measures. Last week, Meta sued the California attorney general in order to block a new law designed to reduce youth social media addiction.

Meta said that its services "collectively enable billions of people around the world to share ideas, offer support, [and] discuss important topics." The company argued that the law, set to get into effect in January, would "make it unlawful for Meta to disseminate speech to minors altogether."

#524. New Flutter CEO bets on FanDuel promotions to win customers as profits suffer

Flutter's new CEO Dan Taylor is counting on better products and more generous customer rewards to help convert FanDuel's improving engagement into stronger profits.

Taylor's been at the helm for just five weeks and issued his first earnings report as CEO on Wednesday, with third-quarter revenue and adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization missing Wall Street's expectations.

The company's stock is down more than 60% year to date.

Yet Taylor says the company is now on the right track.

"We made some changes to the product," Taylor told CNBC in an interview. "Fundamentally, the way you win this business is by delighting customers, delivering the very best experiences and delivering to them in the way that they want, with the right overall proposition."

FanDuel also launched a nationwide rewards program in September that Taylor said has "resonated really, really well."

The early customer numbers are improving. Average monthly players on FanDuel's U.S. sportsbook rose 9% in the third quarter, while bet counts increased 15%, Taylor said.

Flutter's earnings release showed total U.S. average monthly players up 12%, including 24% growth in iGaming. Sportsbook handle, or the total amount wagered, rose 13%, but sportsbook revenue grew just 4% as promotional spending increased.

Taylor said the company plans to keep investing in customer experience. He said Flutter sees room to improve its structural margins over time by changing the mix of products customers use.

Flutter has increased its media spend to build FanDuel Predicts, its predictions platform to rival Kalshi and Polymarket, but the cost of sales as a percent of revenue is forecast to be 58% for the full-year 2026 and into 2027.

Taylor said there's fierce industry competition for customers but even still, FanDuel trading volume has skyrocketed 271% since July.

The FanDuel Predicts app added 140,000 new customers in October, Taylor said.

Taylor also defended the long-term opportunity in the U.S., pointing out about half the country still does not have access to regulated sports betting and 90% lacks regulated online gaming.

He argued that expanding legal markets is not a partisan issue because regulated betting offers consumer protections and gives tax revenue to states.

For the third quarter, Flutter reported an adjusted loss of 18 cents per share, compared with analysts' expectations for a loss of 28 cents, according to LSEG. Revenue rose 2% to $3.87 billion, below the roughly $4 billion LSEG consensus, while adjusted EBITDA fell roughly 25% year over year to $360 million, also below expectations.

The company posted a net loss of $1.15 billion, including an $873 million non-cash impairment tied primarily to Brazil, which banned sports betting and online gaming in September.

Taylor said the company was disappointed by Brazil's provisional measure, but took the impairment to "set ourselves up to win long-term."

The complications in Brazil and unfavorable sports results in the U.S. and internationally led Flutter to cut its full-year revenue midpoint by $300 million to $17.61 billion and lower its 2026 adjusted EBITDA outlook by $160 million to nearly $2.5 billion.

In the meantime, capital returns are on hold: Taylor said Flutter needs to bring leverage back into its target range of 2 to 2.5 times over roughly the next 24 months before it can consider buybacks again. For now, he said, the priority is organic growth and paying down debt.

— CNBC's Mallory Leonard contributed to this report.

#521. NYT Connections hints today: Clues, answers for November 9, 2026

The NYT Connections puzzle today is not too difficult if you talk a good game.

Connections is the one of the most popular New York Times word games that's captured the public's attention. The game is all about finding the "common threads between words." And just like Wordle, Connections resets after midnight and each new set of words gets trickier and trickier—so we've served up some hints and tips to get you over the hurdle.

If you just want to be told today's puzzle, you can jump to the end of this article for today's Connections solution. But if you'd rather solve it yourself, keep reading for some clues, tips, and strategies to assist you.

The NYT's latest daily word game has become a social media hit. The Times credits associate puzzle editor Wyna Liu with helping to create the new word game and bringing it to the publications' Games section. Connections can be played on both web browsers and mobile devices and require players to group four words that share something in common.

Each puzzle features 16 words and each grouping of words is split into four categories. These sets could comprise of anything from book titles, software, country names, etc. Even though multiple words will seem like they fit together, there's only one correct answer.

If a player gets all four words in a set correct, those words are removed from the board. Guess wrong and it counts as a mistake—players get up to four mistakes until the game ends.

Players can also rearrange and shuffle the board to make spotting connections easier. Additionally, each group is color-coded with yellow being the easiest, followed by green, blue, and purple. Like Wordle, you can share the results with your friends on social media.

#518. A New AI Tool From Google DeepMind Proved the Signal Protocol Is Secure

Google DeepMind researchers used agentic AI to mathematically prove, or “formally verify,” a number of the Signal protocol’s key cryptographic guarantees—a landmark development for the widely used cryptography and AI itself.

Conducted as a sort of proof-of-concept project in just four weeks, the work is significant in two ways. It serves as further independent validation of the cryptography that protects massive quantities of the world's communications on platforms like the Signal app, Google Messages, and WhatsApp. And it demonstrates Google DeepMind's formal verification capabilities using a new AI framework called AlphaProof Code.

More broadly, AI-aided formal verification is having a moment, but it is unclear how practical it will be for widespread use and how effective it can be at securing large amounts of code. Proponents of formal verification have long referred to it as a sort of holy grail in software security, because it uses math to check and validate that software is functioning as intended—in other words, that it doesn't have critical vulnerabilities.

Before the AI era, it was incredibly time-consuming for people to describe the characteristics of software they wanted to prove, turn these summaries into formulas, and then develop the proof. Agentic AI can significantly cut down the time of the latter project, enabling analyses like DeepMind's Signal evaluation. But even this analysis was limited in scope—it did not, for example, formally verify the entire Signal mobile app—and DeepMind researchers emphasize that their findings are just one milestone in a larger process.

“We are very excited by this result, but there is a long distance to go before we are able to scale this to all the software of the world, and we want to do that,” Swarat Chaudhuri, a DeepMind research scientist, tells WIRED.

The DeepMind analysis did not find any new vulnerabilities in the portions of the Signal protocol it looked at, but when the researchers used AlphaProof Code on older versions of Signal that contain known and long-fixed vulnerabilities, it correctly identified them and showed that the protocol could not be formally verified. The researchers are releasing the specification and proof they developed for the Signal protocol analysis to aid other formal verification work.

While the speed of the project was notable, the DeepMind researchers point out that they were able to get a head start to a degree because of some past formal verification work that the Signal Foundation has conducted.

For critical services used in high-stakes settings, it would be a dream to be able to formally verify every code base and entire software supply chains. “If you can now verify the correctness of these implementations, then you can rest easy,” says Pushmeet Kohli, chief scientist at Google Cloud and DeepMind's vice president of research. “You can imagine banks, imagine all those other companies who rely on assurances and who worry about bugs who could now say, ‘I can prove these properties.’"

The open question across the tech sector, though, is whether AI-automated formal verification can truly scale—and whether it can do so at a workable cost. DeepMind conducted the Signal analysis free of charge as a research project, and Google declined to share the cost of the analysis. The company also declined to comment on whether it has plans to release a commercial formal verification product or service.

The researchers say that some of their crucial advancements are in developing a “human-AI collaboration process,” as Chaudhuri puts it, that allows agents to contribute and self-correct at almost every phase of the process while still seeking human input and review at key junctures. “We don’t want this sort of vibe world where AI is generating the goals and AI is generating the proofs and basically taking tests that are self-written," Chaudhuri says. “There’s a middle ground.”

#509. CVS Health plans to split itself up. Here's why a breakup could make sense now

CVS Health plans to break itself up — and this time, the bet looks a lot more viable.

The healthcare conglomerate on Wednesday announced plans to split into two publicly traded companies by the first half of 2028. CVS would separate its pharmacy business – including roughly 9,000 retail locations – from its major insurance business and pharmacy benefit manager, Caremark.

The move comes more than two years after CVS considered a breakup but ultimately walked away from the idea. At the time, analysts questioned whether the retail pharmacy business could survive on its own.

“Back then, the idea of retail standing alone was unthinkable,” Brian Tanquilut, senior equity research analyst at Jefferies, told CNBC.

But the economics of the businesses have changed: CVS's retail pharmacy business has become more consistently profitable, while its insurer, Aetna, has improved its performance despite persistently high medical costs in Medicare Advantage plans. That could make it easier for the company to separate the units — and for investors to recognize the value of each on its own, rather than valuing them together as one sprawling healthcare conglomerate.

“For us, it's showing the stability of the pharmacy,” Michael Cherny, senior research analyst at Leerink Partners, said in an interview. “This business is a much different business from the one that's been considered three years ago, five years ago, 10 years ago, where there had been a litany of headwinds that the pharmacy just couldn't overcome.”

CVS CEO David Joyner told CNBC’s "Squawk on the Street" on Wednesday that back in 2024, the company was "in a turnaround" without the flexibility, cash or business performance to execute a split. Now, CVS has posted eight consecutive quarters of beating Wall Street earnings estimates and has raised its guidance several times over the last two years, thanks to improvement across its PBM, pharmacy and insurer.

Since taking the helm in 2024, Joyner has pursued an aggressive turnaround plan that involves $2 billion in cost cuts, store closures, leadership shakeups and efforts to rein in Medicare Advantage costs.

The company also now expects to generate at least $15.5 billion in operating cash flow in 2026, a roughly 70% increase from $9.1 billion in 2024, and says it has reached its leverage target ahead of schedule.

"What's changed in two years is the fact that we now can look at this business and the assets that are assembled and really determine, without any distress or without anything that we have to do, what is the right strategic decision to make," Joyner told analysts on a call Wednesday, adding that the decision comes "out of strength."

While investors have largely cheered CVS's progress in recent years, Wall Street appeared more pessimistic about Wednesday's announcement, as the company's shares dropped about 12%.

CVS's retail business has benefited from changes in the competitive landscape, including the widespread retrenchment and store closures of Walgreens, Rite Aid and other pharmacy operators over the last few years. That’s created opportunities for CVS to gain market share, some analysts told CNBC.

CVS has also introduced changes to its pharmacy reimbursement model. Its CostVantage program is designed to replace the traditional process with a more transparent, cost-based model. Some analysts say that has helped the company better align reimbursement with the cost of dispensing prescriptions and contributed to steadier profits.

Those developments represent a meaningful shift for a business that has faced years of pressure from declining prescription reimbursement, store closures and competition from online pharmacies and other retailers.

That doesn't mean the retail pharmacy has become a high-growth business. CVS's retail pharmacy segment remains a relatively low-margin operation, with adjusted operating margins of roughly 4% to 5%. But analysts say it may now be stable and profitable enough to operate independently – even if investors value it at a discount to CVS's other businesses.

In a note Wednesday, RBC Capital Markets analyst Ben Hendrix said the lower valuation investors assign to the retail pharmacy unit has weighed on CVS's overall value and obscured the earnings strength of businesses like Aetna and Caremark.

The underlying growth of the insurance and PBM businesses is also stronger than that of retail pharmacy, Tanquilut said, after accounting for changes to Caremark's rebate arrangements.

Aetna in particular has gotten a better handle on medical costs by exiting unprofitable markets, expanding value-based care models, and tightening utilization management.

The breakup also comes as Caremark faces mounting scrutiny over PBM practices, including state-level legal and regulatory challenges involving the businesses and their relationships with pharmacies.

Republicans in Tennessee and Arkansas have passed laws that would ban any one company from owning both a pharmacy and a PBM — thought the Arkansas measure has been held up in court. Cherny noted that legal hurdles involving PBMs in other states have continued to pile up.

“Through what seems to be a never-ending litany of state lawsuits, it feels like it's been under more pressure,” Cherny said. “It's unclear if it just became, for lack of a better term, too tenuous to move forward with those two entities combined as one. I’m not sure.”

Even so, Joyner told the Wall Street Journal that the company wasn’t splitting up because of regulatory issues. CVS has said the separation follows a strategic review and is intended to give each business greater focus and flexibility.

The move also marks a significant departure from the integrated healthcare strategy CVS spent years building.

CVS spent roughly $70 billion to acquire Aetna in 2018 and argued that combining health insurance, pharmacy benefit management, retail pharmacies and health providers under one roof would help coordinate care and create a competitive advantage. CVS was still promoting that model as recently as its investor day last year.

The proposed split suggests the company now sees more value in separating at least some of those businesses – though it is not abandoning vertical integration altogether.

Aetna and Caremark will remain together in the healthcare company, alongside CVS’s specialty pharmacy, its primary care clinic Oak Street Health and its home-visit provider Signify Health. The separate pharmacy company will include roughly 9,000 retail stores, mail pharmacy operations and MinuteClinic, a retail medical clinic located inside certain CVS locations.

Both sides are large enough that they can stand on their own, Cherny said. Caremark is one of the country's three largest PBMs, while CVS has one of the largest retail pharmacy footprints in the U.S.

But separating the company raises questions about how much of the commercial relationship between the businesses can survive.

The relationship has historically benefited both sides. As it stands, Caremark can use CVS's extensive retail footprint to offer members convenient access to prescriptions and steer them toward CVS locations through preferred pharmacy arrangements. Programs such as Maintenance Choice, for example, give eligible members the option to fill certain maintenance medications at CVS retail pharmacies or through mail order.

Those arrangements also helped direct prescription volume to CVS's stores, giving the retail pharmacy unit an important source of customers. Because the businesses shared a parent company, the financial benefits of that relationship ultimately went to CVS as a whole.

In a note, RBC's Hendrix also raised questions about how the split could impact Aetna's Medicare Advantage quality ratings, which determine whether health plans qualify for bonus payments from the government. Retail pharmacists have played a role in engaging Aetna members, and a separation could complicate that relationship.

But Joyner told analysts Wednesday that the synergy between the two businesses would be "preserved in commercial agreements between the two organizations," adding that "we don't need the integration of an operating structure."

Joyner said CVS will be working on those potential agreements over the next 15 to 18 months.

Preferred pharmacy networks are common across the industry, and separate ownership does not prevent Caremark from continuing to favor CVS pharmacies through negotiated arrangements, Cherny said. The difference is that under separate ownership, the profits from those arrangements would no longer accrue to a single company, he noted.

“The issue is not whether the companies can continue working together in some capacity, but whether any future contractual arrangements preserve the SAME competitive advantages and flexibility as today under common ownership,” Cherny said in an afternoon note on Wednesday.

Still, both businesses face challenges on their own.

Tanquilut cautioned that some of CVS's recent retail pharmacy gains have come as Walgreens and other competitors have closed stores, raising questions about whether the growth can be sustained. The business also remains exposed to consumer pressure and competition from online pharmacies.

Caremark, meanwhile, faces continued regulatory scrutiny and potential headwinds from changes to the federal 340B drug discount program, which requires drugmakers to offer discounted medications to eligible hospitals and other healthcare providers serving low-income and vulnerable patients.

CVS participates in the program by providing pharmacy services to those organizations, but tweaks to manufacturer data-sharing policies and potential reimbursement changes could weigh on that business in 2027, Tanquilut said.

Aetna, meanwhile, is still working through a multiyear plan to rebuild its margins, as it looks to improve the profitability of its Medicare Advantage business.

Ultimately, CVS is betting that the value of letting investors assess its businesses separately will outweigh the benefits of keeping them under one roof. The split will test whether each can thrive independently.

#506. Elon Musk insists Cybertruck demand is strong as Tesla scraps it

Tesla Cybertruck is dead. The massive, steel-plated electric pickup has never sold particularly well, and Tesla CEO Elon Musk said last week that Cybertruck production will wind down next year.

Musk would like the world to know, however, that the company isn't scrapping Cybertruck due to unprofitability or lack of demand.

"The Cybertruck order rate far exceeds our production rate. We will try to make as many as possible before shutting down the line in about 8 months, but will have to return deposits for a lot of people," wrote Musk in a tweet on Monday. "This is not a case of Cybertruck being unprofitable, as has been misreported by legacy media, but rather that the opportunity cost of the complexity of Cybertruck is vastly outweighed by the value of incremental Cybercab production," he wrote.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

That's an interesting logical hoop Musk would like you to jump through. Given that Tesla doesn't report precise Cybertruck sales numbers, and typically doesn't talk about profitability of individual models, it's hard for an outsider to definitively call this or that model unprofitable. We've never said it, by the way; we merely pointed to poor sales figures, based on reports and Tesla's own figures, which lump Cybertruck with "other models" in quarterly reports.

But how do we know the sales figures are bad? Well, we merely compare Musk's predictions with actual sales numbers. In 2023, Musk predicted Tesla would sell 250,000 to 500,000 Cybertruck units annually. In the third quarter of 2026, Tesla delivered 8,295 "other models," which include Cybertruck, Model X, and Model S. The total deliveries for this category in the past four quarters are 48,431; even if all those were Cybertrucks (which they definitely weren't), that's still less than a fifth of Musk's lower projected figure for Cybertruck annual sales.

So yes: Cybertruck isn't selling nearly as well as Musk predicted. The company is placing all bets on autonomous driving, and has scrapped Model S, Model X, and Cybertruck while ramping up Cybercab production. Musk is free to call the Cybertruck a great victory, but we bet the company wouldn't be winding down production so easily had the demand been five or ten times bigger than what it was.

#505. E.l.f. Beauty posts 51% sales increase, raises full-year guidance

E.l.f. Beauty on Wednesday blew past Wall Street expectations as it posted a 51% increase in net sales in its second fiscal quarter.

The beauty company said it saw its 31st consecutive quarter of net sales growth, attributing the streak to strong performance across its portfolio. The revenue spike was driven by both retail and e-commerce channels across the U.S. and internationally, the company added.

"The thing I'm most pleased about is we saw strength across our entire portfolio," CEO Tarang Amin told CNBC in an interview. "Every one of our brands contributed strong growth this quarter."

Amin added that E.l.f. is the most-searched brand on TikTok Shop and the No. 2 brand in cosmetics on the platform. The company also said it saw strong results for Rhode, the skincare brand it acquired last year.

The company raised its full fiscal-year guidance following the better-than-anticipated quarter. It now expects net sales in the range of $2 billion to $2.03 billion, compared with a previous range of between $1.94 billion and $1.97 billion. It also upped its adjusted EBITDA projection to a range of $421 million to $427 million from between $401 million and $407 million.

E.l.f. said it now expects adjusted earnings per share for the year to come in between $3.82 and $3.86, exceeding prior guidance of between $3.50 and $3.55 per share. It's also expecting a net income range of $231 million to $233 million, up from a previous range of $212 million to $215 million.

It had already raised its guidance last quarter after outperforming its expectations.

Amin said the company is "very bullish" on the holiday season, expecting high sales through the end of the year.

Here's how the company performed in its second fiscal quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

For the period ended Sept. 30, E.l.f. reported net income of $30.03 million, or 50 cents per share, up significantly from roughly $3 million, or 5 cents per share, the year prior.

E.l.f. also saw significant growth in its international channels, with business up 165%. Amin told CNBC that only 25% of the company's business is outside of the U.S. and that it has a "tremendous opportunity" to build on that momentum.

The company also said its gross margin increased 1.6% during the quarter, including a 0.3% benefit from tariff refunds. Amin previously said the retailer planned to fully reinvest that money into pricing and marketing to continue driving growth.

Though E.l.f. received most of its refunds in its first fiscal quarter, some of those funds flowed through the second quarter, and the company expects to receive a yearly total of around $55 million. Amin told CNBC on Wednesday that the company lowered the price of about 10% of its units by $1 because of the tariff benefits.

"We're seeing great unit momentum based on that," he said.

#500. Consumers are spending on physical gifts even as travel tops wish lists, Visa survey finds

Consumers are hoping for more travel and experiences this holiday season, but physical gifts continue to win the lion's share of their wallets, according to a new report from Visa on Thursday.

The report found that more than half of survey respondents are putting travel and live events at the top of their wish lists. Still, only 15% of those respondents are prioritizing spending on experiences for others, while 27% of them plan to favor physical gifts.

That gap between what shoppers want and what they plan to buy is largely due to higher travel costs, lower consumer sentiment and an emphasis on value, according to Michael Brown, Visa's principal U.S. economist.

"There is some interesting trade-offs there that we're seeing on the part of consumers," Brown told CNBC. "If I'm traveling more, I'm probably going to be gifting those physical goods less, so this is really the broader context."

The survey was conducted among roughly 4,660 U.S. adults ages 18 and older, designed to be representative of the broader population of consumers.

Brown said budget pressure also factors into traveling for the holidays, as Visa expects more people to celebrate the holidays "a little bit closer to home" than in the past given rising airfares and general inflation. In September, consumer prices rose 3.7% annually, driven higher by surging energy costs.

Airlines have hiked the price of plane tickets this year as their fuel costs have risen by billions of dollars due to the war in the Middle East. And even if fuel prices drop, executives have said fares are likely to stay high, with fares up more than 22% in September compared with a year earlier, according to the consumer price index.

Those trends mean consumers will likely be more intentional about where they're spending, especially as inflation drives up prices across the board.

"Consumers are aspirational. They want those experiences, they want to travel, they want to be out in the world," Brown said. "However, the economic reality is with prices rising, with economic uncertainty, they need to balance the reality of today's economic environment with those aspirations."

The report also found that only households with an income above $200,000 spent more on trips departing between Nov. 1 and early next year compared with 2025 into 2026. On the other hand, many other income groups pulled back on their travel spending.

"If you think about that segment, a lot of them own equities, and the equity market runup over the last several months has created a lot of positivity among that specific affluent demographic, and I think that's one of the factors that continues to motivate even aggregate macro spend today," Brown said.

Meanwhile, less affluent shoppers are placing an emphasis on value. Visa's consumer survey from October showed that roughly one-third of consumers with an income below $100,000 are going to switch to lower-cost brands or stores when shopping for holiday gifts this season.

Though Visa is projecting a 4.5% year-over-year increase in holiday sales growth, much of that figure is due to inflation. Without those price increases, sales are expected to rise just 1.1% this year, down from the 1.3% increase during the 2025 holiday season.

Ultimately, Brown said the survey findings may be encouraging for the broader retail scene this holiday season. With less emphasis on travel and more projected spending on physical gifts, it could boost the sector at a time when spending is under pressure.

At the same time, he said that consumers may just be expecting to spend more this holiday season because they've become accustomed to the higher prices.

"But, of course, consumers are internalizing higher prices of things, and I think that's why they're reporting higher anticipated spend this year," Brown said. "I think these numbers just need to be looked at through the lens of the inflation and particularly consumers' perception of where inflation is going to go. I think that weighed heavily on consumer psychology this season."

#493. Expedia CEO says AI agents aren't changing booking habits — yet

Expedia CEO Ariane Gorin said Thursday the company hasn't yet seen artificial intelligence agents affecting the way customers book, but the early signs show potential for an industry shift.

"It's a great way for people to do inspiration, to do trip planning," Gorin said in an interview with CNBC. "We haven't seen it change the way they're actually doing booking."

The comments come as personal AI agents like Meta's Muse and OpenAI's Dots enter the mainstream, promising to tackle everything from scheduling to shopping for everyday Americans. Opportunities in travel planning and booking could pose a competitive threat to traditional sites like Expedia.

Gorin said Expedia is "working with all of the agents" to make sure it surfaces in AI results, to understand how the agents get information and to make sure that the company has the necessary integrations.

She acknowledged it's possible that AI agents could develop their own travel programs and eventually claim market share, but she said complexities within the industry — customer service, for example — would be difficult to fully replace with artificial intelligence.

"People were already shopping across a number of places, so the agent can do that in a much easier way," Gorin said. "But when it comes to actually booking — being confident that the content that you're getting is right, the amenities are right, the price is right, if something goes wrong, someone's going to actually either solve it for you, answer the phone, be there for you — I don't think those agents are going to do it for you, and that's why people trust Expedia and come to us."

Shares of Expedia were down about 12% Thursday after the company delivered weaker-than-expected guidance for the fourth quarter on Wednesday. The company forecast revenue of between $3.69 billion and $3.75 billion for the final three months of the year, making the midpoint lower than analyst estimates of $3.74 billion, according to FactSet.

Gorin attributed the "moderation" in Expedia's fourth-quarter guidance to "geopolitical events in the Middle East and Europe," the midterm elections and the company moving its fall sale from the fourth quarter to the third quarter. But she noted the company reported a strong third quarter, as total gross bookings rose 11% and revenue climbed 12%.
